Multiplicative Ehresmann connections Thanks: RLF was partially supported by NSF grants DMS-2003223 and DMS-2303586.
Rui Loja Fernandes, Ioan Mărcu t ,

Abstract
. We develop the theory of multiplicative Ehresmann connections for Lie groupoid submersions covering the identity, as well as their infinitesimal counterparts. We construct obstructions to the existence of such connections, and we prove existence for several interesting classes of Lie groupoids and Lie algebroids, including all proper Lie groupoids. We show that many notions from the theory of principal bundle connections have analogues in this general setup, including connections 1-forms, curvature 2-forms, Bianchi identity, etc. In [ 19 ] we provide a non-trivial application of the results obtained here to construct local models in Poisson geometry and to obtain linearization results around Poisson submanifolds.



1.
Introduction

The notion of principal bundle connection is ubiquitous in differential geometry and its applications. In this work we study a far reaching extension of this notion. In order to explain it, recall that given a principal
G
-bundle
p:P\to M
one has an associated gauge groupoid
\mathcal{G}:=P\times_{G}P\rightrightarrows M
and its anchor is a groupoid submersion
\Phi:=(\mathbf{t},\mathbf{s}):\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
onto the pair groupoid
\mathcal{H}:=M\times M\rightrightarrows M
. Then there is a 1-to-1 correspondence:

\left\{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-7.04445pt\vbox{\halign{\relax\hfil\txtline@@{#}\hfil\cr\hbox{{\ignorespaces principal bundle}\\\hbox{{\ignorespaces\ connections for $p:P\to M$ }\\\hbox{{\ignorespaces\, }\crcr}}}}\right\}\tilde{\longleftrightarrow}\left\{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-8.01666pt\vbox{\halign{\relax\hfil\txtline@@{#}\hfil\cr\hbox{{\ignorespaces multiplicative Ehresmann}\\\hbox{{\ignorespaces\ connections for $\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}$ }\\\hbox{{\ignorespaces\,}\crcr}}}}\right\}}}}}}}

Here, by a
multiplicative
Ehresmann connection we mean a distribution
E
in
\mathcal{G}
such that
T\mathcal{G}=E\oplus\ker\operatorname{d}\Phi
, which is a
subgroupoid
of the tangent groupoid
T\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows TM
.

We study in this paper multiplicative Ehresmann connections for
any surjective, submersive, Lie groupoid map
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
covering the identity. We will see that such connections share many properties with principal bundle connections. One reason for this similarity is that given such a groupoid map its kernel
\mathcal{K}=\ker\Phi
is a bundle of Lie groups and one obtains for each
x\in M
a principal bundle
\Phi:\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{G}}^{-1}(x)\to\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}(x)
with structure group
\mathcal{K}_{x}
. We will see that a multiplicative Ehresmann connection
E
for
\Phi
gives rise to a family of principal connections, one for each principal bundle
\Phi:\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{G}}^{-1}(x)\to\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}(x)
. One should think of this family as a
leafwise component
of
E
.

Recall that a Cartan connection on a Lie groupoid
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows M
is an Ehresmann connection for the source map
\mathbf{s}:\mathcal{G}\to M
, which is at the same time a multiplicative distribution. Such connections have been studied extensively, sometimes under different names (see, e.g.,
[
1
,
3
,
4
,
2
,
13
,
32
]
). For a bundle of Lie groups
p:\mathcal{K}\to M
, they coincide with our notion of multiplicative Ehresmann connection, if we think of the bundle projection
p
as a groupoid morphism onto the identity groupoid. We will see that a multiplicative Ehresmann connection for
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
also gives rise to a Cartan connection
E^{\mathcal{K}}
on the kernel
\mathcal{K}=\ker\Phi
. We think of
E^{\mathcal{K}}
as the
kernel component
of
E
.

The existence of Cartan connections is, in general, rather mysterious. There are examples of proper groupoids (even transitive!) groupoids which do not admit any Cartan connection. By contrast, we will see that multiplicative Ehresmann connections for a morphism
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
often (but not always!) exist. For example, they always exist for the anchor map
(\mathbf{t},\mathbf{s}):\mathcal{G}\to M\times M
of a transitive Lie groupoid, because of the 1-to-1 correspondence with principal connections, explained in the first paragraph. Also, we will prove the following:

Theorem
.

If
\mathcal{G}
is a proper Lie groupoid, any morphism
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
admits a multiplicative Ehresmann connection.

The reason why this result holds is that multiplicative Ehresmann connections, unlike Cartan connections, can be transported along Morita equivalences. However, this requires to consider multiplicative Ehresmann connections for slightly more general objects than groupoid morphisms. Given a Lie groupoid
\mathcal{G}
with Lie algebroid
A
we will define multiplicative Ehresmann connections for any bundle of ideals
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
which are invariant under the conjugation action of
\mathcal{G}
. When
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
is a morphism as above and
\mathfrak{k}
is the bundle of ideals induced by its kernel
\mathcal{K}
(i.e., the Lie algebroid of
\mathcal{K}
), the two notions coincide.

A bundle of ideals
\mathfrak{k}
for a Lie groupoid
\mathcal{G}
is called
partially split
if it admits a multiplicative Ehresmann connection
E
. Note that this more general notion appears naturally in applications. We were originally led to introduce them in the study of local models around Poisson submanifolds in Poisson geometry.
There one finds a constant rank multiplicative 2-form
\omega\in\Omega^{2}_{M}(\mathcal{G})
whose kernel defines a bundle of ideals. A multiplicative Ehresmann connection for this bundle of ideals is necessary to construct a groupoid coisotropic embedding of
(\mathcal{G},\omega)
into a symplectic groupoid. The infinitesimal version of this embedding is precisely a local model for a Poisson submanifold. We will not discuss any of these applications here and we instead refer the reader to
[
19
]
.

Just like for principal connections, rather than specifying a multiplicative Ehresmann connection via a distribution
E\subset T\mathcal{G}
one can give instead a
multiplicative connection 1-form
. This is a multiplicative 1-form
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathcal{G};\mathfrak{k})
with coefficients in the bundle of ideals
\mathfrak{k}
which additionally must satisfy:

\alpha(\xi^{L})=\xi,\quad(\xi\in\Gamma(\mathfrak{k})),

where
\xi^{L}\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{G})
is the left-invariant vector field associated to the section
\xi
. The
curvature of the connection
is then a multiplicative 2-form with coefficients in
\mathfrak{k}
, denoted
\Omega=D\alpha\in\Omega^{2}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathcal{G};\mathfrak{k})
. It measures the failure in
E
being an involutive distribution.
One advantage of specifying a multiplicative Ehresmann connection via a connection 1-form is that is leads immediately to its infinitesimal version. Namely, given a Lie algebroid
A\Rightarrow M
, integrable or not, and a bundle of ideals
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
, an
IM (infinitesimal multiplicative) Ehresmann connection
is given by an IM 1-form on
A
with coefficients in
\mathfrak{k}
(see, e.g.,
[
14
]
), whose symbol
l:A\to\mathfrak{k}
satisfies:

l(\xi)=\xi,\quad(\xi\in\Gamma(\mathfrak{k})).

The multiplicative Ehresmann connections studied in this paper have been studied before in
[
25
]
, for the special case of a groupoid extension in the context of the theory of non-abelian gerbes. There, by a groupoid extension the authors mean a groupoid map
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{K}
between Hausdorff Lie groupoids which is locally trivial. It implies, in particular, that whenever it admits a connection, its kernel is a locally trivial bundle of Lie groups. Many of the results we obtain in Section
2
recover, in this special case, results of
[
25
]
. Our result mentioned above concerning transporting multiplicative connections along Morita equivalences also extends a theorem from
[
25
]
, which establishes Morita invariance of the existence of connections. Note that the notion of Morita equivalence of groupoid extensions, used in
[
25
]
, is more restrictive than the Morita equivalence of Lie groupoids used here. In particular, Theorem
Theorem
does not follow from the results of
[
25
]
.

In
[
9
, Section 6.5]
the authors consider multiplicative tensors
K\in\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{G},T\mathcal{G})
satisfying
K^{2}=K
, which they call
multiplicative projections
. These include, as a special case, multiplicative Ehresmann connections, and the so-called Frölicher-Nijenhuis bracket of
K
(see
[
9
]
) coincide with our curvature 2-forms. Also, the notion of matched pair of Lie algebroid from
[
9
]
can be interpreted as a version of our IM connections.

There are many questions related to our theory of connections and its applications that we do not discuss in this paper. Besides the applications to Poisson geometry
[
19
]
and to gerbes
[
25
]
already mentioned, it is also natural to consider Ehresmann connections for morphisms of groupoids over different bases or more general types of bundles of ideals
[
23
]
. Another intriguing question is to define appropriate moduli spaces of flat multiplicative connections. We plan to return to some these questions in future work.

This paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
we introduce multiplicative Ehresmann connections, and we discuss:

\bullet

alternative characterizations of connections;

\bullet

obstructions to their existence;

\bullet

necessary and sufficient criteria for completeness;

\bullet

curvature, structure equation and Bianchi’s identity;

\bullet

various notions of flatness and relationship to semi-direct products.

In Section
3
, we give many classes of Lie groupoids and morphisms that admit multiplicative Ehresmann connections. Section
4
is dedicated to the proof of Morita invariance and to show the existence of multiplicative Ehresmann connections for proper groupoids. In this section, we also illustrate our results with an application to the theory of
S^{1}
-gerbes over a manifold, recovering the classical result of Murray about representing the Dixmier-Douady class in real cohomology. In Section
5
, we introduce IM Ehresmann connections, and we discuss:

\bullet

alternative characterizations of IM connections;

\bullet

obstructions to their existence;

\bullet

a theory of coupling forms for IM connections, generalizing the classical coupling description of symplectic fibrations;

\bullet

curvature of IM connections;

\bullet

various notions of flatness and relationship to semi-direct products of Lie algebroids.

In Section
6
, we give many classes of Lie algebroids and morphisms that admit IM Ehresmann connections, including non-integrable algebroids. At the end of the paper, we have included an appendix with background and results on multiplicative forms and IM forms with coefficients, needed throughout the paper. The results in Section
A.3
concerning covariant differentiation of such forms seem to be new.

Acknowledgments.
We would like to thank Henrique Bursztyn for bringing to our attention his work
[
9
]
. We specially would like to thank Camille Laurent Gengoux for many comments on a first version of this paper posted in the arXiv, and for pointing out the connections to his joint work with Mathieu Stiénon and Ping Xu
[
25
]
, which we were unaware of.

Conventions and notations.

\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows M
, with source/target
\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}:\mathcal{G}\to M
and multiplication

5.6321pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.5361pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.5361pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.19138pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.56526pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 5.6321pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22267pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.58762pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-9.7347pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22267pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.58762pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.54321pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.5361pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.5361pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.19138pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.56526pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 4.54321pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}
. We denote a Lie algebroid by
A\Rightarrow M
, with Lie bracket
[\cdot,\cdot]_{A}:\Gamma(A)\times\Gamma(A)\to\Gamma(A)
and anchor
\rho_{A}:A\to TM
. Our conventions are as in
[
11
]
, so the Lie algebroid of
\mathcal{G}
is the vector bundle
A=\ker(\operatorname{d}\mathbf{t})|_{M}
with Lie bracket induced from the bracket of left-invariant vector fields and anchor
\rho=\operatorname{d}\mathbf{s}
. Also,
Proper Lie groupoids are assumed Hausdorff, while general Lie groupoids are not necessarily Hausdorff, unless stated otherwise.

2.
Multiplicative Ehresmann connections

2.1.
Ehresmann connection for a Lie groupoid submersion

Let
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows M
and
\mathcal{H}\rightrightarrows M
be two Lie groupoids, and

\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}

be a Lie groupoid map covering
\mathrm{Id}_{M}
which is a surjective submersion. We are investigating the existence problem for the following objects.

Definition 2.1
.

A
multiplicative Ehresmann connection
for
\Phi
is a “horizontal” distribution

T\mathcal{G}=\ker(\operatorname{d}\Phi)\oplus E

which is also a Lie subgroupoid
E\rightrightarrows TM
of the tangent groupoid
T\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows TM
.

Notice that the kernel of a Lie groupoid map
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
as above

\mathcal{K}:=\ker\Phi=\Phi^{-1}(\textrm{units})\subset\mathcal{G}

is a bundle of Lie subgroups of
\mathcal{G}
which is
normal
, i.e., conjugation by any
g\in\mathcal{G}
gives a Lie group isomorphism:

\operatorname{Ad}_{g}:\mathcal{K}_{\mathbf{s}(g)}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-1.59442pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\smash{$\sim$}}}\mathcal{K}_{\mathbf{t}(g)},\quad k\mapsto gkg^{-1}.

Let
A\Rightarrow M
and
B\Rightarrow M
denote the Lie algebroids of
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows M
and
\mathcal{H}\rightrightarrows M
, respectively, and let
\phi:A\to B
be the Lie algebroid map induced by
\Phi
. Then the Lie algebroid
\mathfrak{k}\Rightarrow M
of
\mathcal{K}
is a bundle of Lie algebras that fits into the short exact sequence of Lie algebroids:

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathfrak{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 64.7778pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 64.7778pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 85.02034pt\raise 6.1111pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\phi}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 102.2778pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 102.2778pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 140.3646pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 140.3646pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

The “conjugation action” induces an action on the isotropies:

\operatorname{Ad}_{g}:\ker\rho|_{\mathbf{s}(g)}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-1.59442pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\smash{$\sim$}}}\ker\rho|_{\mathbf{t}(g)},\quad g\cdot\alpha=\frac{\operatorname{d}}{\operatorname{d}t}\Big|_{t=0}g\,\exp_{\mathbf{s}(g)}(t\alpha)\,g^{-1},

(2.1)

where
\exp_{x}:\ker\rho|_{x}\to\mathcal{G}_{x}
is the Lie group exponential. Since
\mathcal{K}
is invariant under conjugation, we obtain a representation of
\mathcal{G}
on
\mathfrak{k}
.

Finally, note that the subbundle
K:=\ker(\operatorname{d}\Phi)
can be recovered from
\mathfrak{k}
by using (left or right) translations:

K_{g}=(\ker\operatorname{d}\Phi)|_{g}=\operatorname{d}L_{g}(\mathfrak{k}|_{\mathbf{s}(g)})=\operatorname{d}R_{g}(\mathfrak{k}|_{\mathbf{t}(g)})\subset\ker(\operatorname{d}\mathbf{s})\cap\ker(\operatorname{d}\mathbf{t}).

Remark 2.2
.

Connections in the sense of Definition
2.1
were introduced in
[
25
]
under the extra assumption that
\Phi
is a locally trivial fibration. The authors call them “connections for Lie groupoid extensions”.

2.2.
Partially split bundles of ideals

It turns out that multiplicative Ehresmann connections can be defined in a more general setting, without the presence of a Lie groupoid morphism.

Definition 2.3
.

Given a Lie groupoid
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows M
with Lie algebroid
A\Rightarrow M
, we call a vector subbundle
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
a
bundle of ideals
of
\mathcal{G}
if
\mathfrak{k}\subset\ker\rho
and
\mathfrak{k}
is invariant under the
\mathcal{G}
-action by conjugation (
2.1
).

The fact that
\mathfrak{k}
is invariant under conjugation implies that

\alpha\in\Gamma(A),\beta\in\Gamma(\mathfrak{k})\quad\Longrightarrow\quad[\alpha,\beta]\in\Gamma(\mathfrak{k}).

When
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows M
is source-connected, this condition is equivalent to
\mathfrak{k}\subset\ker\rho
being a bundle of ideals (see, e.g., Appendix B
[
27
]
). So this condition defines the notion of a
bundle of ideals
for any Lie algebroid
A
, integrable or not. We will explore this in Section
5
, where we study the infinitesimal analog of multiplicative Ehresmann connections.

Given a bundle of ideals
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
for
\mathcal{G}
, by using (left or right) translations we obtain the involutive distribution on
\mathcal{G}
:

K_{g}:=\operatorname{d}L_{g}(\mathfrak{k}_{\mathbf{s}(g)})=\operatorname{d}R_{g}(\mathfrak{k}_{\mathbf{t}(g)})\subset\ker(\operatorname{d}\mathbf{s})\cap\ker(\operatorname{d}\mathbf{t}).

(2.2)

Note that
K\rightrightarrows 0_{M}
is a subgroupoid of the tangent groupoid
T\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows TM
.

Definition 2.4
.

A bundle of ideals
\mathfrak{k}
of
\mathcal{G}
is said to be
partially split
if it admits a
multiplicative Ehresmann connection
, that is, if there is a wide Lie subgroupoid
E\subset T\mathcal{G}
such that

T\mathcal{G}=E\oplus K

where
K
is the subgroupoid (
2.2
).

Remark 2.5
.

Note that, given a bundle of ideals
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
, we still have a short exact sequence of Lie algebroids:

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathfrak{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 64.7778pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 64.7778pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 102.2778pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 102.2778pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 140.3646pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 140.3646pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

where
B:=A/\mathfrak{k}
. However, at the groupoid level, there may not exist a closed embedded subgroupoid
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{G}
integrating
\mathfrak{k}
and even
B
may fail to be integrable (see Example
3.7
). So in this more general setup a Lie groupoid morphism
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
as in Definition
2.1
may not exist. If it exists, then
K=\ker\operatorname{d}\Phi
.

Remark 2.6
.

We will be using the following nomenclature:

\bullet

A
multiplicative Ehresmann connection for a morphism

\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
, as in Definition
2.1
. We will always assume that it is a surjective, submersive, Lie groupoid morphism covering the identity, without explicitly mentioning it;

\bullet

A
multiplicative Ehresmann connection for a bundle of ideals

\mathfrak{k}
of a groupoid
\mathcal{G}
, as in Definition
2.4
;

\bullet

A
Cartan connection for a groupoid

\mathcal{G}
: by this we mean a multiplicative distribution
E\subset T\mathcal{G}
such that

T\mathcal{G}=\ker\operatorname{d}\mathbf{s}\oplus E.

The last notion has been discussed and used extensively in the literature, sometimes under different names (see, e.g.,
[
1
,
3
,
4
,
2
,
13
,
32
]
). For a general groupoid, the source map
\mathbf{s}:\mathcal{G}\to M
cannot be viewed as groupoid morphism, and this notion is distinct from the notions introduced above. However, for a bundle of Lie groups
p:\mathcal{G}\to M
, where
\mathbf{s}=\mathbf{t}=p
, one can view the projection as a groupoid morphism onto the identity groupoid
M\rightrightarrows M
. The corresponding bundle of ideals is the Lie algebroid
A
of
\mathcal{G}
. So a Cartan connection for a bundle of Lie groups
p:\mathcal{G}\to M
is the same as a multiplicative Ehresmann connection for the morphism
p
, or a multiplicative Ehresmann connection for the bundle of ideals
A
.

2.3.
The partially split condition

We will now look for alternative characterizations of partially split bundles of ideals. We start by observing that
K
is a actually a semi-direct product:

Lemma 2.7
.

The subgroupoid
K\rightrightarrows 0_{M}
of
T\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows TM
is canonically isomorphic to the semi-direct product
\mathcal{G}\times_{M}\mathfrak{k}\rightrightarrows M
, with multiplication:

(g,v)\cdot(h,w)=(gh,h^{-1}\cdot v+w),\quad\text{if}\quad\mathbf{s}(g)=\mathbf{t}(h),v\in\mathfrak{k}_{\mathbf{s}(g)},w\in\mathfrak{k}_{\mathbf{s}(h)}.

\textstyle{{}\ignorespaces K\,\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\mathcal{G}\times_{M}\mathfrak{k}\simeq

\textstyle{T\mathcal{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0_{M}\,\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TM\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M}

Proof.

One checks immediately that the map:

\mathcal{G}\times_{M}\mathfrak{k}\to K,\quad(g,v)\mapsto\operatorname{d}L_{g}(v),

is a groupoid isomorphism.
∎

The Lie groupoids
T\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows TM
and
K\rightrightarrows 0_{M}
are examples of VB groupoids with cores
A\to M
and
\mathfrak{k}\to M
, respectively. Under the duality operation in the category of VB groupoids (see
[
26
,
7
,
21
]
), these have duals the VB groupoids
T^{*}\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows A^{*}
and
K^{*}\rightrightarrows\mathfrak{k}^{*}
, with cores
T^{*}M\to M
and
0_{M}\to M
, respectively. The groupoid
K^{*}\rightrightarrows\mathfrak{k}^{*}
is isomorphic to the action groupoid
\mathcal{G}\ltimes\mathfrak{k}^{*}\rightrightarrows\mathfrak{k}^{*}
resulting from the dual action of
\mathcal{G}
on
\mathfrak{k}^{*}
.
Moreover, by applying duality to the inclusion map, the restriction map
T^{*}\mathcal{G}\to K^{*}
is a groupoid morphism:

\textstyle{{}\ignorespaces K^{*}\,\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\mathcal{G}\ltimes\mathfrak{k}^{*}\simeq

\textstyle{T^{*}\mathcal{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{k}^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M}

The following proposition gives useful characterizations of the partially split condition
(for the terminology, see the appendix):

Proposition 2.8
.

Let
\mathfrak{k}
be a bundle of ideals for
\mathcal{G}
. The following structures are in 1-to-1 correspondence:

(i)

VB subgroupoids
E\subset T\mathcal{G}
that are complementary to
K
:

T\mathcal{G}=K\oplus E;

(ii)

VB groupoid morphisms
\Theta:\mathcal{G}\ltimes\mathfrak{k}^{*}\to T^{*}\mathcal{G}
that are splittings of the projection
p:T^{*}\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}\ltimes\mathfrak{k}^{*}
:

p\circ\,\Theta=\mathrm{Id};

(iii)

\mathfrak{k}
-valued, multiplicative, 1-forms
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathcal{G},\mathfrak{k})
that restrict to the identity on
\mathfrak{k}\subset T_{M}\mathcal{G}
:

\alpha|_{\mathfrak{k}}=\mathrm{Id};

(iv)

linear, closed, multiplicative, 2-forms
\omega\in\Omega^{2}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathcal{G}\ltimes\mathfrak{k}^{*})
(see Definition
A.14
) that restrict to the canonical symplectic form on
\mathfrak{k}\times_{M}\mathfrak{k}^{*}\subset T_{M}(\mathcal{G}\ltimes\mathfrak{k}^{*})
:

\omega((v_{1},\xi_{1}),(v_{2},\xi_{2}))=\xi_{2}(v_{1})-\xi_{1}(v_{2}),\quad\text{if}\quad(v_{k},\xi_{k})\in\mathfrak{k}\times_{M}\mathfrak{k}^{*}.

Proof.

The equivalence between (i) and (ii) follows from the discussion above.

Let
A
be the Lie algebroid of
\mathcal{G}
. A bundle map

\textstyle{\mathcal{G}\ltimes\mathfrak{k}^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Theta}
\textstyle{T^{*}\mathcal{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Id}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{k}^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M}

is a VB groupoid morphism if and only if its dual is a VB groupoid morphism:

\textstyle{T\mathcal{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Theta^{\vee}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{G}\times_{M}\mathfrak{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Id}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TM\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0_{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M}

But a VB groupoid morphism
\Theta^{\vee}:T\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}\times_{M}\mathfrak{k}
covering the identity is the same thing as a
\mathfrak{k}
-valued, multiplicative 1-form
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathcal{G},\mathfrak{k})
(see Example
A.5
). This gives a correspondence
\Theta\leftrightarrow\alpha
, where the conditions
p\circ\,\Theta=\mathrm{Id}
and
\alpha|_{\mathfrak{k}}=\mathrm{Id}
correspond to each other, proving the equivalence between (ii) and (iii).

By Lemma
A.13
, there is a 1-to-1 correspondence between linear, closed forms
\omega\in\Omega^{2}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathcal{G}\ltimes\mathfrak{k}^{*})
and vector bundle maps
\Theta:\mathcal{G}\ltimes\mathfrak{k}^{*}\to T^{*}\mathcal{G}
covering the identity, determined by the relation:

\omega=\Theta^{*}(\omega_{\mathrm{can}}).

Clearly, if
\Theta
is a groupoid map, it follows that
\omega
is multiplicative. Conversely, if
\omega
is multiplicative, then we have a VB groupoid morphism:

\textstyle{T(\mathcal{G}\ltimes\mathfrak{k}^{*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega^{\flat}}
\textstyle{T^{*}(\mathcal{G}\ltimes\mathfrak{k}^{*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T\mathfrak{k}^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(A\ltimes\mathfrak{k}^{*})^{*}}

Via the assignment of Lemma
A.13
, the map
\Theta
is the composition:

\mathcal{G}\ltimes\mathfrak{k}^{*}\hookrightarrow T_{\mathcal{G}}(\mathcal{G}\ltimes\mathfrak{k}^{*})\mathrel{{\mathop{\longrightarrow}\limits}}T_{\mathcal{G}}^{*}(\mathcal{G}\ltimes\mathfrak{k}^{*})\longrightarrow T^{*}\mathcal{G}.

Hence,
\Theta
is a groupoid morphism. Under the correspondence
\Theta\leftrightarrow\omega
, the extra conditions in (ii) and (iv) correspond to each other, proving the equivalence between these two items.
∎

The characterization of multiplicative Ehresmann connections in terms of multiplicative 1-forms (item (iii) in the previous proposition) also appears in
[
25
, Thm 4.12]
for the case of Lie groupoid extensions (cf. Remark
2.2
). We will be making extensive use of it.

Definition 2.9
.

Given a partially split bundle of ideals
\mathfrak{k}
with a multiplicative Ehresmann connection
E\subset T\mathcal{G}
, the corresponding
\mathfrak{k}
-valued 1-form
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathcal{G},\mathfrak{k})
, given by Proposition
2.8
(iii), is called
the
multiplicative connection 1-form
.

Next we deduce two obstructions for a bundle of ideals to be partially split. We start by observing that for bundles of Lie groups multiplicative connections induce ordinary connections, a remark that will also be useful in future sections. This remark follows, e.g., by observing that a multiplicative connection on a bundle of Lie groups is an example of a Cartan connection (see Remark
2.6
), hence it induces an infinitesimal Cartan connection on its Lie algebroid (see
[
5
]
for more details and further references). We give an independent proof:

Proposition 2.10
.

Let
p:\mathcal{K}\to M
be a bundle of Lie groups endowed with a multiplicative Ehresmann connection:
T\mathcal{K}=E^{\mathcal{K}}\oplus\ker\operatorname{d}p
. Then the corresponding bundle of Lie algebras
\mathfrak{k}\to M
has an induced linear connection
\nabla
which preserves the Lie bracket:

\nabla_{X}[\xi,\eta]_{\mathfrak{k}}=[\nabla_{X}\xi,\eta]_{\mathfrak{k}}+[\xi,\nabla_{X}\eta]_{\mathfrak{k}},

for all
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
,
\xi,\eta\in\Gamma(\mathfrak{k})
.
The corresponding linear Ehresmann connection
E^{\mathfrak{k}}\subset T\mathfrak{k}
is related to
E^{\mathcal{K}}
via the exponential map
\exp:\mathfrak{k}\to\mathcal{K}
on a neighborhood
U\subset\mathfrak{k}
of the zero section:

(\operatorname{d}\exp)(E^{\mathfrak{k}}|_{U})=E^{\mathcal{K}}|_{\exp(U)}.

(2.3)

Proof.

The connection is defined by:

\nabla_{X}\xi=[\widetilde{X},\xi^{L}]|_{M},\quad X\in\mathfrak{X}(M),\ \xi\in\Gamma(\mathfrak{k}),

(2.4)

where
\widetilde{X}
is the horizontal lift with respect to
E^{\mathcal{K}}
and
\xi^{L}\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{K})
is the left-invariant vector field corresponding to
\xi
. Multiplicativity of
E^{\mathcal{K}}
implies that
[\widetilde{X},\xi^{L}]
is a left-invariant vector field, so the Jacobi identity shows that the connection preserves the Lie bracket on
\mathfrak{k}
.

We now claim that if
U\subset\mathfrak{k}
is a neighborhood of the zero section where
\exp
is a diffeomorphism, then (
2.3
) holds. For this, we will show that the left-hand side is a multiplicative distribution. It then follows that both sides are (local) Lie subgroupoids of
T\mathcal{K}
with the same Lie algebroid, so they must coincide. Hence, we assume that
\mathcal{K}=\mathfrak{k}
and multiplication is given by the (fiberwise) Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff series
\mathrm{BCH}(\cdot,\cdot)
.

Notice that:

E^{\mathfrak{k}}_{u}=\big\{(\operatorname{d}_{x}s)(v):v\in T_{x}M,\,s\in\Gamma(\mathfrak{k}),\,s(x)=u,\,(\nabla s)|_{x}=0\big\}.

So it is enough to check that for
s_{1},s_{2}\in\Gamma(\mathfrak{k})
:

(\nabla s_{1})|_{x}=(\nabla s_{2})|_{x}=0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad(\nabla\textrm{BCH}(s_{1},s_{2}))|_{x}=0.

This follows from the expression of
\mathrm{BCH}(\cdot,\cdot)
as a sum of commutators because
\nabla
preserves the brackets.
∎

Corollary 2.11
.

Given a partially split bundle of ideals
\mathfrak{k}
for a Lie groupoid
\mathcal{G}
each multiplicative Ehresmann connection
E\subset T\mathcal{G}
determines a linear connection
\nabla^{E}
on the bundle of Lie algebras
\mathfrak{k}\to M
which preserves the Lie bracket.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{G}(\mathfrak{k})
be the bundle of simply connected Lie groups integrating
\mathfrak{k}
and
i:\mathcal{G}(\mathfrak{k})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{G}
the immersion integrating the inclusion. Given a multiplicative Ehresmann connection
E
on
\mathcal{G}
for
\mathfrak{k}
the pullback
(\operatorname{d}i)^{-1}(E)\subset T\mathcal{G}(\mathfrak{k})
is a multiplicative Ehresmann connection for the bundle of Lie groups
p:\mathcal{G}(\mathfrak{k})\to M
. Hence, the result follows from the proposition.
∎

Corollary 2.12
.

If a bundle of ideals
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
for
\mathcal{G}
is partially split, then
\mathfrak{k}
is a locally trivial bundle of Lie algebras.

The corollary implies that
\mathcal{G}(\mathfrak{k})
is a Hausdorff manifold (recall that bundles of Lie algebras may fail to have Hausdorff integrations).

The partial split condition also places restrictions on the isotropy Lie algebras:

Corollary 2.13
.

If a bundle of ideals
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
for
\mathcal{G}
is partially split, then for each
x\in M
the isotropy Lie algebra splits into a direct sum of ideals:

\mathfrak{g}_{x}=\mathfrak{k}_{x}\oplus\mathfrak{l}_{x}.

Proof.

Observe that if
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
is partially split for
\mathcal{G}
then for each
x\in M
the ideal
\mathfrak{k}_{x}\subset\mathfrak{g}_{x}
is partially split for
\mathcal{G}_{x}^{0}
, the connected component of the isotropy Lie group. This follows, e.g., by restricting a multiplicative connection 1-form
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathcal{G},\mathfrak{k})
to
\mathcal{G}_{x}^{0}
. The corollary now follows from the fact that an ideal
\mathfrak{k}\subset\mathfrak{g}
for a connected Lie group
G
is partially split if and if only it admits a complementary ideal in
\mathfrak{g}
, as discussed in the example from Subsection
3.1
.
∎

Remark 2.14
.

In the case of connections for Lie groupoid extensions, the associated linear connection
\nabla
and Proposition
2.10
was also deduced in
[
25
]
(see Corollary 4.5, Lemma 4.6 and Proposition 4.21 there).

2.4.
Completeness

In this section we will assume that all Lie groupoids are Hausdorff. Given a multiplicative Ehresmann connection for a groupoid homomorphism
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
covering the identity we can ask if it is complete, i.e., if one can lift any path in
\mathcal{H}
to an horizontal path in
\mathcal{G}
. In this section we prove the following result which completely settles this question.

Theorem 2.15
.

Let
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
be a surjective, submersive, Lie groupoid homomorphism covering the identity. A multiplicative Ehresmann connection for
\Phi
is complete if and only the kernel
\mathcal{K}=\ker\Phi
is a locally trivial bundle of Lie groups.

Remark 2.16
.

Given a surjective submersion
\Phi:M\to N
between two manifolds one has (see, e.g.,
[
17
,
20
]
):

(i)

\Phi
is locally trivial if and only if
\Phi:M\to N
admits a complete Ehresmann connection;

(ii)

\Phi
is proper if and only if every Ehresmann connection on
\Phi:M\to N
is complete.

One can think of the theorem as a multiplicative version of (i). On the other hand, it it is not hard to see that a surjective, submersive, groupoid morphism
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
covering the identity is proper if and only if its kernel
\mathcal{K}
is proper (i.e.,
p:\mathcal{K}\to M
is a proper map).

The rest of this section is devoted to the proof of the theorem. As before, set

K:=\ker(\operatorname{d}\Phi)\subset T\mathcal{G}.

We also denote by
p:=\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{K}}=\mathbf{t}_{\mathcal{K}}
the common source and target of the bundle of Lie groups
\mathcal{K}\to M
. Denoting by
i:\mathcal{K}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{G}
the inclusion, notice that

E^{\mathcal{K}}:=(\operatorname{d}i)^{-1}(E)=E\cap T\mathcal{K},

gives a multiplicative Ehresmann connection on the bundle
p:\mathcal{K}\to M
:

T\mathcal{K}=\ker(\operatorname{d}p)\oplus E^{\mathcal{K}},\text{ where }E^{\mathcal{K}}:=E\cap T\mathcal{K}.

In terms of the nomenclature of Remark
2.6
,
E^{\mathcal{K}}
is a Cartan connection for
\mathcal{K}
. The resulting linear connection on the Lie algebroid
\mathfrak{k}
of
\mathcal{K}
is precisely the linear connection
\nabla^{E}
of Proposition
2.10
.

The following result reduces the proof of Theorem
2.15
to the case of a bundle of Lie groups.

Proposition 2.17
.

A multiplicative Ehresmann connection for a groupoid homomorphism
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
is complete if and only if the multiplicative Ehresmann connection induced on its kernel
p:\mathcal{K}\to M
is complete.

Proof.

In one direction the statement is obvious.

For the converse, assume that
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
admits a multiplicative Ehresmann connection
E\subset T\mathcal{G}
such that the induced Ehresmann connection on the kernel
E^{\mathcal{K}}\subset T\mathcal{K}
is complete. We observe that the following properties hold, independent of completeness:

(i)

If
g:I\to\mathcal{G}
is a horizontal path, then
t\mapsto g(t)^{-1}
is also horizontal;

(ii)

If
g_{1},g_{2}:I\to\mathcal{G}
are horizontal paths with
\mathbf{s}(g_{1}(t))=\mathbf{t}(g_{2}(t))
for all
t\in I
, then
t\mapsto g_{1}(t)g_{2}(t)
is also horizontal;

(iii)

if
g:I\to\mathcal{G}
is a horizontal lift of a path
h:I\to\mathcal{H}
and
k:I\to\mathcal{K}
is a horizontal lift of
\gamma:=\mathbf{s}\circ h:I\to M
, then

\overline{g}:I\to\mathcal{G},\quad\overline{g}(t):=g(t)k(t),

is also a horizontal lift of
h:I\to\mathcal{H}
;

(iv)

any two horizontal lifts
g_{1},g_{2}:[a,b]\to\mathcal{G}
of
h:[a,b]\to\mathcal{H}
are related by:

g_{2}(t)=g_{1}(t)k(t)\quad(t\in[a,b]),

where
k:[a,b]\to\mathcal{K}
is the horizontal lift of
\mathbf{s}\circ h:[a,b]\to M
with
k(a)=g_{1}(a)^{-1}g_{2}(a)
.

Indeed, (i) and (ii) follow immediately from the fact that
E\rightrightarrows TM
is a subgroupoid of
T\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows TM
. Then (iii) follows from (i) and the fact that the connection
E^{\mathcal{K}}
on
\mathcal{K}\to M
is the restriction of
E
to
T\mathcal{K}
. Finally, (iv) follows from (i)-(iii) and the uniqueness of horizontal lifts.

Now, using these facts, we have the following standard argument showing that for every path
h:[0,1]\to\mathcal{H}
and any
g_{0}\in\mathcal{G}
with
\Phi(g_{0})=h(0)
there exists a horizontal lift
g:[0,1]\to\mathcal{G}
starting at
g_{0}
. First, we always have an horizontal lift
g:[0,t_{0})\to\mathcal{G}
, defined for some
t_{0}>0
. Then we can take any horizontal lift
\overline{g}:(t_{0}-\varepsilon,t_{0}+\varepsilon)\to\mathcal{G}
of
h:(t_{0}-\varepsilon,t_{0}+\varepsilon)\to\mathcal{H}
. Then we can write:

\overline{g}(t)=g(t)k(t),\quad t\in(t_{0}-\varepsilon,t_{0}),

where
k:(t_{0}-\varepsilon,t_{0})\to\mathcal{K}
is a horizontal lift of
\mathbf{s}\circ h:(t_{0}-\varepsilon,t_{0})\to M
.
Since
E^{\mathcal{K}}\subset T\mathcal{K}
is complete, the path
k:(t_{0}-\varepsilon,t_{0})\to\mathcal{K}
extends to a (unique) horizontal lift of
\mathbf{s}\circ h:[0,1]\to M
on the whole interval
[0,1]
. But then we obtain an horizontal extension
g:[0,t_{0}+\varepsilon)\to\mathcal{G}
by setting
g(t):=\overline{g}(t)k(t)^{-1}
, for
t\in(t_{0}-\varepsilon,t_{0}+\varepsilon)
. We conclude that
g(t)
extends to whole interval
[0,1]
.
∎

To complete the proof of Theorem
2.15
we will show the following:

Proposition 2.18
.

Let
p:\mathcal{K}\to M
be a bundle of Lie groups. The following are equivalent:

(a)

\mathcal{K}
admits a complete multiplicative Ehresmann connection;

(b)

p:\mathcal{K}\to M
is a locally trivial bundle of groups.

Moreover, in this case any multiplicative Ehresmann on
\mathcal{K}
is complete.

Proof.

Suppose a multiplicative Ehresmann connection exists which is complete. Then if we fix
x_{0}\in M
and take a ball
B_{\varepsilon}(x_{0})
inside some chart, we define:

\phi:B_{\varepsilon}(x_{0})\times\mathcal{K}_{x_{0}}\to\mathcal{K},\quad(x,k)\mapsto\widetilde{\gamma}_{x}^{k}(1),

where
\widetilde{\gamma}_{x}^{k}:[0,1]\to\mathcal{K}
denotes the horizontal lift through
k
of the line segment
\gamma_{x}:[0,1]\to B_{\varepsilon}(x_{0})
joining
x_{0}
to
x
. Since the Ehresmann connection is multiplicative, for each
x\in B_{\varepsilon}(x_{0})
the map
k\mapsto\phi(x,k)
is a group isomorphism, so
\phi
defines a local trivialization around
x_{0}
.

For the converse, assume that
\mathcal{K}
is locally trivial, and fix an open cover
\{U_{i}\}
over which
\mathcal{K}
trivializes. On each
U_{i}
consider the trivial multiplicative Ehresmann connection (see the example from Subsection
3.2
) with corresponding multiplicative connection 1-form
\alpha_{i}\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathcal{K}|_{U_{i}};\mathfrak{k})
. Then choosing a partition of unity
\{\chi_{i}\}
subordinate to this cover, we obtain a multiplicative connection 1-form

\alpha=\sum_{i}\chi_{i}\alpha_{i}\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathcal{K};\mathfrak{k}).

Next, we show that any multiplicative Ehresmann connection restricted to the connected component of the identity, denoted
\mathcal{K}^{0}
, is complete. This will not use that
\mathcal{K}
is locally trivial.
Let
\gamma:[0,1]\to M
be a path. The lift at
1_{\gamma(0)}
is defined as
1_{\gamma(t)}
for all
t\in[0,1]
. Therefore there is some neighborhood
U\in\mathcal{K}^{0}_{\gamma(0)}
such that for any
g\in U
the horizontal lift
\widetilde{\gamma}^{g}
is defined on
[0,1]
. By a well-known result in Lie theory, we have:

\mathcal{K}^{0}_{\gamma(0)}=\bigcup_{n\geq 1}U^{n}.

Hence, given any
g\in\mathcal{K}^{0}_{\gamma(0)}
we can factor it as a product:

g=g_{1}\cdots g_{N},\quad g_{i}\in U.

Then the horizontal lift of
\gamma
through
g
is defined on
[0,1]
and is given by:

\widetilde{\gamma}^{g}(t):=\widetilde{\gamma}^{g_{1}}(t)\cdots\widetilde{\gamma}^{g_{N}}(t),

(see item (ii) in the proof of Proposition
2.17
). This proves the claim for
\mathcal{K}^{0}
.

For the general case, since
\mathcal{K}
is locally trivial bundle of Lie groups, it follows that
\mathcal{K}/\mathcal{K}^{0}\to M
is a covering map. Hence any path
\gamma:[0,1]\to M
has a complete lift
\widetilde{\gamma}^{[g]}:[0,1]\to\mathcal{K}/\mathcal{K}^{0}
, for any initial value
[g]\in\mathcal{K}/\mathcal{K}^{0}|_{\gamma(0)}
. Next, we apply Proposition
2.17
to the groupoid homomorphism:

\Phi:\mathcal{K}\to\mathcal{K}/\mathcal{K}^{0},

with kernel
\mathcal{K}^{0}
. Then we know that
\widetilde{\gamma}^{[g]}
lifts to a horizontal path
\widetilde{\gamma}^{g}:[0,1]\to\mathcal{K}
, with any starting condition
g\in\mathcal{K}_{\gamma(0)}
. This proves completeness.

Note that the specific form of the Ehresmann connection did not play a role, and therefore we obtain the last claim of the statement.
∎

From the proof, we deduce:

Corollary 2.19
.

A bundle of connected Lie groups is locally trivial if and only if it admits a multiplicative Ehresmann connection. Any such connection is complete.

Note also that there are bundle of groups which are not locally trivial and admit multiplicative Ehresmann connections (necessarily non-complete). For example, bundles of discrete groups admit unique multiplicative Ehresmann connections.

Remark 2.20
.

In
[
25
, Prop 4.4]
the authors show that connections for a Lie groupoid extension are always complete. This also follows from our results. Indeed, the proof of our Proposition
2.17
shows that a multiplicative connection on a bundle of Lie groups which is a trivial fibration must be complete: for completeness all that is used in the proof is that
\mathcal{K}/\mathcal{K}^{0}\to M
is a covering map, and this follows if
\mathcal{K}\to M
is a locally trivial fibration.

2.5.
Curvature

In this section we fix a partially split bundle of ideals
\mathfrak{k}
for
\mathcal{G}
with a multiplicative Ehresmann connection
E\subset T\mathcal{G}
. We denote by

h:T\mathcal{G}\to E
the horizontal projection relative the decomposition

T\mathcal{G}=K\oplus E.

We denote by
\nabla:=\nabla^{E}
the induced linear connection on the bundle
\mathfrak{k}\to M
given by Corollary
2.11
, and by
\nabla^{\mathbf{s}}:=\mathbf{s}^{*}\nabla
the induced connection on
\mathbf{s}^{*}\mathfrak{k}=\mathcal{G}\times_{M}\mathfrak{k}\to\mathcal{G}
. This allows us to differentiate forms in
\mathcal{G}
with coefficients in
\mathfrak{k}
(see Section
A.3
).
We introduce the
exterior covariant derivative
of
E
as the operator
\operatorname{D}:\Omega^{k}(\mathcal{G},\mathfrak{k})\to\Omega^{k+1}(\mathcal{G},\mathfrak{k})
given by

(\operatorname{D}\omega)(X_{1},\dots,X_{k+1}):=(\operatorname{d}^{\nabla}\omega)(h(X_{1}),\dots,h(X_{k+1})).

Definition 2.21
.

The
curvature
of the multiplicative Ehresmann connection
E
is the 2-form:

\Omega:=\operatorname{D}\alpha\in\Omega^{2}(\mathcal{G},\mathfrak{k}),

where
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathcal{G},\mathfrak{k})
is the multiplicative connection 1-form of
E
.

The next properties of the curvature are reminiscent of properties of principal connections. In fact, as we will see in Subsection
3.4
, principal connections can be seen as examples of multiplicative Ehresmann connections.

Proposition 2.22
.

The curvature of a multiplicative Ehresmann connection
E\subset T\mathcal{G}
is a multiplicative form
\Omega\in\Omega^{2}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathcal{G},\mathfrak{k})
, which for horizontal vector fields
X,Y\in\Gamma(E)
is given by:

\operatorname{d}L_{g}\cdot\Omega(X,Y)_{g}=\big(h([X,Y])-[X,Y]\big)_{g}.

(2.5)

In particular, if
\mathfrak{k}
is induced by a Lie groupoid submersion
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
, and
\widetilde{U},\widetilde{V}\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{G})
are the horizontal lifts of
U,V\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{H})
, then

\operatorname{d}L_{g}\cdot\Omega(\tilde{U},\tilde{V})_{g}=\big(\widetilde{[U,V]}-[\widetilde{U},\widetilde{V}]\big)_{g}.

Corollary 2.23
.

A multiplicative Ehresmann connection is involutive if and only if its curvature 2-form vanishes identically.

Proof.

Let
\nabla^{\mathbf{s}}
be the pullback of the connection
\nabla
to
\mathbf{s}^{*}\mathfrak{k}
. The multiplicative connection 1-form
\alpha
vanishes on horizontal vectors. Hence, by applying the definition of
\operatorname{d}^{\nabla}
, we find for any horizontal vector fields
X,Y\in\Gamma(E)\subset\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{G})
:

\displaystyle\Omega(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{d}^{\nabla}\alpha(X,Y)

\displaystyle=\nabla^{\mathbf{s}}_{X}(\alpha(Y))-\nabla^{\mathbf{s}}_{Y}(\alpha(X))-\alpha([X,Y])

\displaystyle=\alpha(h([X,Y])-[X,Y]).

Since
h([X,Y])-[X,Y]\in K
, the definition of
\alpha
implies (
2.5
).

Let
\overline{\Omega}\in\Omega^{2}(\mathcal{G},K)
be the
K
-valued 2-form on
\mathcal{G}
defined by

\overline{\Omega}(X,Y)_{g}:=\operatorname{d}L_{g}\cdot\Omega(X,Y)_{g}.

By the first part, if
X,Y\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{G})
are horizontal vector fields, we have:

\overline{\Omega}(X,Y)=h([X,Y])-[X,Y].

(2.6)

On the other hand, the multiplicativity of
\Omega
is equivalent to:

\displaystyle\overline{\Omega}(\operatorname{d}m(v_{1},v_{2}),\operatorname{d}m(w_{1},w_{2}))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{d}R_{g_{2}}\cdot\overline{\Omega}(v_{1},w_{1})+\operatorname{d}L_{g_{1}}\cdot\overline{\Omega}(v_{2},w_{2})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{d}m(\overline{\Omega}(v_{1},w_{1}),\overline{\Omega}(v_{2},w_{2})),

(2.7)

for any pair of composable arrows
(g_{1},g_{2})\in\mathcal{G}^{(2)}
and any two pairs of composable tangent vectors
(v_{1},v_{2}),(w_{1},w_{2})\in T_{(g_{1},g_{2})}\mathcal{G}^{(2)}
. Decomposing each tangent vector into horizontal and vertical component, and using the fact that
\overline{\Omega}(v,w)
vanishes if either
v
or
w
is vertical, we see that (
2.5
) holds if and only if holds for horizontal tangent vectors.

Now observe that, since multiplication in a groupoid is a submersion, given any composable tangent vectors
(v,w)\in T_{(g_{1},g_{2})}\mathcal{G}^{(2)}
, we can find (local) vector fields
V,W,V_{i},W_{i}\in\mathfrak{X}(G)
such that

\displaystyle V=m_{*}(V_{1},V_{2}),

\displaystyle W=m_{*}(W_{1},W_{2}),

\displaystyle(v_{1},v_{2})=(V_{1},V_{2})|_{(g_{1},g_{2})},

\displaystyle(w_{1},w_{2})=(W_{1},W_{2})|_{(g_{1},g_{2})}.

Moreover,
h:T\mathcal{G}\to E
is a groupoid morphism, so it follows that:

h(V)=m_{*}(h(V_{1}),h(V_{2})),\quad h(W)=m_{*}(h(W_{1}),h(W_{2})).

Hence, to prove the multiplicativity property (
2.5
), it is enough to prove that for any
horizontal
(local) vector fields
V,W,V_{i},W_{i}\in\mathfrak{X}(G)
such that
V=m_{*}(V_{1},V_{2})
and

W=m_{*}(W_{1},W_{2})
one must have:

\overline{\Omega}(m_{*}(V_{1},V_{2}),m_{*}(W_{1},W_{2}))=m_{*}(\overline{\Omega}(V_{1},W_{1}),\overline{\Omega}(V_{2},W_{2})).

Since all vector fields are horizontal we can use (
2.6
), and we find:

\displaystyle\overline{\Omega}(m_{*}(V_{1},V_{2}),

\displaystyle m_{*}(W_{1},W_{2}))=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h([m_{*}(V_{1},V_{2}),m_{*}(W_{1},W_{2})])-[m_{*}(V_{1},V_{2}),m_{*}(W_{1},W_{2})]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle h(m_{*}([(V_{1},V_{2}),(W_{1},W_{2})]))-m_{*}([(V_{1},V_{2}),(W_{1},W_{2})])

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m_{*}(h([V_{1},W_{1}]),h([V_{2},W_{2}]))-m_{*}([V_{1},W_{1}],[V_{2},W_{2}])

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m_{*}(\overline{\Omega}(V_{1},W_{1}),\overline{\Omega}(V_{2},W_{2})),

as desired.
∎

To list further properties of the curvature, given
\mathfrak{k}
-valued forms
\beta\in\Omega^{k}(\mathcal{G},\mathfrak{k})
and
\gamma\in\Omega^{l}(\mathcal{G},\mathfrak{k})
we will denote by
[\beta,\gamma]_{\mathfrak{k}}
the
\mathfrak{k}
-valued form of degree
k+l
given by:

[\beta,\gamma]_{\mathfrak{k}}(X_{1},\dots,X_{k+l})=\sum_{\sigma\in S_{k+l}}(-1)^{|\sigma|}[\beta(X_{\sigma(1)},\dots,X_{\sigma(k)}),\gamma(X_{\sigma(k+1)},\dots,X_{\sigma(k+l)})]_{\mathfrak{k}}.

We have the following suggestive result:

Proposition 2.24
.

The curvature
\Omega
of a multiplicative Ehresmann connection with multiplicative connection 1-form
\alpha
satisfies:

(i)

Structure equation:
\Omega=\operatorname{d}^{\nabla}\alpha+\frac{1}{2}[\alpha,\alpha]_{\mathfrak{k}}
;

(ii)

Bianchi’s identity:
\operatorname{D}\Omega=0
.

Proof.

To prove (i), observe that from the definitions:

\big(\operatorname{d}^{\nabla}\alpha+\tfrac{1}{2}[\alpha,\alpha]_{\mathfrak{k}}\big)(X,Y)=\nabla^{\mathbf{s}}_{X}\alpha(Y)-\nabla^{\mathbf{s}}_{Y}\alpha(X)-\alpha([X,Y])+[\alpha(X),\alpha(Y)]_{\mathfrak{k}}.

We consider various cases:

\bullet

X=\xi^{L}
and
Y=\eta^{L}
,
\xi,\eta\in\Gamma(\mathfrak{k})
, are vertical and left invariant:
then
\Omega(X,Y)=0
. On the other hand,
\xi=\alpha(\xi^{L})|_{M}
,
\eta=\alpha(\eta^{L})|_{M}
and:

\alpha(\xi^{L})=s^{*}\xi,\quad\alpha(\eta^{L})=s^{*}\eta,\quad s_{*}\xi^{L}=s_{*}\eta^{L}=0.

Hence,
\nabla^{\mathbf{s}}_{X}\alpha(Y)=\nabla^{\mathbf{s}}_{Y}\alpha(X)=0
, and
[X,Y]=[\xi,\eta]^{L}
, so

\alpha([X,Y])=\mathbf{s}^{*}[\xi,\eta]_{\mathfrak{k}}=[\alpha(X),\alpha(Y)]_{\mathfrak{k}}.

Hence:

\big(\operatorname{d}^{\nabla}\alpha+\tfrac{1}{2}[\alpha,\alpha]_{\mathfrak{k}}\big)(X,Y)=-\alpha([X,Y])+\alpha([X,Y])=0=\Omega(X,Y).

\bullet

X
and
Y
horizontal:
then
\alpha(X)=\alpha(Y)=0
and we find:

\big(\operatorname{d}^{\nabla}\alpha+\tfrac{1}{2}[\alpha,\alpha]_{\mathfrak{k}}\big)(X,Y)=-\alpha([Y,X])=\Omega(X,Y),

where the last identity follows from the previous proposition.

\bullet

X=\xi^{L}
,
\xi\in\Gamma(\mathfrak{k})
, vertical and left invariant, and
Y
horizontal and
\mathbf{s}
-projectable to
U=\mathbf{s}_{*}(Y)\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
:
Then
\Omega(X,Y)=0
and
\alpha(Y)=0
. We claim that the following relation holds:

\nabla^{\mathbf{s}}_{Y}\alpha(X)=\alpha([Y,X]),

(2.8)

which implies (i) in this case:

\big(\operatorname{d}^{\nabla}\alpha+\tfrac{1}{2}[\alpha,\alpha]_{\mathfrak{k}}\big)(X,Y)=-\alpha([Y,X])+\alpha([X,Y])=0=\Omega(X,Y).

To prove (
2.8
), we calculate Lie bracket using the commutator of the flows:

[X,Y]_{g}=\frac{\operatorname{d}}{\operatorname{d}\varepsilon}\Big|_{\varepsilon=0}\frac{\operatorname{d}}{\operatorname{d}t}\Big|_{t=0}c_{t,\varepsilon}(g),

where:

c_{t,\varepsilon}(g):=\phi_{\xi^{L}}^{-\varepsilon}\circ\phi_{Y}^{-t}\circ\phi_{\xi^{L}}^{\varepsilon}\circ\phi_{Y}^{t}(g)=\phi_{Y}^{-t}\big(\phi_{Y}^{t}(g)\cdot\exp(\varepsilon\xi|_{\phi_{U}^{t}(x)})\big)\cdot\exp(-\varepsilon\xi|_{x}),

with
x=\mathbf{s}(g)
and
\exp:\mathfrak{k}\to\mathcal{G}
the exponential map of the bundle of Lie algebras
\mathfrak{k}
. Note that in the non-Hausdorff case, the flows might not be unique - however, they do exist in local charts, and this suffices to prove (
2.8
). We find first:

\displaystyle\frac{\operatorname{d}}{\operatorname{d}t}\Big|_{t=0}c_{t,\varepsilon}(g)=\operatorname{d}R_{k_{\varepsilon}^{-1}}\Big(-Y|_{g\cdot k_{\varepsilon}}+\operatorname{d}m(Y|_{g},\frac{\operatorname{d}}{\operatorname{d}t}\Big|_{t=0}\exp(\varepsilon\xi|_{\phi_{U}^{t}(x)}))\Big)\in T_{g}\mathcal{G},

where
k_{\varepsilon}:=\exp(\varepsilon\xi|_{x})
and
R_{k}
denotes right translation by
k
. Using that
\alpha
is multiplicative and vanishes on
E
, we then have:

\displaystyle\alpha\Big(\frac{\operatorname{d}}{\operatorname{d}t}\Big|_{t=0}c_{t,\varepsilon}(g)\Big)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Ad}_{k_{\varepsilon}}\circ\,\alpha\Big(\operatorname{d}m\big(Y|_{g},\frac{\operatorname{d}}{\operatorname{d}t}\Big|_{t=0}\exp(\varepsilon\xi|_{\phi_{U}^{t}(x)})\big)\Big)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Ad}_{k_{\varepsilon}}\circ\,\alpha\Big(\frac{\operatorname{d}}{\operatorname{d}t}\Big|_{t=0}\exp(\varepsilon\xi|_{\phi_{U}^{t}(x)})\Big).

The exponential map factors as
\exp=i\circ\exp^{\mathfrak{k}}
, where
\exp^{\mathfrak{k}}:\mathfrak{k}\to\mathcal{G}(\mathfrak{k})
is the exponential map of the bundle of 1-connected Lie groups
\mathcal{G}(\mathfrak{k})
integrating
\mathfrak{k}
, and
i:\mathcal{G}(\mathfrak{k})\hookrightarrow\mathcal{G}
the induced immersion. By Corollary
2.11
, the linear connection
\nabla
on
\mathfrak{k}
is obtained by differentiating the Ehresmann connection
\operatorname{d}i^{-1}(E)\subset T\mathcal{G}(\mathfrak{k})
and, by Proposition
2.10
,
\exp^{\mathfrak{k}}:\mathfrak{k}\to\mathcal{G}(\mathfrak{k})
is connection preserving around the zero-section. So we have the following decomposition into horizontal and vertical component:

\frac{\operatorname{d}}{\operatorname{d}t}\Big|_{t=0}\exp\big(\varepsilon\xi|_{\phi_{U}^{t}(x)}\big)=\widetilde{U}_{k_{\varepsilon}}\oplus\frac{\operatorname{d}}{\operatorname{d}t}\Big|_{t=0}\exp\big(\varepsilon(\xi+t\nabla_{U}\xi)|_{x}\big)\in E_{k_{\varepsilon}}\oplus K_{k_{\varepsilon}},

for some
\widetilde{U}_{k_{\varepsilon}}\in E_{k_{\varepsilon}}
that projects to
U|_{x}=\operatorname{d}\mathbf{s}(Y|_{g})
. Therefore, we have that:

\displaystyle\alpha\Big(\frac{\operatorname{d}}{\operatorname{d}t}\Big|_{t=0}c_{t,\varepsilon}(g)\Big)

\displaystyle=\frac{\operatorname{d}}{\operatorname{d}t}\Big|_{t=0}\exp\big(\varepsilon(\xi+t\nabla_{U}\xi)|_{x}\big)\exp\big(-\varepsilon\xi|_{x}\big)\in\mathfrak{k}_{x}.

and so we conclude that:

\alpha([X,Y]_{g})=\alpha\Big(\frac{\operatorname{d}}{\operatorname{d}\varepsilon}\Big|_{\varepsilon=0}\frac{\operatorname{d}}{\operatorname{d}t}\Big|_{t=0}c_{t,\varepsilon}(g)\Big|_{t=\varepsilon=0}\Big)=(\nabla_{U}\xi)\big|_{x}.

But then:

\big(\nabla^{\mathbf{s}}_{Y}\alpha(X)\big)\big|_{g}=\mathbf{s}^{*}(\nabla_{\mathbf{s}_{*}(Y)}\xi)|_{g}=\nabla_{U}\xi|_{x}=\alpha([Y,X]|_{g}).

so we obtained (
2.8
).

Since the vectors fields of the types considered in the cases above span all tangent vectors, item (i) is proven.

To prove (ii), note that
\operatorname{D}\Omega(X,Y,Z)
vanishes if one of the vectors is vertical. So we can assume that
X,Y,Z\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{G})
are horizontal vector fields, and we find:

\displaystyle\operatorname{D}\Omega(X,Y,Z)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{d}^{\nabla}\Omega(X,Y,Z)

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{d}^{\nabla})^{2}\alpha(X,Y,Z)+[\operatorname{d}^{\nabla}\alpha,\alpha]_{\mathfrak{k}}(X,Y,Z)

\displaystyle=R^{\nabla}(X,Y)\alpha(Z)+R^{\nabla}(Z,X)\alpha(Y)+R^{\nabla}(Y,Z)\alpha(X)=0,

since
\alpha(X)=\alpha(Y)=\alpha(Z)=0
.
∎

Remark 2.25
.

For connections on a Lie groupoid extension
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
the authors of
[
25
]
also introduce the curvature 2-form and prove analogues of the structure equations and Bianchi identity. See loc. cit. Section 4.6.

Consider now a surjective, submersive, Lie groupoid morphism
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
. Given a multiplicative Ehresmann connection
E
for
\Phi
we have the induced connection
E^{\mathcal{K}}
on the kernel
p:\mathcal{K}\to M
and the linear connection
\nabla:=\nabla^{E}
on
\mathfrak{k}=\ker\operatorname{d}p|_{M}
. The curvature of
E^{\mathcal{K}}
is the multiplicative 2-form
i^{*}\Omega
, where
i:\mathcal{K}\to\mathcal{G}
is the inclusion and
\Omega
is the curvature of
E
. By Proposition
2.22
, this form is multiplicative.

Proposition 2.26
.

The IM 2-form associated with
i^{*}\Omega\in\Omega^{2}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathcal{K},\mathfrak{k})
is given by
(R^{\nabla},0)\in\Omega^{2}_{\mathrm{IM}}(\mathfrak{k},\mathfrak{k})
. In particular, if
E^{\mathcal{K}}
is involutive then
R^{\nabla}=0
, and the converse holds if
\mathcal{K}
has connected fibers.

Proof.

Denote the horizontal lift of a vector field
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
to
\mathcal{K}
by
\widetilde{X}\in\Gamma(E^{\mathcal{K}})
. Then Proposition
2.22
, implies that

(i^{*}\Omega)(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{Y})_{k}=\operatorname{d}L_{k^{-1}}\big(\widetilde{[X,Y]}-[\widetilde{X},\widetilde{Y}]\big)\in\mathfrak{k}_{p(k)}.

To find the IM form corresponding to
i^{*}\Omega
we apply (
A.5
) and (
A.6
). For the symbol we obtain, for
\xi\in\Gamma(\mathfrak{k})
,

l(\xi)=(i_{\xi^{L}}i^{*}\Omega)|_{TM}=0,

since
\xi^{L}\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{K})
is a vertical vector field.
On the other hand, the flow of
\xi^{L}
is given by
\phi^{t}_{\xi^{L}}(k)=k\exp(t\xi)
, so for
X,Y\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
we find

\displaystyle(i^{*}\Omega)(\operatorname{d}\phi_{\alpha^{L}}^{t}(X),\operatorname{d}\phi_{\alpha^{L}}^{t}(Y))

\displaystyle=(i^{*}\Omega)(\operatorname{d}R_{\exp(t\xi)}(X),\operatorname{d}R_{\exp(t\xi)}(Y))

\displaystyle=(i^{*}\Omega)(h(\operatorname{d}R_{\exp(t\xi)}(X)),h(\operatorname{d}R_{\exp(t\xi)}(Y)))

\displaystyle=(i^{*}\Omega)(\widetilde{X},\widetilde{Y})_{\exp(t\xi)}.

Then (
A.6
) reduces to:

L(\xi)(X,Y)=\frac{\operatorname{d}}{\operatorname{d}t}\Big|_{t=0}\operatorname{d}R_{\exp(-t\xi)}\big(\widetilde{[X,Y]}-[\widetilde{X},\widetilde{Y}]\big)_{{}_{\exp(t\xi)}}.

The exponential
\exp:\mathfrak{k}\to\mathcal{K}
maps the
\nabla
-horizontal lift
\overline{X}\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathfrak{k})
to the
E^{\mathcal{K}}
-horizontal lift
\widetilde{X}\in\mathfrak{X}(\mathcal{K})
(see Proposition
2.10
).
It follows then that:

L(\xi)(X,Y)=\frac{\operatorname{d}}{\operatorname{d}t}\Big|_{t=0}\big(\overline{[X,Y]}-[\overline{X},\overline{Y}]\big)_{t\xi}=R^{\nabla}(X,Y)(\xi).

The multiplicative 2-form
i^{*}(\Omega)
vanishes on the bundle
\mathcal{K}^{0}
of connected components of the identities if and only if the corresponding IM 2-form
(R^{\nabla},0)
vanishes. This implies the last part of the statement.
∎

2.6.
Flat multiplicative connections

Given a multiplicative Ehresmann connections
E
for a morphism
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
, there are various levels of flatness one can require.

We start by looking at the case where the induced Ehresmann connection
E^{\mathcal{K}}
is involutive, and hence, by Proposition
2.26
, the linear connection
\nabla
on
\mathfrak{k}
is flat.

Definition 2.27
.

A multiplicative Ehresmann connection
E
for a groupoid morphism
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
is called
kernel flat
if
E^{\mathcal{K}}
is involutive.

In order to state the basic properties of kernel flat connections, let us make the following general observation about
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
. The
\mathcal{G}
-action on
\mathfrak{k}
restricts to a
\mathcal{K}
-action on
\mathfrak{k}
which is just the fiberwise adjoint action. Hence, its fixed point set is a collection of vector subspaces of the center:

\mathfrak{k}^{\mathcal{K}}\subset z(\mathfrak{k}).

In general, this fails to be a vector subbundle. It is one if either:

(a)

\mathfrak{k}
is locally trivial and
\mathcal{K}
has connected fibers, in which case
\mathfrak{k}^{\mathcal{K}}=z(\mathfrak{k})
, or

(b)

\mathcal{K}
is locally trivial, in which case one can have
\mathfrak{k}^{\mathcal{K}}\subsetneq z(\mathfrak{k})
.

The
\mathcal{G}
-action preserves
\mathfrak{k}^{\mathcal{K}}
and descends to an
\mathcal{H}
-action on
\mathfrak{k}^{\mathcal{K}}
. So when
\mathfrak{k}^{\mathcal{K}}
is subbundle we obtain a representation of
\mathcal{H}
.

Lemma 2.28
.

If
\mathfrak{k}^{\mathcal{K}}
is a subbundle then the linear connection
\nabla
restricts on
\mathfrak{k}^{\mathcal{K}}
to an
\mathcal{H}
-invariant connection.

Proof.

Consider the center of the bundle of Lie groups
p:\mathcal{K}\to M
, denoted

Z(\mathcal{K}):=\{z\in\mathcal{K}:kzk^{-1}=z,\ \forall\ k\in p^{-1}(p(z))\},

and its connected components of the identity, denoted
Z(\mathcal{K})^{\circ}
. Under the assumption that
\mathfrak{k}^{\mathcal{K}}
is a subbundle of
\mathfrak{k}
, this is a bundle of Lie groups integrating
\mathfrak{k}^{\mathcal{K}}

\mathfrak{k}^{\mathcal{K}}_{x}=T_{1_{x}}Z(\mathcal{K})^{\circ},\quad(x\in M).

We claim that for all
z\in Z(\mathcal{K})^{\circ}
we have:

E^{\mathcal{K}}_{z}\subset T_{z}Z(\mathcal{K})^{\circ}.

This claim implies that
\nabla
restricts to a linear connection on
\mathfrak{k}^{\mathcal{K}}
.

To prove the claim, we consider any smooth curve
z_{t}\in Z(\mathcal{K})^{\circ}
and show that, given the decomposition into vertical and horizontal vectors:

\dot{z}_{0}=\dot{z}_{0}^{h}\oplus\dot{z}_{0}^{v}\in E^{\mathcal{K}}_{z_{0}}\oplus K_{z_{0}},

we have
\dot{z}_{0}^{v}\in T_{z_{0}}Z(\mathcal{K})^{\circ}
. Then
\dot{z}_{0}^{h}\in T_{z_{0}}Z(\mathcal{K})^{\circ}
and the claim follows. Let
k\in\mathcal{K}_{p(z_{0})}
and
k_{t}
be the parallel transport of
k
along the curve
p(z_{t})
. Then:

z_{t}=k_{t}z_{t}k^{-1}_{t}.

Differentiating both sides at
t=0
and decomposing into horizontal and vertical components, using the multiplicativity of
E^{\mathcal{K}}
, we obtain:

\dot{z}_{0}^{v}=0_{k}\cdot\dot{z}_{0}^{v}\cdot 0_{k^{-1}},

where multiplication and inverse are the operations in the tangent group
T(\mathcal{K}_{p(z_{0})})
. Writing
\dot{z}_{0}^{v}=0_{z_{0}}\cdot w
, with
w\in\mathfrak{k}_{p(z_{0})}
, and using that
z_{0}\in Z(\mathcal{K})
, we obtain:

w=\operatorname{Ad}_{k}(w),\quad\forall\,k\in\mathcal{K}.

Hence
w\in\mathfrak{k}^{\mathcal{K}}
, and so
\dot{z}_{0}^{v}\in T_{z_{0}}Z(\mathcal{K})^{\circ}
.

We now show that
\nabla
restricts on
\mathfrak{k}^{\mathcal{K}}
to a
\mathcal{H}
-invariant connection. For this, it is enough to show that if
z_{t}\in Z(\mathcal{K})^{\circ}
is a horizontal curve and
h_{t}\in\mathcal{H}
is any curve with
\mathbf{s}(h_{t})=p(z_{t})
then
h_{t}\cdot z_{t}
is a horizontal curve. For this observe that

h_{t}\cdot z_{t}=\widetilde{h}_{t}\cdot z_{t},

where
\widetilde{h}_{t}\in\mathcal{G}
is any curve with
\Phi(\widetilde{h}_{t})=h_{t}
. In particular, we can take
\widetilde{h}_{t}
to be the horizontal lift of
h_{t}
and multiplicativity of
E
gives that
h_{t}\cdot z_{t}
is horizontal.
∎

Let us now assume that
E
is kernel flat:

Proposition 2.29
.

Let
E
be a kernel flat multiplicative Ehresmann connection for a morphism
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
. Then:

(i)

The curvature
\Omega
takes values in
\mathfrak{k}^{\mathcal{K}}
;

(ii)

If
\mathfrak{k}^{\mathcal{K}}
is a vector bundle, there is a multiplicative form
\underline{\Omega}\in\Omega^{2}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathcal{H},\mathfrak{k}^{\mathcal{K}})
such that:

\Omega=\Phi^{*}\underline{\Omega},

and which is
\operatorname{d}^{\nabla}
-closed.

Proof.

Let
g_{1},g_{2}\in\mathcal{G}
such that
\Phi(g_{1})=h=\Phi(g_{2})
. Let
X,Y\in T_{h}\mathcal{H}
, and consider their horizontal lifts
\widetilde{X}_{g_{i}},\widetilde{Y}_{g_{i}}\in T_{g_{i}}\mathcal{G}
, for
i=1,2
. First, write
g_{2}=g_{1}k
, for
k\in\mathcal{K}
. Let
\overline{X}_{k},\overline{Y}_{k}\in T_{k}\mathcal{K}
be the horizontal lift of
\operatorname{d}_{h}\mathbf{s}X
and
\operatorname{d}_{h}\mathbf{s}Y
with respect to the connection
E^{\mathcal{K}}
. Since
E
is a subgroupoid of
T\mathcal{G}
, we have that:

\operatorname{d}m(\widetilde{X}_{g_{1}},\overline{X}_{k})=\widetilde{X}_{g_{2}},\quad\operatorname{d}m(\widetilde{Y}_{g_{1}},\overline{Y}_{k})=\widetilde{Y}_{g_{2}}.

Since
\Omega
is multiplicative, this yields:

\Omega(\widetilde{X}_{g_{2}},\widetilde{Y}_{g_{2}})=k^{-1}\cdot\Omega(\widetilde{X}_{g_{1}},\widetilde{Y}_{g_{1}})+\Omega(\overline{X}_{k},\overline{Y}_{k})=k^{-1}\cdot\Omega(\widetilde{X}_{g_{1}},\widetilde{Y}_{g_{1}}),

where in the last equation we used that
E^{\mathcal{K}}
is flat, so
i^{*}\Omega=0
. Next, write
g_{2}=lg_{1}
, with
l\in\mathcal{K}
and let
\overline{X}_{l},\overline{Y}_{l}\in T_{l}\mathcal{K}
be the horizontal lift of
\operatorname{d}_{h}\mathbf{t}(X)
and
\operatorname{d}_{h}\mathbf{t}(Y)
with respect to the connection
E^{\mathcal{K}}
. Then we also have that:

\operatorname{d}m(\overline{X}_{l},\widetilde{X}_{g_{1}})=\widetilde{X}_{g_{2}},\quad\operatorname{d}m(\overline{Y}_{l},\widetilde{Y}_{g_{1}})=\widetilde{Y}_{g_{2}},

and so, since
\Omega
is multiplicative:

\Omega(\widetilde{X}_{g_{2}},\widetilde{Y}_{g_{2}})=g_{1}^{-1}\cdot\Omega(\overline{X}_{l},\overline{Y}_{l})+\Omega(\widetilde{X}_{g_{1}},\widetilde{Y}_{g_{1}})=\Omega(\widetilde{X}_{g_{1}},\widetilde{Y}_{g_{1}}).

Since
\Omega
vanishes on vertical vectors, the obtained equations show that
\Omega
takes values in
\mathfrak{k}^{\mathcal{K}}
. They also show that we obtain a well-defined
\mathfrak{k}^{\mathcal{K}}
-valued 2-form on
\mathcal{H}
, by setting for
X,Y\in T_{h}\mathcal{H}
:

\underline{\Omega}(X,Y)=\Omega(\widetilde{X}_{g},\widetilde{Y}_{g})\quad(g\in\Phi^{-1}(h)).

When
\mathfrak{k}^{\mathcal{K}}
is a vector bundle the
\mathcal{G}
-action factors through the
\mathcal{H}
-action, and the multiplicativity of
\underline{\Omega}
follows from the multiplicativity of
\Omega
.

The fact that
\underline{\Omega}
is
\operatorname{d}^{\nabla}
-closed follows from the Bianchi identity for
\Omega
.
∎

Remark 2.30
.

Kernel flat connections play an important role in the theory of gerbes developed in
[
25
]
. There, for a groupoid extension, the authors obtain a version of the previous proposition (see loc. sit. Theorem 6.38).

We now describe another type of flatness. Observe that, if
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
is a surjective, submersive, morphism covering the identity, for each
x\in M
the restriction:

\Phi_{x}:=\Phi|_{\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{G}}^{-1}(x)}:\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{G}}^{-1}(x)\to\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}(x).

is a principal
\mathcal{K}_{x}
-bundle. Given a multiplicative Ehresmann connection
E
for a
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
one obtains an Ehresmann connection on the restriction:

\ker\operatorname{d}_{g}\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{G}}=K_{g}\oplus E_{g}^{x},\quad E^{x}_{g}:=\ker\operatorname{d}_{g}\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{G}}\cap E\quad(g\in\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{G}}^{-1}(x)).

Lemma 2.31
.

For each
x\in M
, the restricted connection
E^{x}
is a principal bundle connection.

Proof.

Using the multiplicativity of
E
one finds that if
k\in\mathcal{K}_{x}
and
v\in E^{x}_{g}
, for
g\in\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{G}}^{-1}(x)
, then:

v\cdot k=\operatorname{d}_{(g,k)}m(v,0_{k})\in E^{x}_{gk}.

Hence,
E^{x}
is
\mathcal{K}_{x}
-invariant.
∎

If
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathcal{G},\mathfrak{k})
and
\Omega\in\Omega^{2}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathcal{G},\mathfrak{k})
are the multiplicative connection 1-form and curvature form of
E
then the principal bundle connection
E^{x}
has connection 1-form
\alpha^{x}\in\Omega^{1}(\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{G}}^{-1}(x),\mathfrak{k}_{x})
and curvature form
\Omega^{x}\in\Omega^{2}(\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{G}}^{-1}(x),\mathfrak{k}_{x})
the restrictions:

\alpha^{x}=i^{*}_{x}\alpha,\quad\Omega^{x}=i^{*}_{x}\Omega,

where
i_{x}:\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{G}}^{-1}(x)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{G}
is the inclusion.

Definition 2.32
.

A multiplicative Ehresmann connection
E
for a groupoid morphism
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
is called
leafwise flat
if
\Omega^{x}=0
for every
x\in M
.

We have the following characterizations:

Lemma 2.33
.

Given a multiplicative Ehresmann connection
E
for a groupoid morphism
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
the following are equivalent:

(i)

E
is leafwise flat;

(ii)

\ker\operatorname{d}\mathbf{s}\cap E\subset T\mathcal{G}
is involutive;

(iii)

B^{\prime}:=(\ker\operatorname{d}\mathbf{s}\cap E)|_{M}\subset A
is a Lie subalgebroid.

Proof.

Clearly, (i) and (ii) are equivalent. Equivalence between (ii) and (iii) uses that
\ker\operatorname{d}\mathbf{s}\cap E
is spanned by right invariant extensions of elements in
B^{\prime}
.
∎

Remark 2.34
.

The pair
(\mathcal{G},E)
is an example of a Pfaffian groupoid (see
[
31
]
).

The lemma shows that if
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
admits a leafwise flat multiplicative Ehresmann connection, then the induced Lie algebroid map
\phi:A\to B
admits a Lie algebroid splitting with image
B^{\prime}
. We briefly recall the groupoid version of this condition.

Let
\mathcal{H}\rightrightarrows M
be a Lie groupoid which acts on a bundle of Lie groups
p:\mathcal{K}\to M
preserving the group structure on the fibers. The
semi-direct product
is the Lie groupoid
\mathcal{G}:=\mathcal{H}\times_{M}\mathcal{K}\rightrightarrows M
with multiplication is given by:

(h_{1},k_{1})(h_{2},k_{2})=(h_{1}h_{2},(h_{2}^{-1}\cdot k_{1})k_{2}).

This fits into a short exact sequence of groupoids:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}\times_{M}\mathcal{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{1}}

which is canonically split by the groupoid morphism:

\sigma:\mathcal{H}\longrightarrow\mathcal{H}\times_{M}\mathcal{K},\quad h\mapsto(h,1_{\mathbf{s}(h)}).

Conversely, given a surjective, submersive, Lie groupoid map
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
covering the identity, which admits a
Lie groupoid splitting
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{K}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{H}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}

we obtain an action of
\mathcal{H}\rightrightarrows M
on the bundle of Lie groups
p:\mathcal{K}\to M
:

h\cdot k=\sigma(h)k\sigma(h)^{-1},

preserving the group structure on the fibers, and a Lie groupoid isomorphism:

\mathcal{H}\times_{M}\mathcal{K}\simeq\mathcal{G},\quad(h,k)\mapsto\sigma(h)k.

Given a leafwise flat multiplicative Ehresmann
E
for a groupoid map
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
, the induced Lie algebroid splitting integrates to a groupoid morphism on a possibly different Lie groupoid with the same Lie algebroid as
\mathcal{H}
. To explain this, let
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}\rightrightarrows M
be the universal covering groupoid of
\mathcal{H}^{0}\rightrightarrows M
, the connected component of the identity of
\mathcal{H}\rightrightarrows M
. Recall that it consists of
\mathbf{s}
-leafwise path-homotopy classes of paths in
\mathcal{H}
starting at the identity:

\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}=\big\{[\gamma]~|~\gamma:[0,1]\to\mathcal{H},\,\mathbf{s}(\gamma(t))=x,\,\gamma(0)=1_{x},\,x\in M\big\}.

Recall that principal bundle connections are always complete, so the horizontal lifts of
\mathbf{s}
-leafwise paths are always defined, and we can set:

Definition 2.35
.

Given a leafwise flat, multiplicative, Ehresmann connection
E
for
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
the
holonomy
of
E
is the map:

\operatorname{Hol}:\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}\to\mathcal{G},\quad[\gamma]\mapsto\widetilde{\gamma}(1),

where
\widetilde{\gamma}:[0,1]\to\mathcal{G}
is the horizontal lift of
\gamma
starting at
1_{\mathbf{s}(\gamma(0))}
.

Proposition 2.36
.

The holonomy of a leafwise flat, multiplicative Ehresmann connection
E
for
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
is a Lie groupoid morphism making the following diagram commute:

\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{Hol}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi}
\textstyle{\mathcal{H}}

Proof.

The smoothness of
\operatorname{Hol}
follows from smoothness of flows of horizontal vector fields. Clearly, from the definition
\operatorname{Hol}(1_{x})=1_{x}
. The fact that
\Phi
preserves multiplication follows from the fact that products of horizontal lifts are horizontal and the definition of multiplication on
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}
.
∎

Notice that the kernel of
\operatorname{Hol}:\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}\to\mathcal{G}
is contained in the kernel of the covering map
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}\to\mathcal{H}
.
We will say that a leafwise flat, multiplicative Ehresmann connection
E
for
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
has
trivial holonomy
if the two kernels coincide. Then we find:

Corollary 2.37
.

Let
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
be a morphism covering the identity and assume
\mathcal{H}
is source connected. If
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
admits a leafwise flat, multiplicative, Ehresmann connection with trivial holonomy then it admits a Lie groupoid splitting.

Proof.

If the holonomy is trivial, then the holonomy morphism
\operatorname{Hol}:\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}\to\mathcal{G}
descends to a groupoid splitting
\sigma:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{G}
.
∎

We will see later (Corollary
4.3
) that when
\mathcal{G}
is proper and
\ker\Phi
is abelian, then the converse holds: if
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
admits a Lie groupoid splitting then it admits a leafwise flat, multiplicative Ehresmann connection with trivial holonomy.

Next, we look for the conditions which ensure that a semi-direct product admits a leafwise flat, multiplicative Ehresmann connection. For that, we introduce an extension of Definition
A.6
to non-linear connections.

Definition 2.38
.

Let
\mathcal{H}\rightrightarrows M
be a Lie groupoid which acts on a submersion
p:N\to M
. An Ehresmann connection
E
on
p:N\to M
is called
\mathcal{H}
-invariant
if the diffeomorphism:

\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern
