8.071pt{p}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.5361pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.5361pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.19138pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.56526pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mathbf{t}}}}}\times^{{\kern-10.75204pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.5361pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.5361pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.19138pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.56526pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 5.6321pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.46532pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.76094pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-7.30847pt{\mathbf{t}}\kern 5.6321pt{p}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.5361pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.5361pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.19138pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.56526pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mathbf{t}}}}}\times^{{\kern-9.66316pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.5361pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.5361pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.19138pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.56526pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.54321pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.46532pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.76094pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-6.21959pt{\mathbf{t}}\kern 4.54321pt{p}}}}N\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 126.23767pt\raise-21.01215pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-22.27777pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 112.73766pt\raise-22.27777pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.55554pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{H}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}}\qquad(g,p)\mapsto(g,g\cdot p),

induces an isomorphism
\operatorname{d}\Psi:(\operatorname{d}\mathbf{s})^{*}E\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-1.68887pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\smash{$\sim$}}}(\operatorname{d}\mathbf{t})^{*}E
.

Note that in the distributions
(\operatorname{d}\mathbf{s})^{*}E
and
(\operatorname{d}\mathbf{t})^{*}E
appearing in this definition are Ehresmann connections for the projections in the diagram. We now have:

Proposition 2.39
.

Let
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
be a surjective, submersive groupoid map, and let
\mathcal{K}:=\ker\Phi
. The following are equivalent:

(i)

There is a multiplicative Ehresmann connection
E
on
\mathcal{G}
and
\Phi
admits a groupoid splitting
\sigma:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{G}
which is horizontal with respect to
E
.

(ii)

\mathcal{G}
is isomorphic to a semidirect product
\mathcal{G}\simeq\mathcal{H}\times_{M}\mathcal{K}
, where
\mathcal{K}
admits an
\mathcal{H}
-invariant multiplicative connection.

If these hold, then
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
admits a leafwise flat multiplicative Ehresmann connection with trivial holonomy.

Proof.

We first prove (i)
\Rightarrow
(ii). For this, we use
\sigma
to identify
\mathcal{G}\simeq\mathcal{H}\times_{M}\mathcal{K}
. Then any path of the form
t\mapsto(h_{t},1_{\mathbf{s}(h_{t})})
is
E
-horizontal. We claim that the multiplicative connection
E^{\mathcal{K}}=T\mathcal{K}\cap E
on
\mathcal{K}
is
\mathcal{H}
-invariant. To see this let
h:[0,1]\to\mathcal{H}
be any path, and let
k:[0,1]\to\mathcal{K}
be an
E^{\mathcal{K}}
-horizontal path covering
t\mapsto\mathbf{s}(h_{t})
. Then
t\mapsto(h_{t},k_{t})\in\mathcal{H}\times_{M}\mathcal{K}
is horizontal for the connection
(\operatorname{d}\mathbf{s})^{*}E^{\mathcal{K}}
. Moreover, all
(\operatorname{d}\mathbf{s})^{*}E^{\mathcal{K}}
-horizontal paths are of this form. It suffices to show that the pair
t\mapsto(h_{t},h_{t}\cdot k_{t})
is horizontal for
(\operatorname{d}\mathbf{t})^{*}E^{\mathcal{K}}
, or equivalently, that
t\mapsto h_{t}\cdot k_{t}
is horizontal for
E^{\mathcal{K}}
. This follows because
t\mapsto(h_{t},1_{\mathbf{s}(h_{t})})
and
t\mapsto(1_{\mathbf{s}(h_{t})},k_{t})
are
E
-horizontal,
E
is multiplicative, and so also the following product is
E
-horizontal:

t\mapsto(h_{t},1_{\mathbf{s}(h_{t})})\cdot(1_{\mathbf{s}(h_{t})},k_{t})\cdot(h_{t},1_{\mathbf{s}(h_{t})})^{-1}=(1_{\mathbf{t}(h_{t})},h_{t}\cdot k_{t}).

Conversely, to prove that (ii)
\Rightarrow
(i). If
E^{\mathcal{K}}
is an
\mathcal{H}
-invariant multiplicative Ehresmann connection on
p:\mathcal{K}\to M
, then
E=(\operatorname{d}\mathbf{s})^{*}E^{\mathcal{K}}
is an Ehresmann connection on
\mathcal{H}\times_{M}\mathcal{K}
for which a path
(h_{t},k_{t})\in\mathcal{H}\times_{M}\mathcal{K}
is
E
-horizontal if and only if
k_{t}
is
E^{\mathcal{K}}
-horizontal. In particular, the splitting
h\to(h,1_{\mathbf{s}(h)})
is horizontal. To show that
E
is multiplicative it suffices to show that for any pair of paths
(h_{t},k_{t})
and
(\tilde{h}_{t},\tilde{k}_{t})
in
\mathcal{H}\times_{M}\mathcal{K}
that are pointwise composable and
E
-horizontal, their product is also
E
-horizontal. But
E^{\mathcal{K}}
is
\mathcal{H}
-invariant, so we have that
\tilde{h}_{t}^{-1}\cdot k_{t}
is
E^{\mathcal{K}}
-horizontal. Since
E^{\mathcal{K}}
is multiplicative, also
(\tilde{h}_{t}^{-1}\cdot k_{t})\,\tilde{k}_{t}
is
E^{\mathcal{K}}
-horizontal, or equivalently,
(h_{t},k_{t})\cdot(\tilde{h}_{t},\tilde{k}_{t})
is
E
-horizontal. Since

For the last part of the proposition, notice that the connection
E=(\operatorname{d}\mathbf{s})^{*}E^{\mathcal{K}}
is leafwise flat with trivial holonomy.
∎

For semi-direct products with abelian kernel we obtain:

Corollary 2.40
.

Let
\mathcal{H}\rightrightarrows M
be a Lie groupoid which acts on a bundle of abelian groups
p:A\to M
preserving addition on the fibers. Then
\operatorname{pr}_{1}:\mathcal{H}\times_{M}A\to\mathcal{H}
admits a multiplicative Ehresmann connection if and only if
p:A\to M
admits an
\mathcal{H}
-invariant multiplicative connection.

Proof.

Assume that
\operatorname{pr}_{1}:\mathcal{H}\times_{M}A\to\mathcal{H}
admits a multiplicative Ehresmann connection with connection 1-form
\alpha
. We can insure that
\sigma^{*}\alpha=0
, by replacing
\alpha
by
\alpha-\operatorname{pr}_{1}^{*}\sigma^{*}(\alpha)
. This form is multiplicative because
A
being abelian implies that the action of
\mathcal{H}\times_{M}A
on
A
is the pullback of the action of
\mathcal{H}
on
A
. The corollary now follows from the proposition.
∎

For semi-direct products which have linear kernel, we obtain the following:

Corollary 2.41
.

Given a Lie groupoid
\mathcal{H}\rightrightarrows M
and a representation of
\mathcal{H}
on a vector bundle
p:V\to M
, the projection
\operatorname{pr}_{1}:\mathcal{H}\times_{M}V\to\mathcal{H}
admits a multiplicative Ehresmann connection if and only if
V
admits an
\mathcal{H}
-invariant connection
\nabla
.

Proof.

Note that an Ehresmann connection
E^{V}\subset TV
is multiplicative if and only if it defines a linear connection
\nabla
. Also note that, for linear connections on
V
, the two notions of
\mathcal{H}
-invariance from Definitions
2.38
and
A.6
coincide. Hence, the corollary follows from the previous one.
∎

Finally, we discuss the situation where a multiplicative Ehresmann connection
E
for a morphism
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
is involutive, in which case we say that
E
is
totally flat
. The following proposition shows that when the connection is complete then, up to a cover,
\mathcal{G}
is a trivial product and
\Phi
is the projection:

Proposition 2.42
.

Let
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
be a morphism which admits a complete, totally flat, multiplicative, Ehresmann connection, and assume that
\mathcal{G}
is target connected. Then there is a covering space of the base
p:\widetilde{M}\to M
and a commutative diagram of Lie groupoid morphisms

\textstyle{p^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}\times G\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{pr}}
\textstyle{p^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi}
\textstyle{\mathcal{H}}

where
G\simeq\mathcal{K}_{x}
is a Lie group, the vertical maps are surjective local diffeomorphisms covering
p:\widetilde{M}\to M
, and the pullback of the Ehresmann connection under the morphism on the left is
T(p^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}})\times 0_{G}
.

Proof.

By completeness, the parallel transport exists along a path
h:[0,1]\to\mathcal{H}
:

\tau_{h}:\Phi^{-1}(h(0))\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-1.59442pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\smash{$\sim$}}}\Phi^{-1}(h(1)).

We note the following equalities, for any
h:[0,1]\to\mathcal{H}
, with
h(0)=1_{x}
and
\mathbf{s}\circ h=x
:

\tau_{h}(k)=\tau_{h}(1_{x})k=\tau_{1_{\mathbf{t}\circ h}}(k)\tau_{h}(1_{x}),\quad(k\in\mathcal{K}_{x})

(2.9)

These follow because
E
is multiplicative, so all the paths above are
E
-horizontal, they cover
h
and start at
k
.

Since
E
is leafwise flat, the holonomy map of Definition
2.35
is a groupoid map:

\operatorname{Hol}:\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}\to\mathcal{G},\quad[h]\mapsto\tau_{h}(1_{x}),\quad x=\mathbf{s}([h])

where
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}
is the target 1-connected cover of
\mathcal{H}
- which has connected
\mathbf{t}
-fibers, because
\mathcal{G}
has. We have an induced action of
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}
on
\mathcal{K}
by group automorphisms, and so a surjective groupoid morphism

\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}\times_{M}\mathcal{K}\to\mathcal{G},\quad([h],k)\mapsto\tau_{h}(1_{x})k.

From (
2.9
) we deduce that the action of
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}
on
\mathcal{K}
is given by:

[h]\cdot k:=\tau_{h}(1_{x})k\tau_{h}(1_{x})^{-1}=\tau_{1_{\mathbf{t}\circ h}}(k),\quad k\in\mathcal{K}_{x},\quad x=\mathbf{s}([h]).

In other words,
[h]
acts via the parallel transport on
{\mathcal{K}}
along its base path
[\mathbf{t}\circ h]
with respect to the flat connection
E^{\mathcal{K}}
. Consider the holonomy cover
p:\widetilde{M}\to M
for
E^{\mathcal{K}}
, i.e., the smallest cover where
E^{\mathcal{K}}
becomes the trivial connection. Upon pullback to
\widetilde{M}
,
p^{*}\mathcal{K}=\widetilde{M}\times G
, where
G\simeq\mathcal{K}_{x}
, and the action of
p^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}
on
p^{*}\mathcal{K}
becomes the trivial action; hence we have a groupoid isomorphism:
p^{*}(\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}\times_{M}\mathcal{K})\simeq p^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}\times G
. By comparing the parallel transport maps, one obtains the claim about the pullback of
E
to
p^{*}\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}\times G
.
∎

3.
Examples and applications (groupoids)

We discuss the partially split condition for several classes of groupoids with bundles of ideals.

3.1.
Lie groups

Let
G
be a connected Lie group with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. We claim that an ideal
\mathfrak{k}\subset\mathfrak{g}
is partially split for
G
if and only if we have a decomposition
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{k}\oplus\mathfrak{h}
, for some ideal
\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{g}
. To see this, using left translations, we identify
TG
with the semi-direct:

TG\simeq G\ltimes\mathfrak{g},

where
G
acts on
\mathfrak{g}
via the adjoint action. Under this isomorphism,
K\subset TG
is identified with the subgroup

K\simeq G\ltimes\mathfrak{k}.

Moreover, one checks easily that a distribution
E\subset TG
is a subgroup if and only if it takes the form:

E\simeq G\ltimes\mathfrak{h},

where
\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{g}
is an ideal. Hence, the claim follows.

When
\mathfrak{k}
integrates to a closed subgroup of
G
, we have a surjective Lie group morphism
\Phi:G\to H
inducing
\mathfrak{k}
. The distributions
E\subset TG
, induced by ideals
\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{g}
as above, are then multiplicative Ehresmann connections for
\Phi
, which are complete by Theorem
2.15
.

3.2.
Products

Let
\mathcal{H}\rightrightarrows M
be a Lie groupoid with Lie algebroid
B\Rightarrow M
and
G
be a Lie group with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
. The product

\mathcal{G}:=\mathcal{H}\times G\rightrightarrows M

comes with the bundle of ideals
\mathfrak{k}:=0_{M}\times\mathfrak{g}\subset B\times\mathfrak{g}
, which corresponds to the Lie groupoid morphism
\operatorname{pr}_{1}:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
. This is partially split with complete, multiplicative Ehresmann connection
E=T\mathcal{H}\times G\subset T(\mathcal{H}\times G)
.

3.3.
Bundle of groups

As we already pointed out before, a bundle of groups
p:\mathcal{G}\to M
can be thought of as a groupoid submersion onto the identity groupoid
M\rightrightarrows M
. The corresponding bundle of ideals is its Lie algebroid
\mathfrak{k}=A
. By Corollary
2.12
, if
\mathfrak{k}
is partially split for
\mathcal{G}
, then
\mathfrak{k}
must be locally trivial. The converse, in general, may fail, but it holds if
\mathcal{G}
is assumed to be a bundle of simply connected Lie groups. This follows from Corollary
2.19
, since in this case
\mathcal{G}
is locally trivial (it follows also from the global to infinitesimal correspondence given in Theorem
5.6
).

A simple example of a bundle of simply connected groups that is not locally trivial is
\mathcal{G}:=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
, with multiplication given at
x\in\mathbb{R}
by:

(u_{1},v_{1})\cdot(u_{2},v_{2})=(u_{1}+u_{2},v_{1}+e^{xu_{1}}v_{2}).

Therefore, this does not admit multiplicative Ehresmann connections.

We note that, in the case of a vector bundle
\mathcal{G}=V\to M
, viewed as a bundle of Lie groups, a multiplicative Ehresmann connection is the same as a linear connection on
V
.

3.4.
Transitive groupoids

For a transitive Lie groupoid
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows M
the bundle of isotropy Lie algebras
\mathfrak{k}:=\ker\rho
is a bundle of ideals, with

K=\ker\operatorname{d}\mathbf{s}\cap\ker\operatorname{d}\mathbf{t}\subset T\mathcal{G}.

This is the bundle of ideals determined by the groupoid submersion

\Phi:=(\mathbf{t},\mathbf{s}):\mathcal{G}\to M\times M

and it is always partially split. Indeed, recall that
\mathcal{G}
can be identified with a gauge groupoid

\mathcal{G}(P):=P\times_{G}P\rightrightarrows M,

where, for a fixed
x\in M
,
G
denotes the isotropy group
\mathcal{G}_{x}
, and
P:=\mathbf{s}^{-1}(x)
is a principal
G
-bundle with projection
\mathbf{t}:P\to M
. Under this identification, the bundle
\mathfrak{k}
coincides with the adjoint bundle:

\mathfrak{k}=P[\mathfrak{g}]:=P\times_{G}\mathfrak{g}.

If we choose a principal bundle connection
\eta\in\Omega^{1}(P;\mathfrak{g})
, the 1-form
\operatorname{pr}_{2}^{*}\eta-\operatorname{pr}_{1}^{*}\eta\in\Omega^{1}(P\times P;\mathfrak{g})
descends to a 1-form
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{G}(P);P[\mathfrak{g}])
:

q^{*}\alpha=\operatorname{pr}_{2}^{*}\eta-\operatorname{pr}_{1}^{*}\eta,

where
q:P\times P\to\mathcal{G}(P)
is the projection. This gives a multiplicative connection 1-form
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathcal{G}(P);P[\mathfrak{g}])
, and so
\mathfrak{k}=P[\mathfrak{g}]
is partially split.

In fact, this assignment yields a 1-to-1
correspondence:

\left\{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-7.26447pt\vbox{\halign{\relax\hfil\txtline@@{#}\hfil\cr\hbox{{\ignorespaces principal bundle}\\\hbox{{\ignorespaces connections $\eta\in\Omega^{1}(P;\mathfrak{g})$\,}\crcr}}}}\right\}\tilde{\longleftrightarrow}\left\{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-7.26447pt\vbox{\halign{\relax\hfil\txtline@@{#}\hfil\cr\hbox{{\ignorespaces multiplicative connections}\\\hbox{{\ignorespaces{ $\alpha\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathcal{G}(P);P[\mathfrak{g}])$ } \,}\crcr}}}}\right\}}}}}

Since principal bundle connections are always complete, we see that any multiplicative Ehresmann connection for
\Phi:=(\mathbf{t},\mathbf{s}):\mathcal{G}\to M\times M
is complete. This agrees, of course, with Theorem
2.15
since the kernel of
\Phi
is the locally trivial bundle of groups
P[G]=P\times_{G}G\to M
(
G
acts by conjugation).

3.5.
Principal type

Let
\mathcal{G}(P)=P\times_{G}P\rightrightarrows M
be the gauge groupoid of a principal
G
-bundle
P\to M
, and
\mathcal{H}\rightrightarrows M
be any Lie groupoid. Consider the fiber product:

\mathcal{G}:=\{(g,h)\in\mathcal{G}(P)\times\mathcal{H}\,:\,\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{G}(P)}(g)=\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{H}}(h),\,\mathbf{t}_{\mathcal{G}(P)}(g)=\mathbf{t}_{\mathcal{H}}(h)\}\rightrightarrows M,

with groupoid structure such that the inclusion
\mathcal{G}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{G}(P)\times\mathcal{H}
is a groupoid map. Smoothness of
\mathcal{G}
follows because the anchor map
(\mathbf{t}_{\mathcal{G}(P)},\mathbf{s}_{\mathcal{G}(P)}):\mathcal{G}(P)\to M\times M
is a surjective submersion. This also implies that the groupoid morphism:

\Phi=\operatorname{pr}_{2}:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}

is a surjective submersion. This Lie groupoid map admits a multiplicative Ehresmann connection, constructed as follows. Let
A(P)=TP/G
be the Lie algebroid of
\mathcal{G}(P)
, and
B
that of
\mathcal{H}
. Identifying the Lie algebroid of
\mathcal{G}
with
A=A(P)\times_{TM}B
, we see that the bundle of ideals
\mathfrak{k}
corresponding to
K=\ker\operatorname{d}\Phi
is identified with the isotropy bundle of
A(P)
which, as we saw in the previous example, is
P[\mathfrak{g}]
. Also as in the previous example, any principal connection
\eta\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{M}}(P;\mathfrak{g})
gives rise to a multiplicative connection 1-form
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{G}(P),P[\mathfrak{g}])
. Using the projection
\operatorname{pr}_{1}:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}(P)
, which is a groupoid map, we obtain a multiplicative 1-form on
\mathcal{G}
:

\operatorname{pr}_{1}^{*}\alpha\in\Omega_{\mathrm{M}}^{1}(\mathcal{G},\mathfrak{k}).

We conclude that
\mathfrak{k}
is partially split.

The groupoids endowed with a bundle of ideals obtained via this construction will be called of
principal type
. Some of the previous examples fit into this setting:

\bullet

When
P
is the trivial principal bundle,
P=G\times M
, one obtains the product
\mathcal{G}=G\times\mathcal{H}
from Subsection
3.2
.

\bullet

For a pair groupoid
\mathcal{H}=M\times M
, we obtain back the transitive Lie groupoids
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{G}(P)
with
K=\ker\operatorname{d}\mathbf{s}\cap\ker\operatorname{d}\mathbf{t}
from Subsection
3.4
.

\bullet

When
\mathcal{H}
is a bundle of groups, we obtain the bundle of groups which is the fiberwise product
\mathcal{G}=P[G]\times_{M}\mathcal{H}
, where
P[G]=P\times_{G}G
.

The kernel of the groupoid morphism
\Phi=\operatorname{pr}_{2}:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
is
P[G]=P\times_{G}G\to M
, which is locally trivial. Hence, by Theorem
2.15
, the multiplicative Ehresmann connections for this class of examples are complete.

3.6.
Action groupoids

Consider an action groupoid

\mathcal{G}:=G\ltimes M\rightrightarrows M

associated with an action of a Lie group
G
on a manifold
M
. The Lie algebroid is the action algebroid:

A:=\mathfrak{g}\ltimes M\implies M

associated with the infinitesimal action
\rho:\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{X}(M)
. We have
(v,x)\in\ker\rho_{x}
if and only if
v
lies in the isotropy Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{x}
of the infinitesimal action. Moreover, the action of an arrow
(g,x)\in G\ltimes M
on an element
(v,x)\in\ker\rho_{x}
is given by:

(g,x)\cdot(v,x)=(\operatorname{Ad}_{g}v,gx).

It follows that a subbundle
\mathfrak{k}\subset\mathfrak{g}\ltimes M
is a bundle of ideals if and only if it satisfies:

(i)

\mathfrak{k}_{x}\subset\ker\rho_{x}
;

(ii)

\operatorname{Ad}_{g}(\mathfrak{k}_{x})=\mathfrak{k}_{gx}
.

The following is immediate:

Proposition 3.1
.

If the inclusion of a bundle of ideals
\mathfrak{k}\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{g}\times M
admits a
G
-equivariant splitting
l:\mathfrak{g}\times M\to\mathfrak{k}
, then
\mathfrak{k}
is partially split with multiplicative connection 1-form
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{M}}(G\ltimes M;\mathfrak{k})
given by:

\alpha_{(g,x)}(\operatorname{d}L_{g}(v),w):=l(v,x).

In particular, if the action of
G
on
M
is proper, or if
\mathfrak{g}
admits a
G
-invariant inner product, then
\mathfrak{k}
is partially split.

3.7.
Non-integrable quotient

Let
G
be a compact semi-simple Lie group, and restrict the adjoint action of
G
to the unit sphere
M\subset\mathfrak{g}
, with respect to an invariant inner product. The action groupoid
\mathcal{G}:=G\ltimes M\rightrightarrows M
has the bundle of ideals

\mathfrak{k}\subset A:=\mathfrak{g}\ltimes M,\quad\mathfrak{k}|_{x}:=\mathbb{R}x,\ x\in M.

By Proposition
3.1
,
\mathfrak{k}
is partially split. However, for
\mathfrak{g}\not\simeq\mathfrak{so}(3,\mathbb{R})
, the Lie algebroid
B:=A/\mathfrak{k}
is not integrable. To see this, it is enough to consider the case when
G
is simply-connected. If
\mathcal{H}\rightrightarrows M
is a Lie groupoid integrating
B
, then we have an induced groupoid morphism
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
. So for each
x\in M
, we have an induced Lie group map between the isotropy groups
\Phi_{x}:G_{x}\to\mathcal{H}_{x}
. The kernel of
\Phi_{x}
is a 1-dimensional group whose connected component is
\exp(\mathbb{R}x)\subset H_{x}
. However, for
\mathfrak{g}\not\simeq\mathfrak{so}(3,\mathbb{R})
, there are
x\in M
for which
\exp(\mathbb{R}x)
is not a closed submanifold of
G
. This is a contradiction.

3.8.
Groupoids with bi-invariant metrics

For a general groupoid
\mathcal{G}
the notion of bi-invariant metric does not make sense: for this we need to lift the right and left actions of
\mathcal{G}
on itself to its tangent bundle. So assume that
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows M
is equipped with a Cartan connection, i.e., a multiplicative distribution complementary to the source fibers:

T\mathcal{G}=\ker\operatorname{d}\mathbf{s}\oplus H.

Then one can lift the left and right actions of
\mathcal{G}
on itself to
T\mathcal{G}
by setting:

\displaystyle\lambda^{L}_{g}:T_{h}\mathcal{G}\to T_{gh}\mathcal{G},

\displaystyle\quad v\mapsto gv:=\operatorname{d}_{(g,h)}m(w,v)

\displaystyle\lambda^{R}_{h}:T_{g}\mathcal{G}\to T_{gh}\mathcal{G},

\displaystyle\quad v^{\prime}\mapsto v^{\prime}h:=\operatorname{d}_{(g,h)}m(v^{\prime},w^{\prime}).

where
w\in H_{g}
and
w^{\prime}\in H_{h}
are the unique tangent vectors such that
\operatorname{d}\mathbf{s}(w)=\operatorname{d}\mathbf{t}(v)
and
\operatorname{d}\mathbf{s}(v^{\prime})=\operatorname{d}\mathbf{t}(w^{\prime})
.
These actions extend the left and right actions of
\mathcal{G}
on
\ker\operatorname{d}\mathbf{t}
and
\ker\operatorname{d}\mathbf{s}
and they are related by the groupoid inversion.

Now, given a groupoid
\mathcal{G}
with a multiplicative connection
H\subset T\mathcal{G}
, one says that a Riemannian metric
\eta
on
\mathcal{G}
is
bi-invariant
if it is invariant under left translations, right translations and inversion (see
[
24
]
).
If
\mathcal{G}
admits a bi-invariant metric, then any bundle of ideals
\mathfrak{k}
is partially split. In fact, for a bi-invariant metric
\eta
the distribution orthogonal to
K
:

E:=K^{\perp}\subset T\mathcal{G},

is a VB subgroupoid of
T\mathcal{G}
, so satisfies Proposition
2.8
(i). We leave the details to the reader.

Remark 3.2
.

An action groupoid
G\ltimes M\rightrightarrows M
has an obvious multiplicative connection. If the action of
G
on
M
is proper, using averaging, one obtains a bi-invariant metric on
G\ltimes M
. This gives an alternative argument that if the action of
G
on
M
is proper then
\mathfrak{k}
is partially split, a fact also deduced in Proposition
3.1
. We will see in Section
6.4
the infinitesimal analog of this result.

3.9.
Over-symplectic groupoids

A closed multiplicative 2-form
\omega\in\Omega^{2}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathcal{G})
is called an
over-symplectic structure
on the Lie groupoid
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows M

[
8
]
if it satisfies:

\ker\omega\subset\ker\operatorname{d}\mathbf{t}\cap\ker\operatorname{d}\mathbf{s}.

It follows from this condition that rank of
\omega
is constant, equal to
2\dim M
(see
[
8
, Proposition 4.5]
). Then
\mathfrak{k}:=\ker\omega|_{M}
defines a bundle of ideals whose associate subgroupoid (
2.2
) is
K=\ker\omega
. In this case, the existence of partial splittings plays an important role in the study of local models around Poisson submanifolds. For example, it implies that
(\mathcal{G},\omega)
embeds coisotropically in some symplectic groupoid. We refer to
[
19
]
where this class of groupoids is discussed in detail.

4.
Morita invariance and properness

In this section we prove the following fundamental result.

Theorem 4.1

(Morita invariance)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{1}\rightrightarrows N_{1}
and
\mathcal{H}_{2}\rightrightarrows N_{2}
be two Lie groupoids. A Morita equivalence
\mathcal{H}_{1}\cong\mathcal{H}_{2}
induces a one-to-one correspondence between bundles of ideals in
\mathcal{H}_{1}
and bundles of ideals in
\mathcal{H}_{2}
, under which partially split bundles of ideals are sent to partially split bundles of ideals.

We start by deducing from this result the following important fact, which implies the theorem from the introduction.

Theorem 4.2
.

A bundle of ideals in a proper Lie groupoid is partially split.

Proof.

Let
x\in M
and denote by
\mathcal{O}
the orbit of
\mathcal{G}
through
x
. By the slice theorem for proper Lie groupoids
[
15
,
35
,
36
]
, there is a transversal
T
to
\mathcal{O}
such that the restriction

\mathcal{G}|_{T}\rightrightarrows T
is isomorphic to an action Lie groupoid
G_{x}\ltimes V\rightrightarrows V
, for some open set
0\in V\subset\nu_{x}(\mathcal{O})
. Since
\mathcal{G}
is proper, the isotropy group
G_{x}
is compact so this is a proper groupoid. On the other hand,
\mathcal{G}|_{T}\rightrightarrows T
is Morita equivalent to
\mathcal{G}|_{U}\rightrightarrows U
, where
U
is the saturation of
T
(see, e.g.,
[
15
]
). Therefore, it follows from Proposition
3.1
and Theorem
4.1
that
\mathfrak{k}|_{U}
is a partially split bundle of ideals of
\mathcal{G}|_{U}

Hence, we can cover
M
by saturated open sets
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
such that, for each
i\in I
, there exists a multiplicative connection 1-form
\alpha_{U_{i}}\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathcal{G}|_{U_{i}},\mathfrak{k})
. Since
\mathcal{G}
is proper, there exists a
\mathcal{G}
-invariant partition of unity
\{\rho_{i}\}_{i\in I}
subordinate to the cover
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
(see, e.g.,
[
12
, Proposition 8]
). Then the
\mathfrak{k}
-valued 1-form:

\alpha:=\sum_{i\in I}(\rho_{i}\circ\mathbf{s})\alpha_{U_{i}}\in\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{G};\mathfrak{k}).

is multiplicative and satisfies:

\alpha|_{\mathfrak{k}}=\mathrm{Id}.

Hence,
\alpha
is a multiplicative connection 1-form, so
\mathfrak{k}
is partially split.
∎

The theorem also has the following consequence:

Corollary 4.3
.

Let
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
be a groupoid morphism with abelian kernel and assume that
\mathcal{G}
is target-connected and proper. Then
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
admits a Lie groupoid splitting if and only if it admits a leafwise flat, multiplicative Ehresmann connection with trivial holonomy.

Proof.

Corollary
2.37
gives one direction. To prove the other direction, we can assume that
\Phi=\operatorname{pr}_{1}:\mathcal{H}\times_{M}A\to\mathcal{H}
where
p:A\to M
is an abelian bundle of Lie groups. Since
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{H}\times_{M}A
is assumed proper, Theorem
4.2
gives a multiplicative Ehresmann connection for
\operatorname{pr}_{1}:\mathcal{H}\times_{M}A\to\mathcal{H}
, and then Corollary
2.40
shows that
p:A\to M
admits an
\mathcal{H}
-invariant multiplicative Ehresmann connection
E^{A}
. Then
E=(\operatorname{d}\mathbf{s})^{*}E^{A}
is a leafwise flat, multiplicative Ehresmann connection for
\operatorname{pr}_{1}:\mathcal{H}\times_{M}A\to\mathcal{H}
with trivial holonomy (see Proposition
2.39
).
∎

4.1.
Morita invariance

We now turn to the proof of Morita invariance.
For the general theory of Morita equivalences see
[
16
]
and
[
29
]
. We take the point of view that a Morita equivalence is given by a Lie groupoid
\mathcal{G}
and Morita maps
\Phi_{1}:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}_{1}
and
\Phi_{2}:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}_{2}
which are surjective submersions. This is equivalent to the bibundle definition via the following constructions. The base of
\mathcal{G}
is a principal
(\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2})
-bibundle, and to a principal bibundle
\mathcal{H}_{1}\curvearrowright M\curvearrowleft\mathcal{H}_{2}
one associates the groupoid

(\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows M):=(M\times_{N_{1}}\mathcal{H}_{1}\times_{N_{1}}M\rightrightarrows M)\simeq(M\times_{N_{2}}\mathcal{H}_{2}\times_{N_{2}}M\rightrightarrows M).

We will call a Morita map that is a surjective submersion a
Morita fibration
. Such maps can be characterized as follows:

Lemma 4.4
.

A groupoid map
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
is a Morita fibration if and only if it covers a surjective submersion
\phi:M\to N
and
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows M
is isomorphic to the pullback of
\mathcal{H}\rightrightarrows N
along
\phi
:

\phi^{*}(\mathcal{H})=M\times_{N}\mathcal{H}\times_{N}M\rightrightarrows M

via the map:

\overline{\Phi}:\mathcal{G}\to\phi^{*}(\mathcal{H}),\quad\overline{\Phi}(g)=(\mathbf{t}(g),\Phi(g),\mathbf{s}(g)).

Given a Morita fibration
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
, covering a submersion
\phi:M\to N
, it follows from the properties above that its kernel:

\mathcal{K}=\{g\in\mathcal{G}:\Phi(g)=1_{x},x\in N\},

is a submersion groupoid: an element
g\in\mathcal{K}
is uniquely determined by its source and target, and the restriction of
\overline{\Phi}
gives an isomorphism of Lie groupoids:

\mathcal{K}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-1.59442pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\smash{$\sim$}}}M\times_{N}M,\quad g\mapsto(\mathbf{t}(g),\mathbf{s}(g)).

On submersion groupoids, multiplicative forms are multiplicatively exact:

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
\phi:M\to N
be a surjective submersion, and
E\to N
be a vector bundle. Any multiplicative form on the submersion groupoid

\alpha\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{M}}(M\times_{N}M;\phi^{*}(E))

is multiplicativly exact, i.e., there exists
\theta\in\Omega^{\bullet}(M;\phi^{*}(E))
such that
\alpha=\delta\theta
, where

\delta:\Omega^{\bullet}(M;\phi^{*}(E))\to\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{M}}(M\times_{N}M;\phi^{*}(E))

is the simplicial differential of
M\times_{N}M
with coefficients in
\phi^{*}(E)
(see (
A.4
)).

Proof.

Let
U\subset N
be an open set on which there exists a local section
\sigma:U\to M
of
\phi
. Define
\theta\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\phi^{-1}(U);\phi^{*}(E))
by
\theta(v):=\alpha(v,\operatorname{d}(\sigma\circ\phi)(v))
, for
v\in\wedge^{\bullet}T\phi^{-1}(U)
. Multiplicativity of
\alpha
implies that
\alpha|_{\phi^{-1}(U)}=\delta\theta
:

\displaystyle\alpha(v_{1},v_{2})

\displaystyle=m^{*}(\alpha)\big((v_{1},\operatorname{d}\sigma(u)),(\operatorname{d}\sigma(u),v_{2})\big)=\alpha(v_{1},\operatorname{d}\sigma(u))+\alpha(\operatorname{d}\sigma(u),v_{2})

\displaystyle=\theta(v_{1})-\theta(v_{2})=\delta\theta(v_{1},v_{2}),

for all
v_{1},v_{2}\in\wedge^{\bullet}T\phi^{-1}(U)
with
\operatorname{d}\phi(v_{1})=u=\operatorname{d}\phi(v_{2})
.

In general, consider an open cover
\{U_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of
N
for which local sections of
\phi
exist. Then the corresponding local primitives
\theta_{i}\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\phi^{-1}(U_{i});\phi^{*}(E))
of
\alpha
can be glued, using a partition of unity
\{\chi_{i}\}_{i\in I}
subordinated to the cover, to a global primitive:

\theta:=\sum_{i\in I}\chi_{i}\circ\phi\cdot\theta_{i}
.
∎

The following two propositions prove Theorem
4.1
.

Proposition 4.6
.

A Morita fibration
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
gives a 1-to-1 correspondence between bundles of ideals in
\mathcal{G}
and bundles of ideals in
\mathcal{H}
.

Proof.

For each
x\in M
,
\Phi
restricts to an isomorphism of Lie groups:
\mathcal{G}_{x}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-1.59442pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\smash{$\sim$}}}\mathcal{H}_{\phi(x)}
. So we can define a map of bundles of ideals:

A(\mathcal{H})\supset\mathfrak{k}\longmapsto\Phi^{*}(\mathfrak{k}),\quad\text{where}\quad\Phi^{*}(\mathfrak{k})_{x}=\{v\in\ker\rho_{x}:\operatorname{d}_{x}\Phi(v)\in\mathfrak{k}|_{\phi(x)}\}\subset A(\mathcal{G}),

For the inverse map, given a bundle of ideals
\mathfrak{k}
in
\mathcal{G}
, one needs to show that if
y=\phi(x)=\phi(x^{\prime})
then
\operatorname{d}\Phi(\mathfrak{k}_{x})=\operatorname{d}\Phi(\mathfrak{k}_{x^{\prime}})
. As we observed above, there is a unique
k\in\mathcal{K}
such that
\mathbf{s}(k)=x
and
\mathbf{t}(k)=x^{\prime}
, and we have:

\mathfrak{k}_{x^{\prime}}=k\cdot\mathfrak{k}_{x}.

Since
k
belongs to the kernel, we have
\Phi(kgk^{-1})=\Phi(g)
, and so we conclude that:

\operatorname{d}\Phi(\mathfrak{k}_{x^{\prime}})=\operatorname{d}\Phi(k\cdot\mathfrak{k}_{x})=\operatorname{d}\Phi(\mathfrak{k}_{x}).

Therefore we get a map of bundles of ideals:

A(\mathcal{G})\supset\mathfrak{k}\longmapsto\Phi_{*}(\mathfrak{k}),\quad\text{where}\quad\Phi_{*}(\mathfrak{k})_{\phi(x)}=\operatorname{d}\Phi(\mathfrak{k}_{x})\subset A(\mathcal{H}),

which is the inverse of the map above.
∎

Now if
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathcal{H},\mathfrak{k})
is a multiplicative connection 1-form for
\mathfrak{k}
, then the pullback form:

\Phi^{*}\alpha\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathcal{G},\Phi^{*}\mathfrak{k}),

is easily seen to be a multiplicative connection 1-form for
\Phi^{*}\mathfrak{k}
. This establishes one half of the following proposition:

Proposition 4.7
.

Given a Morita fibration
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
, a bundle of ideals
\mathfrak{k}
in
\mathcal{H}
is partially split if and only if the bundle of ideals
\Phi^{*}\mathfrak{k}
in
\mathcal{G}
is partially split.

Proof.

We already now that if
\mathfrak{k}
is a partially split ideal in
\mathcal{H}
then
\Phi^{*}\mathfrak{k}
is a partially split ideal in
\mathcal{G}
.

For the converse, let
\mathfrak{k}
be a bundle of ideals in
\mathcal{H}
so that
\Phi^{*}\mathfrak{k}
is partially split. Fix a multiplicative connection 1-form
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{G},\phi^{*}\mathfrak{k})
. We will “correct”
\alpha
so that it is the pullback of some multiplicative 1-form in
\mathcal{H}
. As we mentioned already, the kernel
\mathcal{K}
of a Morita fibration
\Phi
is a submersion groupoid. So by Lemma
4.5
applied to the restriction
\alpha|_{\mathcal{K}}
, there is a
\mathfrak{k}
-valued 1-form
\omega\in\Omega^{1}(M,\phi^{*}\mathfrak{k})
such that:

\alpha|_{\mathcal{K}}=\delta\omega,

where
\delta:\Omega^{1}(M,\phi^{*}\mathfrak{k})\to\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathcal{K},\phi^{*}\mathfrak{k})
is the simplicial differential of
\mathcal{K}
with coefficients in
\phi^{*}\mathfrak{k}
(see (
A.4
)). Now consider the 1-form:

\widetilde{\alpha}:=\alpha-\delta\omega\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathcal{G},\phi^{*}\mathfrak{k}),

where
\delta:\Omega^{1}(M,\phi^{*}\mathfrak{k})\to\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathcal{G},\phi^{*}\mathfrak{k})
is the simplicial differential of
\mathcal{G}
with coefficients in
\phi^{*}\mathfrak{k}
. Then the pullback of
\widetilde{\alpha}
to
\mathcal{K}
vanishes. We claim that there exists a unique multiplicative form
\beta\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathcal{H},\mathfrak{k})
such that:

\widetilde{\alpha}=\Phi^{*}\beta.

For this, we use the identification
\mathcal{G}\simeq\phi^{*}(\mathcal{H})
from Lemma
4.4
. Then we have to show that
\widetilde{\alpha}
takes the same value on any two vectors of the form:

(X_{i},Y,Z_{i})\in T_{p_{i}}M\times_{T_{\mathbf{t}(g)}N}T_{g}\mathcal{H}\times_{T_{\mathbf{s}(g)}N}T_{q_{i}}M,\quad i=1,2.

Since
\operatorname{d}\phi(X_{1})=\operatorname{d}\mathbf{t}(Y)=\operatorname{d}\phi(X_{2})
and
\operatorname{d}\phi(Z_{1})=\operatorname{d}\mathbf{s}(Y)=\operatorname{d}\phi(Z_{2})
, we have that:

(X_{1},Y,Z_{1})=(X_{1},\operatorname{d}\phi(X_{1}),X_{2})*(X_{2},Y,Z_{2})*(Z_{2},\operatorname{d}\phi(Z_{1}),Z_{1}),

where
*=\operatorname{d}m
is the multiplication in
T\phi^{*}(\mathcal{H})
. Using that the first and the last element on the right-hand side are in
T\mathcal{K}
, and that
\widetilde{\alpha}
is multiplicative, we obtain the claimed equality:

\widetilde{\alpha}(X_{1},Y,Z_{1})=\widetilde{\alpha}(X_{2},Y,Z_{2})\in\mathfrak{k}_{\mathbf{s}(g)}.

Finally,
\widetilde{\alpha}|_{\Phi^{*}\mathfrak{k}}=\alpha|_{\Phi^{*}\mathfrak{k}}=\mathrm{Id}
implies that
\beta|_{\mathfrak{k}}=\mathrm{Id}
. Hence,
\mathfrak{k}
is partially split.
∎

4.2.
Bundle gerbes and connections

As an application of the results discussed so far, we discuss now how one can recover the curving and 3-curvature for bundle gerbes out of a multiplicative Ehresmann connection. Our aim is not to discuss the general theory of gerbes and multiplicative connections, for which we refer the reader to
[
25
]
, but rather to give a simple illustration of our theory. For that reason we consider only
S^{1}
-gerbes over manifolds (see,
[
22
,
25
,
30
]
). We will see how one can recover from our results the classical theorem of Murray
[
30
]
that the class of the curvature 3-form is the image in
H^{3}(N,\mathbb{R})
of the Dixmier-Douady class in
H^{3}(N,\mathbb{Z})
(see Theorem
4.12
).

Let
N
be a smooth manifold. By an
S^{1}
-central extension
over
N
we mean a surjective submersion
\phi:M\to N
together with a groupoid morphism onto the corresponding submersion groupoid
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to M\times_{\phi}M
, with kernel the trivial
S^{1}
-bundle
\mathcal{K}=S^{1}_{M}=M\times S^{1}
:

\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S^{1}_{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi}
\textstyle{M\times_{\phi}M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1,}

such that for all
g\in\mathcal{G}
and
\theta\in S^{1}
:

g\cdot(\mathbf{s}(g),\theta)=(\mathbf{t}(g),\theta)\cdot g.

A
Morita equivalence
of
S^{1}
-central extensions
\Phi_{1}:\mathcal{G}_{1}\to M_{1}\times_{\phi_{1}}M_{1}
and
\Phi_{2}:\mathcal{G}_{2}\to M_{2}\times_{\phi_{2}}M_{2}
is given by a principal
(\mathcal{G}_{1},\mathcal{G}_{2})
-bibundle

\textstyle{\mathcal{G}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{G}_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M_{1}}
\textstyle{M_{2}}

such that orbits of the actions of the kernels
S^{1}_{M_{1}}
and
S^{1}_{M_{2}}
on
P
coincide. The orbit space of these actions is then a manifold
Q
, which can be canonically identifies with the fiber product
Q=M_{1}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.75833pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.75833pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.16959pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.33542pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\phi_{1}}}}}\times^{{\kern-25.40475pt\mathchoice{\makebox[7.75833pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.75833pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.16959pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.33542pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.071pt\mathchoice{\makebox[7.75833pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.75833pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.16959pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.33542pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-13.3517pt{\phi_{1}}\kern 8.071pt\mathchoice{\makebox[7.75833pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.75833pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.16959pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.33542pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-13.3517pt{\phi_{1}}\kern . Moreover, it is a principal bi-bundle for the submersion groupoids:

\textstyle{M_{1}\times_{\phi_{1}}M_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M_{1}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.75833pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.75833pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.16959pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.33542pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\phi_{1}}}}}\times^{{\kern-25.40475pt\mathchoice{\makebox[7.75833pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.75833pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.16959pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.33542pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern \textstyle{M_{1}}
\textstyle{M_{2}}

and one obtains a map of principal bi-bundles:

\textstyle{M_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M_{1}}
\textstyle{M_{1}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.75833pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.75833pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.16959pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.33542pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\phi_{1}}}}}\times^{{\kern-25.40475pt\mathchoice{\makebox[7.75833pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.75833pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.16959pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.33542pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 5.6321pt\mathchoice{\makebox[7.75833pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.75833pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.16959pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.33542pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-10.07863pt{\phi_{1}}\kern 5.6321pt{\phi_{2}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.75833pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.75833pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.16959pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.33542pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\phi_{1}}}}}\times^{{\kern-17.52765pt\mathchoice{\makebox[7.75833pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.75833pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.16959pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.33542pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.54321pt\mathchoice{\makebox[7.75833pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.75833pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.16959pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.33542pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-8.98975pt{\phi_{1}}\kern 4.54321pt{\phi_{2}}}}}M_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M_{2}}

Definition 4.8
.

An
S^{1}
-gerbe over a manifold

N
is a Morita equivalence class of
S^{1}
-central extensions over
N
.

We recall that an
S^{1}
-gerbe is completely characterized by its
Dixmier-Douady class
which can be defined as follows. Given an
S^{1}
-central extension defined by a submersion
\phi:M\to N
and a groupoid morphism
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to M\times_{\phi}M
, one chooses a good cover
\{U_{i}\}
of
N
for which there exist sections
s_{i}:U_{i}\to M
of
\phi:M\to N
. Then one can find maps
g_{ij}:U_{ij}\to\mathcal{G}
such that

\Phi(g_{ij}(x))=(s_{i}(x),s_{j}(x)).

Since, for each
x\in U_{ijk}
, the composition
g_{ij}(x)\cdot g_{jk}(x)\cdot g_{jk}(x)
is an element of the kernel
S^{1}_{M}
, we have that:

g_{ij}(x)\cdot g_{jk}(x)\cdot g_{ki}(x)=(s_{i}(x),c_{ijk}(x)),

for a 3-cocycle
c_{ijk}:U_{ijk}\to S^{1}
. The class in sheaf cohomology:

c_{2}(\mathcal{G}):=[c_{ijk}]\in H^{2}(N,\underline{S^{1}}),

only depends on the Morita equivalence class of the extension and is called the
Dixmier-Douady class of the
S^{1}
-gerbe
. Using the exponential sequence:

\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{Z}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\exp}
\textstyle{S^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1,}

one can view the Dixmier-Douady class as a class in integer cohomology:

c_{2}(\mathcal{G})\in H^{3}(N,\mathbb{Z}).

In this way, one can think that
S^{1}
-central extensions give geometric representatives of integer cohomology classes in degree 3, the same way as principal
S^{1}
-bundles give geometric representatives of integer cohomology classes in degree 2, via their Chern class. The same way one can use the curvature of a principal connection to obtain representatives in real cohomology of the Chern class, we will see now that one can use multiplicative Ehresmann connections to obtain representatives in real cohomology of the Dixmier-Douady class.

We start by the following proposition:

Proposition 4.9
.

Every
S^{1}
-central extension

\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S^{1}_{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Phi}
\textstyle{M\times_{\phi}M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{1,}

admits a multiplicative Ehresmann connection
E
. The induced multiplicative Ehresmann connection
E^{\mathcal{K}}
on the kernel
\mathcal{K}=S^{1}_{M}
is the canonical flat connection.

Proof.

Notice that
\mathcal{G}
is necessarily a proper groupoid. Hence, it follows from Theorem
4.2
that multiplicative Ehresmann connections exist.

By Proposition
2.10
, a multiplicative Ehresmann connection
E^{S^{1}}
on the trivial
S^{1}
-bundle
S^{1}_{M}
is related to a linear connection
E^{\nabla}
on the trivial line bundle
\mathbb{R}_{M}
by the exponential map:

(\operatorname{d}\exp)(E^{\nabla})=E^{S^{1}_{M}}.

It follows that constant sections of
\mathbb{R}_{M}
,
x\mapsto(x,n)
(
n\in\mathbb{Z}
), are flat. Then any constant section is flat, and so
E^{S^{1}_{M}}
must be the trivial connection.
∎

Hence, given a multiplicative Ehresmann connection
E
for an
S^{1}
-central extension
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to M\times_{\phi}M
, the associated linear connection on
\mathfrak{k}=\mathbb{R}_{M}
is the canonical flat connection:

\nabla_{X}=\mathscr{L}_{X}.

Then, by Proposition
2.29
, the curvature
\Omega\in\Omega^{2}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathcal{G})
of
E
satisfies

\Omega=\Phi^{*}\underline{\Omega},

for a unique closed, multiplicative, 2-form
\underline{\Omega}\in\Omega^{2}_{\mathrm{M}}(M\times_{\phi}M)
. By Lemma
4.5
, there exists a 2-form
F\in\Omega^{2}(M)
(not unique) such that:

\underline{\Omega}=\operatorname{pr}_{1}^{*}F-\operatorname{pr}_{2}^{*}F,

where
\operatorname{pr}_{i}:M\times_{\phi}M\to M
are the source and target of the submersion groupoid.
Equivalently:

\Omega=\mathbf{t}^{*}F-\mathbf{s}^{*}F.

(4.1)

Definition 4.10
.

A form
F\in\Omega^{2}(M)
satisfying (
4.1
) is called a
curving
of the connection
E
.

Finally, observe that:

0=\operatorname{d}\underline{\Omega}=\operatorname{pr}_{1}^{*}\operatorname{d}F-\operatorname{pr}_{2}^{*}\operatorname{d}F.

Therefore, there exists a unique 3-form
G\in\Omega^{3}(N)
such that:

\operatorname{d}F=\phi^{*}G.

(4.2)

Definition 4.11
.

The form
G\in\Omega^{2}(N)
satisfying (
4.2
) is called the
curvature 3-form
of the curving.

Note that two curvings
F
and
F^{\prime}
differ by a form
B=F-F^{\prime}
such that:

\operatorname{pr}_{1}^{*}B-\operatorname{pr}_{2}^{*}B=0.

Hence,
B=\phi^{*}\varphi
for a 2-form
\varphi\in\Omega^{2}(N)
. The corresponding curvature 3-forms are then related by:

G-G^{\prime}=\operatorname{d}\varphi.

In particular, the class
[G]\in H^{3}(N)
does not depend on the choice of curving and is intrinsically associated with the multiplicative Ehresmann connection
E
. We can now state and prove Murray’s result:

Theorem 4.12
.

Let
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to M\times_{\phi}M
be an
S^{1}
-central extension representing an
S^{1}
-gerbe over a manifold
N
. Given any multiplicative Ehresmann connection
E
for
\Phi
, the class
[G]
of a curvature 3-form is the image of the Dixmier-Douady class of the gerbe
c_{2}(\mathcal{G})
under the canonical map:

H^{3}(N,\mathbb{Z})\to H^{3}(N,\mathbb{R}).

Proof.

As above, we pick a good cover
\{U_{i}\}
of
N
for which there exist sections
s_{i}:U_{i}\to M
of
\phi:M\to N
, and maps
g_{ij}:U_{ij}\to\mathcal{G}
such that

\Phi(g_{ij}(x))=(s_{i}(x),s_{j}(x)).

The Dixmier-Douady class is then
c_{2}(\mathcal{G}):=[c_{ijk}]
where the 3-cocycle
c_{ijk}:U_{ijk}\to S^{1}
is given by:

(s_{i},c_{ijk})=g_{ij}\cdot g_{jk}\cdot g_{ki}.

This is an equality of maps
U_{ijk}\to\mathcal{G}
and
we are going to pull back the connection 1-form
\alpha\in\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{G})
under both sides.
For the left-side, it follows from the condition
\alpha(\xi^{R})=\xi
that

(s_{i},c_{ijk})^{*}\alpha=c_{ijk}^{-1}\operatorname{d}c_{ijk}=\operatorname{d}f_{ijk},

where we set
c_{ijk}=\exp(f_{ijk})
, for a map
f_{ijk}:U_{ijk}\to\mathbb{R}
, well-defined up to the addition of an integer. On the other hand, for the right-hand side, using that
\alpha
is multiplicative, we find:

\displaystyle(g_{ij}\cdot g_{jk}\cdot g_{ki})^{*}\alpha

\displaystyle=g_{ij}^{*}\alpha+g_{jk}^{*}\alpha+g_{ki}^{*}\alpha

\displaystyle=A_{ij}+A_{jk}+A_{ki},

where

A_{ij}:=g_{ij}^{*}\alpha\in\Omega^{1}(U_{ij}).

We conclude that:

A_{ij}+A_{jk}+A_{ki}=\operatorname{d}f_{ijk}.

(4.3)

Now, if
F\in\Omega^{2}(M)
is a curving, and we let:

F_{i}:=s_{i}^{*}F\in\Omega^{2}(U_{i}),

it follows from (
4.1
) that on
U_{ij}
we must have:

\displaystyle F_{i}-F_{j}=s_{i}^{*}F-s_{j}^{*}F

\displaystyle=(s_{i},s_{j})^{*}\underline{\Omega}=

\displaystyle=g_{ij}^{*}\Omega=g_{ij}^{*}\operatorname{d}\alpha=\operatorname{d}g_{ij}^{*}\alpha.

In other words,

F_{i}-F_{j}=\operatorname{d}A_{ij}.

(4.4)

On the other hand, the definition (
4.2
) of the curvature 3-form gives:

G|_{U_{i}}=\operatorname{d}F_{i}.

(4.5)

Equations (
4.3
), (
4.4
) and (
4.5
) show that
[G]\in H^{3}(N,\mathbb{R})
corresponds to the class
[\operatorname{d}f_{ijk}]\in H^{2}(N,\underline{\Omega^{1}_{\textrm{cl}}})
, so using that
c_{ijk}=\exp(f_{ijk})
the result follows.
∎

5.
IM Ehresmann connections

5.1.
IM connection for a surjective algebroid morphism

We now turn to the infinitesimal version of multiplicative Ehresmann connections. If one starts with a surjective Lie algebroid morphism
\phi:A\to B
covering the identity, its differential is a morphism of VB algebroids:

\textstyle{TA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{d}\phi}
\textstyle{TB\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TM\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Id}}
\textstyle{TM\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Id}}
\textstyle{M}

We recall that double arrows
\implies
represent Lie algebroids. The kernel of
\operatorname{d}\phi
is a VB subalgebroid of
TA
:

\textstyle{\ker(\operatorname{d}\phi)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0_{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M}

This leads naturally to the infinitesimal version of a multiplicative Ehresmann connection:

Definition 5.1
.

An
IM Ehresmann connection
for
\phi:A\to B
is a VB subalgebroid
E\Rightarrow TM
of
TA\Rightarrow TM
which is “horizontal”:

TA=\ker(\operatorname{d}\phi)\oplus E.

5.2.
Infinitesimal partially split bundles of ideals

A surjective Lie algebroid morphism
\phi:A\to B
covering the identity is completely determined by its kernel:

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathfrak{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 64.7778pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 64.7778pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 85.02034pt\raise 6.1111pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\phi}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 102.2778pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 102.2778pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 140.3646pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 140.3646pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

So the notion of IM Ehresmann connection for
\phi
can be rephrased in terms of the bundle of ideals
\mathfrak{k}
, without any mentioning of
\phi
and
B
. To do this we need to express
\ker(\operatorname{d}\phi)
in terms of
\mathfrak{k}
, which can be done as follows.

Let
A\Rightarrow M
be a Lie algebroid and
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
a
bundle of ideals
, i.e.,
\mathfrak{k}\to M
is a vector subbundle included in
\ker\rho_{A}
and satisfying

\alpha\in\Gamma(A),\ \gamma\in\Gamma(\mathfrak{k})\quad\Longrightarrow\quad[\alpha,\gamma]_{A}\in\Gamma(\mathfrak{k}).

Then we have the canonical representation of
A
on
\mathfrak{k}
:

\nabla^{\mathfrak{k}}_{\alpha}\gamma:=[\alpha,\gamma]_{A},\quad\alpha\in\Gamma(A),\gamma\in\Gamma(\mathfrak{k}).

(5.1)

This is the infinitesimal version of the groupoid representation (
2.1
) and it gives rise to the infinitesimal version of the groupoid
\mathcal{G}\times_{M}\mathfrak{k}\rightrightarrows M
. Namely, we have the semi-direct product Lie algebroid
A\times_{M}\mathfrak{k}\Rightarrow M
, with Lie bracket

[(\alpha_{1},\gamma_{1}),(\alpha_{2},\gamma_{2})]:=([\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}]_{A},\nabla^{\mathfrak{k}}_{\alpha_{1}}\gamma_{2}-\nabla^{\mathfrak{k}}_{\alpha_{2}}\gamma_{1}),

and anchor
\rho_{A}\circ\operatorname{pr}_{A}
. This is a VB algebroid:

\textstyle{A\times_{M}\mathfrak{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M}

and, in fact, we have:

Lemma 5.2
.

Given a bundle of ideals
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
the image of the inclusion

A\times_{M}\mathfrak{k}\hookrightarrow TA,\quad(a,\gamma)\mapsto\frac{\operatorname{d}}{\operatorname{d}t}\Big|_{t=0}(a+t\gamma),

(5.2)

coincides with
\ker(\operatorname{d}\phi)
, where
\phi:A\to B:=A/\mathfrak{k}
is the quotient map.

Proof.

Since
\phi:A\to B
is surjective and covers the identity,
\operatorname{d}_{a}\phi:T_{a}A\to T_{\phi(a)}B
is surjective, for any
a\in A
. Also, if
(a,\gamma)\in A\times_{M}\mathfrak{k}
then
\phi(a+t\gamma)=\phi(a)
so under the natural inclusion (
5.2
)
A\times_{M}\mathfrak{k}
is mapped to the kernel of
\operatorname{d}\phi
. Since

\operatorname{rank}_{A}TA-\operatorname{rank}_{B}TB=\operatorname{rank}_{M}\mathfrak{k}=\operatorname{rank}_{A}(A\times_{M}\mathfrak{k}),

the result follows.
∎

Hence, we can rephrase Definition
5.1
in terms of bundle of ideals as follows:

Definition 5.3
.

A bundle of ideals
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
is called
partially split
if there is a VB subalgebroid
E\Rightarrow TM
of
TA\Rightarrow TM
such that:

TA=(A\times_{M}\mathfrak{k})\oplus E.

We emphasize that, unlike the groupoid case, IM Ehresmann connections for a surjective Lie algebroid morphism and partially split bundle of ideals are equivalent notions. From now on we will mostly assume the perspective of bundle of ideals.

5.3.
The infinitesimal partially split condition

We now look for alternative characterizations of (infinitesimal) partially split ideals.

If we represent the inclusion (
5.2
) by the diagram of VB algebroids:

\textstyle{A\times_{M}\mathfrak{k}\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0_{M}\ \ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TM\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M}

then the VB dual to this inclusion is the projection:

\textstyle{A\ltimes\mathfrak{k}^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T^{*}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{k}^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M}

Here
A\ltimes\mathfrak{k}^{*}\Rightarrow\mathfrak{k}^{*}
is the action algebroid associated with the representation
\nabla^{\mathfrak{k}^{*}}
of
A
on
\mathfrak{k}^{*}
dual to the representation (
5.1
), which is defined by:

\mathscr{L}_{\rho_{A}(\alpha)}(\langle\beta,X\rangle)=\langle\nabla^{\mathfrak{k}}_{\alpha}\beta,X\rangle+\langle\beta,\nabla^{\mathfrak{k}^{*}}_{\alpha}X\rangle.

We have the following infinitesimal analogue of Proposition
2.8
giving alternative characterizations of partial splittings (for the terminology, see the appendix):

Proposition 5.4
.

Given a bundle of ideals
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
, the following structures are in 1-to-1 correspondence:

(i)

VB subalgebroids
E\subset TA
that are complementary to
A\times_{M}\mathfrak{k}
:

TA=(A\times_{M}\mathfrak{k})\oplus E;

(ii)

VB algebroid morphisms
\theta:A\ltimes\mathfrak{k}^{*}\to T^{*}A
that are splittings of the natural projection
p:T^{*}A\to A\ltimes\mathfrak{k}^{*}
:

p\circ\,\theta=\mathrm{Id}_{T^{*}A};

(iii)

\mathfrak{k}
-valued, IM 1-forms
(L,l)\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{IM}}(A,\mathfrak{k})
that restrict to the identity on
\mathfrak{k}
:

l|_{\mathfrak{k}}=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathfrak{k}};

(iv)

linear, closed, IM 2-forms
\mu\in\Omega^{2}_{\mathrm{IM}}(A\ltimes\mathfrak{k}^{*})
that along
\mathfrak{k}\subset(A\ltimes\mathfrak{k}^{*})|_{M}
satisfy:

\big(\operatorname{pr}_{\mathfrak{k}}\circ\mu|_{M}\big)|_{\mathfrak{k}}=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathfrak{k}}.

Proof.

The equivalence between (i) and (ii) follows by passing from the VB algebroid
TA
to its dual VB algebroid
T^{*}A
, as explained above.

A vector bundle map

\textstyle{A\ltimes\mathfrak{k}^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\textstyle{T^{*}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Id}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{k}^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M}

is a VB algebroid morphism covering the identity if and only if its dual is a VB algebroid morphism:

\textstyle{TA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta^{\vee}}
\textstyle{A\oplus\mathfrak{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Id}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TM\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0_{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M}

But a Lie algebroid morphism
\theta^{\vee}:TA\to A\oplus\mathfrak{k}
is the same thing as an
\mathfrak{k}
-valued, IM 1-form
(L,l)\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{IM}}(A;\mathfrak{k})
(see Example
A.5
). This establishes the equivalence between (ii) and (iii), since the extra conditions along
M
correspond to each other.

Next, a closed IM 2-form
\mu:A\ltimes\mathfrak{k}^{*}\to T^{*}\mathfrak{k}^{*}
can be viewed, alternatively, as a VB algebroid morphism:

\textstyle{T(A\ltimes\mathfrak{k}^{*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\omega_{\mu})^{\flat}}
\textstyle{T^{*}(A\ltimes\mathfrak{k}^{*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T\mathfrak{k}^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(A\ltimes\mathfrak{k}^{*})^{*}}

where
\omega_{\mu}\in\Omega^{2}(A\ltimes\mathfrak{k}^{*})
is the fiberwise linear 2-form:

\omega_{\mu}=\mu^{*}\omega_{\mathrm{can}}.

The algebroid morphism
(\omega_{\mu})^{\flat}
restricts to a VB algebroid morphism:

\theta^{\vee}:TA\to A\oplus\mathfrak{k}

covering the identity
\mathrm{Id}:A\to A
, such that the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{TA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta^{\vee}}
\textstyle{A\oplus\mathfrak{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T(A\ltimes\mathfrak{k}^{*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\omega_{\mu})^{\flat}}
\textstyle{T^{*}(A\ltimes\mathfrak{k}^{*})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TM\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T\mathfrak{k}^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(A\ltimes\mathfrak{k}^{*})^{*}}

Moreover,
\theta^{\vee}
(or equivalently
\theta
) completely determines
\mu
: using the fact that
\mu
is a linear IM form, one finds that it coincides with the pullback under the algebroid morphism
\theta:A\ltimes\mathfrak{k}^{*}\to T^{*}A
of the canonical IM 2-form
\mu_{\mathrm{can}}
:

\mu=\theta^{*}\mu_{\mathrm{can}}.

This proves the equivalence between (ii)-(iv) since the additional conditions along
M
correspond to each other.
∎

Definition 5.5
.

Given a partially split bundle of ideals
\mathfrak{k}
with an IM Ehresmann connection
E\subset TA
, the corresponding
\mathfrak{k}
-valued IM 1-form
(L,l)\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{IM}}(A;\mathfrak{k})
, given by Proposition
5.4
(iii), is called
the
IM connection 1-form
.

We can now make precise the statement that IM Ehresmann connections are the infinitesimal counterpart of multiplicative Ehresmann connections:

Theorem 5.6
.

Let
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows M
be a target 1-connected Lie groupoid with Lie algebroid
A\Rightarrow M
. Given a bundle of ideals
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
there is a 1:1 correspondence:

\left\{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-10.5566pt\vbox{\halign{\relax\hfil\txtline@@{#}\hfil\cr\hbox{{\ignorespaces multiplicative Ehresmann}\\\hbox{{\ignorespaces connections $E\subset T\mathcal{G}$\, }\\\hbox{{\ignorespaces for $\mathcal{G}\times_{M}\mathfrak{k}$}\crcr}}}}\right\}\tilde{\longleftrightarrow}\left\{\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-10.13889pt\vbox{\halign{\relax\hfil\txtline@@{#}\hfil\cr\hbox{{\ignorespaces IM Ehresmann}\\\hbox{{\ignorespaces connections $E_{*}\subset TA$\, }\\\hbox{{\ignorespaces for $A\times_{M}\mathfrak{k}$
}\crcr}}}}\right\}}}}}}}

Proof.

The proof follows from the description of multiplicative and IM Ehresmann connections in terms of their connections 1-forms given by Propositions
2.8
and
5.4
, and by observing that for a target 1-connected Lie groupoid one has a canonical isomorphism
[
14
]

\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathcal{G};\mathfrak{k})\simeq\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{IM}}(A;\mathfrak{k}),

under which the extra conditions on the 1-forms correspond to each other.
∎

If
\mathcal{G}
is not target 1-connected the differentiation of multiplicative forms still exists, and one can still associate to a multiplicative Ehresmann connection a IM Ehresmann connection, but not conversely.

Note that for a IM connection 1-form
(L,l)\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{IM}}(A;\mathfrak{k})
, the symbol
l:A\to\mathfrak{k}
satisfies
l|_{\mathfrak{k}}=\mathrm{Id}
and hence gives a splitting of the short exact sequence:

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathfrak{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 64.7778pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 64.7778pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 49.04863pt\raise-11.43054pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{l}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}{}}{\hbox{\kern 40.77586pt\raise-3.14189pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}\lx@xy@spline@}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}{}{{}}{{}{}{}}{}}}}\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 85.02034pt\raise 6.1111pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\phi}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 102.2778pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 102.2778pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 140.3646pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 140.3646pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

In general, the induced splitting
B\to A
is not a Lie algebroid morphism.

The partially split condition implies certain properties of the bundle of ideals. A first consequence is the following infinitesimal versions of Corollaries
2.12
and
2.13
:

Corollary 5.7
.

Let
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
be a partially split bundle of ideals with IM connection 1-form
(L,l)\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{IM}}(A;\mathfrak{k})
, then

\nabla^{L}_{X}\xi:=i_{X}L(\xi),

(5.3)

defines a connection on
\mathfrak{k}
which preserves the Lie bracket:

\nabla^{L}_{X}[\xi,\eta]_{\mathfrak{k}}=[\nabla^{L}_{X}\xi,\eta]_{\mathfrak{k}}+[\xi,\nabla^{L}_{X}\eta]_{\mathfrak{k}},\quad X\in\mathfrak{X}(S),\xi,\eta\in\Gamma(\mathfrak{k}).

In particular,
\mathfrak{k}
is a locally trivial bundle of Lie algebras.

Proof.

Since
l|_{\mathfrak{k}}=\mathrm{Id}
, the symbol equation (
A.7
) implies that (
5.3
) defines a linear connection
\nabla^{L}
on
\mathfrak{k}
. On the other hand, the third equation in the IM condition (
A.2
) shows that one has

\mathscr{L}_{\xi}\eta=[\xi,\eta],\quad\xi,\eta\in\Gamma(\mathfrak{k}).

Then the second equation in (
A.2
) shows that
\nabla^{L}
preserves the Lie bracket.
∎

Corollary 5.8
.

Let
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
be a partially split bundle of ideals and fix
x\in M
. The symbol of any IM connection 1-form
(L,l)\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{IM}}(A;\mathfrak{k})
gives a decomposition of the isotropy Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}_{x}:=\ker\rho_{A}|_{x}
into a direct sum of ideals:

\mathfrak{g}_{x}\simeq(\mathfrak{g}_{x}\cap\ker l)\oplus\mathfrak{k}_{x}.

Proof.

If
\alpha\in\Gamma(A)
and
\beta\in\Gamma(\ker l)
are such that
\alpha_{x},\beta_{x}\in\mathfrak{g}_{x}
, then the third equation in the IM condition (
A.2
) shows that

[\alpha_{x},\beta_{x}]=[\alpha,\beta]|_{x}\in\ker l.

This proves that
\mathfrak{g}_{x}\cap\ker l
is an ideal in
\mathfrak{g}_{x}
, so the corollary follows.
∎

An interesting characterization of the partial split condition can be obtained as follows. Given a (usual) connection
\nabla
on a Lie algebroid
A\Rightarrow M
one has the following associated
A
-connections on
A
and
TM
:

\displaystyle\overline{\nabla}_{\alpha}\beta

\displaystyle:=\nabla_{\rho(\beta)}\alpha+[\alpha,\beta],

\displaystyle\overline{\nabla}_{\alpha}X

\displaystyle:=\rho(\nabla_{X}\alpha)+[\rho(\alpha),X]

They satisfy

\rho(\overline{\nabla}_{\alpha}\beta)=\overline{\nabla}_{\alpha}\rho(\beta).

One defines the
basic curvature
of
\nabla
to be the tensor

R^{\mathrm{bas}}_{\nabla}(\alpha,\beta)(X):=\nabla_{X}([\alpha,\beta])-[\nabla_{X}\alpha,\beta]-[\alpha,\nabla_{X}\beta]-\nabla_{\overline{\nabla}_{\beta}X}\alpha+\nabla_{\overline{\nabla}_{\alpha}X}\beta,

(5.4)

where
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
and
\alpha,\beta\in\Gamma(A)
. When
R^{\mathrm{bas}}_{\nabla}\equiv 0
one calls
\nabla
a Cartan connection and this is precisely the infinitesimal version of a Cartan connection on a Lie groupoid (see, e.g.,
[
1
,
3
,
4
]
).

Proposition 5.9
.

A bundle of ideals
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
is partially split if and only if there is a vector bundle splitting
l:A\to\mathfrak{k}
of the associated short exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

and a (usual) connection
\nabla
on
A
such that:

\overline{\nabla}l=0,\qquad l(R^{\mathrm{bas}}_{\nabla})=0.

(5.5)

In this case, the pair
(l,\nabla)
determines a IM connection 1-form
(L,l)\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{IM}}(A;\mathfrak{k})
by setting:

L:\Gamma(A)\to\Omega^{1}(M;\mathfrak{k}),\quad i_{X}L(\alpha):=l(\nabla_{X}\alpha).

Moreover, every IM connection 1-form
(L,l)
takes this form for some pair
(l,\nabla)
satisfying (
5.5
).

Proof.

Let
l:A\to\mathfrak{k}
be any splitting of the short exact sequence. Given a map
L:\Gamma(A)\to\Omega^{1}(M;\mathfrak{k})
, we claim that the pair
(L,l)
satisfies the IM-condition (
A.7
):

L(f\alpha)=fL(\alpha)+\operatorname{d}f\wedge l(\alpha)

if and only if

i_{X}L(\alpha)=l(\nabla_{X}\alpha)

for some connection
\nabla
in
A
. Clearly, if
L
takes this form then (
A.7
) holds. Conversely, given
(L,l)
satisfying (
A.7
) choose any connection
\nabla^{\prime}
on
A
and consider the difference:

D(\alpha,X):=i_{X}L(\alpha)-l(\nabla^{\prime}_{X}\alpha).

This is
C^{\infty}
-linear in both entries and takes values in
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
. Therefore, we can correct the connection
\nabla^{\prime}
by setting:

\nabla_{X}\alpha:=\nabla^{\prime}_{X}\alpha+D(\alpha,X),

so that
(L,l)
takes the desired form.

It remains to check that a pair
(L,l)
defined by
(l,\nabla)
satisfies the IM-conditions (
A.2
) if and only if (
5.5
) hold. Applying the definition of
\overline{\nabla}
, we obtain:

\displaystyle(\overline{\nabla}_{\alpha}l)(\beta)

\displaystyle=\overline{\nabla}_{\alpha}(l(\beta))-l(\overline{\nabla}_{\alpha}\beta)

\displaystyle=[\alpha,l(\beta)]-l(\nabla_{\rho(\beta)}\alpha+[\alpha,\beta])

\displaystyle=\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}l(\beta)-i_{\rho(\beta)}L(\alpha)-l([\alpha,\beta]),

for any
\alpha,\beta\in\Gamma(A)
. This shows that the condition
\overline{\nabla}l=0
amounts to the last IM condition in (
A.2
). It also shows that this condition can be written as:

l([\alpha,\beta])=[\alpha,l(\beta)]+l(\nabla_{\rho(\beta)}\alpha),

for any
\alpha,\beta\in\Gamma(A)
. Using this last identity, the definition of
L
and the expression for the basic curvature, we now find:

\displaystyle i_{X}L([\alpha,

\displaystyle\beta])-l(R^{\mathrm{bas}}_{\nabla}(\alpha,\beta)(X))=

\displaystyle=l(\nabla_{X}[\alpha,\beta])-l(R^{\mathrm{bas}}_{\nabla}(\alpha,\beta)(X))

\displaystyle=l([\nabla_{X}\alpha,\beta])+l([\alpha,\nabla_{X}\beta])+l(\nabla_{\overline{\nabla}_{\beta}X}\alpha)-l(\nabla_{\overline{\nabla}_{\alpha}X}\beta)

\displaystyle=l([\nabla_{X}\alpha,\beta])+l([\alpha,\nabla_{X}\beta])+l(\nabla_{\rho(\nabla_{X}\beta)+[\rho(\beta),X]}\alpha)-l(\nabla_{\rho(\nabla_{X}\alpha)+[\rho(\alpha),X]}\beta)

\displaystyle=[\alpha,l(\nabla_{X}\beta)]-l(\nabla_{[\rho(\alpha),X]}\beta)-[\beta,l(\nabla_{X}\alpha)]+l(\nabla_{[\rho(\beta),X]}\alpha)

\displaystyle=i_{X}\big(\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}L(\beta)-\mathscr{L}_{\beta}L(\alpha)\big).

So the second IM condition in (
A.2
) holds if and only if
l(R^{\mathrm{bas}}_{\nabla})=0
.
∎

Remark 5.10
.

The connection
\nabla
in the previous proposition is not uniquely determined by the partial IM connection 1-form
(L,l)\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{IM}}(A;\mathfrak{k})
.

5.4.
Couplings

In this section we give a coupling description of algebroids with a partially split bundle of ideals. This is inspired by (and in fact generalizes) the classical coupling description of symplectic fibrations
[
28
]
and horizontally non-degenerate Poisson structures
[
33
,
34
]
.

To state the main result in this section we consider a partially split bundle of ideals
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
with a fixed choice of IM connection 1-form
(L,l)\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{IM}}(A,\mathfrak{k})
. The base map gives a vector bundle splitting of the associated short exact sequence

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{l}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

which we use to identify
A\simeq B\oplus\mathfrak{k}
. Then we associate the following data:

(i)

A linear connection
\nabla^{L}
on the vector bundle
\mathfrak{k}\to M
, by setting:

\nabla^{L}_{X}\xi:=i_{X}L(\xi).

(ii)

A tensor
U\in\Gamma(B^{*}\otimes T^{*}M\otimes\mathfrak{k})
given by:

U(\alpha,X):=-i_{X}L(\alpha).

That this is a connection and a tensor follows from the symbol equation (
A.7
) and the fact that
l|_{\mathfrak{k}}=\mathrm{Id}
. Notice that the connection
\nabla^{L}
has already appeared in Corollary
5.7
.

Proposition 5.11
.

The data
(\nabla^{L},U)
associated with a IM connection 1-form
(L,l)\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{IM}}(A,\mathfrak{k})
satisfies the structure equations:

(S1)

the connection
\nabla^{L}
preserves the Lie bracket
[\cdot,\cdot]_{\mathfrak{k}}
, i.e.,

\nabla^{L}_{X}[\xi,\eta]_{\mathfrak{k}}=[\nabla^{L}_{X}\xi,\eta]_{\mathfrak{k}}+[\xi,\nabla^{L}_{X}\eta]_{\mathfrak{k}};

(S2)

the curvature of
\nabla^{L}
is related to
\mathrm{ad}_{U}
as follows:

\nabla^{L}_{\rho_{B}(\alpha)}\nabla^{L}_{X}-\nabla^{L}_{X}\nabla^{L}_{\rho_{B}(\alpha)}-\nabla^{L}_{[\rho_{B}(\alpha),X]}=[U(\alpha,X),\cdot]_{\mathfrak{k}};

(S3)

U
satisfies the “mixed” cocyle-type equation:

\displaystyle\nabla^{L}_{\rho_{B}(\alpha)}U(\beta,X)

\displaystyle-\nabla^{L}_{\rho_{B}(\beta)}U(\alpha,X)+\nabla^{L}_{X}U(\alpha,\rho_{B}(\beta))

\displaystyle+U(\alpha,[\rho_{B}(\beta),X])-U(\beta,[\rho_{B}(\alpha),X])=U([\alpha,\beta]_{B},X),

for all
X\in\mathfrak{X}^{1}(M)
,
\alpha,\beta\in\Gamma(B)
,
\xi,\eta\in\Gamma(\mathfrak{k})
.

The proof of this proposition follows immediately from the IM conditions (
A.2
) and is left to the reader.

Definition 5.12
.

Let
B\Rightarrow M
be a Lie algebroid and
(\mathfrak{k},[\cdot,\cdot]_{\mathfrak{k}})
a Lie algebra bundle over
M
. A
coupling data
is a pair
(\nabla^{L},U)
, where
\nabla^{L}
is a connection on
\mathfrak{k}
and
U\in\Gamma(B^{*}\otimes T^{*}M\otimes\mathfrak{k})
is a tensor field satisfying the structure equations (S1), (S2) and (S3).

The following result shows that the coupling data gives a way of codifying algebroids with a partially split bundle of ideals.

Proposition 5.13
.

Let
B\Rightarrow M
be a Lie algebroid,
(\mathfrak{k},[\cdot,\cdot]_{\mathfrak{k}})
a Lie algebra bundle over
M
and
(U,\nabla^{L})
coupling data. Then
A:=B\oplus\mathfrak{k}
is a Lie algebroid, with anchor
\rho_{A}(\alpha,\xi):=\rho_{B}(\alpha)
and Lie bracket:

[(\alpha,\xi),(\beta,\eta)]_{A}:=([\alpha,\beta]_{B},U(\alpha,\rho_{B}(\beta))+\nabla^{L}_{\rho_{B}(\alpha)}\eta-\nabla^{L}_{\rho_{B}(\beta)}\xi+[\xi,\eta]_{\mathfrak{k}}).

(5.6)

Then
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
is partially split, with IM connection 1-form
(L,l)
given by:

l=\mathrm{pr}_{\mathfrak{k}},\quad i_{X}L(\alpha,\xi)=\nabla^{L}_{X}\xi-U(\alpha,X).

(5.7)

Moreover, any Lie algebroid
A\Rightarrow M
together with a partially split bundle of ideals
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
is isomorphic to one of this type.

Proof.

Assume we are given coupling data
(U,\nabla^{L})
. The skew-symmetrization with respect to
\alpha
and
\beta
