of (S3) gives:

\nabla^{L}_{X}\big(U(\alpha,\rho_{B}(\beta))+U(\beta,\rho_{B}(\alpha))\big)=0,

which yields:

U(\alpha,\rho_{B}(\beta))=-U(\beta,\rho_{B}(\alpha)).

(5.8)

Hence (
5.6
) defines a skew-symmetric bracket. It is immediate to check that it satisfies the Leibniz identity and that its Jacobiator is given by:

\displaystyle J((0,\xi),(0,\eta),(0,\nu))

\displaystyle=(0,J_{\mathfrak{k}}(\alpha,\eta,\nu))

\displaystyle J((\alpha,0),(0,\xi),(0,\eta))

\displaystyle=(0,C_{1}(\alpha,\xi,\eta))

\displaystyle J((\alpha,0),(\beta,0),(0,\xi))

\displaystyle=(0,C_{2}(\alpha,\beta,\xi)

\displaystyle J((\alpha,0),(\beta,0),(\gamma,0))

\displaystyle=(J_{B}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma),C_{3}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma))

where
J_{\mathfrak{k}}
and
J_{B}
are the Jacobiators of the brackets
[\cdot,\cdot]_{\mathfrak{k}}
and
[\cdot,\cdot]_{B}
and

\displaystyle C_{1}(\alpha,\xi,\eta)

\displaystyle:=\nabla^{L}_{\rho_{B}(\alpha)}[\xi,\eta]_{\mathfrak{k}}-[\nabla^{L}_{\rho_{B}(\alpha)}\xi,\eta]_{\mathfrak{k}}-[\xi,\nabla^{L}_{\rho_{B}(\alpha)}\eta]_{\mathfrak{k}}

\displaystyle C_{2}(\alpha,\beta,\xi)

\displaystyle:=\nabla^{L}_{\rho_{B}(\alpha)}\nabla^{L}_{\rho_{B}(\beta)}\xi-\nabla^{L}_{\rho_{B}(\beta)}\nabla^{L}_{\rho_{B}(\alpha)}\xi-\nabla^{L}_{[\rho_{B}(\alpha),\rho_{B}(\beta)]}\xi-[U(\alpha,\rho_{B}(\beta)),\xi]_{\mathfrak{k}}

\displaystyle C_{3}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)

\displaystyle:=\nabla^{L}_{\rho_{B}(\alpha)}U(\beta,\rho_{B}(\gamma))+U(\alpha,[\rho_{B}(\beta),\rho_{B}(\gamma)])-\nabla^{L}_{\rho_{B}(\beta)}U(\alpha,\rho_{B}(\gamma))

\displaystyle\qquad\quad-U(\beta,[\rho_{B}(\alpha),\rho_{B}(\gamma)])+\nabla^{L}_{\rho_{B}(\gamma)}U(\alpha,\rho_{B}(\beta))-U([\alpha,\beta]_{B},\rho_{B}(\gamma))

If in the structure equations (S1), (S2) and (S3) we replace
X
by
\rho_{B}(\alpha)
,
\rho_{B}(\beta)
and
\rho_{B}(\gamma)
, respectively, we obtain that these 3 expressions vanish. Hence, the bracket (
5.7
) satisfies the Jacobi identity, so we obtain a Lie algebroid.
If we now define
l:A\to\mathfrak{k}
and
L:\Gamma(A)\to\Omega^{1}(M,\mathfrak{k})
by (
5.7
), one checks that the symbol equation (
A.7
) holds and that the IM conditions (
A.2
) are equivalent to (S1)-(S3).

Finally, if we start with a partially split bundle of ideals
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
, as we saw above, a choice of IM connection 1-form
(L,l)\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{IM}}(A,\mathfrak{k})
determines coupling data satisfying the structure equations and a vector bundle isomorphism
A\simeq B\oplus\mathfrak{k}
where
B\simeq\ker l
. The anchor of
A
is then given by
\rho_{A}(\alpha,\xi):=\rho_{B}(\alpha)
and all we have to check is that, under this isomorphism, the Lie bracket becomes (
5.7
). For this we use the third equation in the IM condition (
A.2
) for
(L,l)
and the definitions of
\nabla^{L}
and
U
to conclude that:

\bullet

If
\alpha\in\Gamma(B)\simeq\Gamma(\ker l)
and
\xi\in\Gamma(\mathfrak{k})
then:

[\alpha,\xi]_{A}=\nabla^{L}_{\rho_{B}(\alpha)}\xi;

\bullet

If
\alpha,\beta\in\Gamma(B)\simeq\Gamma(\ker l)
then:

[\alpha,\beta]_{A}=[\alpha,\beta]_{B}+U(\alpha,\rho_{B}(\beta)).

Formula (
5.7
) for the bracket follows from these since
\mathfrak{k}
is a bundle of ideals.
∎

5.5.
Flat IM Ehresmann connections

Let
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
be a bundle of ideals and let
B=A/\mathfrak{k}
. As we saw in Section
5.4
, a choice of IM connection 1-form
(L,l)\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{IM}}(A,\mathfrak{k})
gives rise to the coupling data
(\nabla^{L},U)
, where
U\in\Gamma(B^{*}\otimes T^{*}M\otimes\mathfrak{k})
is a tensor field and
\nabla^{L}
is a usual connection on
\mathfrak{k}
. Using Proposition
5.13
, it is not hard to check that we have a
\mathfrak{k}
-valued IM 2-form

(\tilde{L},\tilde{l})\in\Omega^{2}_{\mathrm{IM}}(A,\mathfrak{k})
, where:

\tilde{L}(\alpha,\xi)=R^{\nabla^{L}}\cdot\xi-\operatorname{d}^{\nabla^{L}}U(\alpha),\qquad\tilde{l}(\alpha,\xi)=-U(\alpha),

(5.9)

for
\alpha\in\Gamma(B)
and
\xi\in\Gamma(\mathfrak{k})
. This is the infinitesimal version of the curvature of the multiplicative Ehresmann connection from Proposition
2.22
. Namely, in the case where
\phi:A\to B
is induced by a groupoid morphism
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
and the IM Ehresmann connection is induced by a multiplicative Ehresmann
E
connection, (
5.9
) is the IM form corresponding to curvature 2-form
\Omega
of
E
.

Definition 5.14
.

An IM Ehresmann connection
(L,l)\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{IM}}(A;\mathfrak{k})
with associated coupling data
(\nabla^{L},U)
is called:

(i)

totally flat
if
R^{\nabla^{L}}=0
and
U=0
;

(ii)

leafwise flat
if the splitting
B\to A
,
\alpha\mapsto(\alpha,0)
, is a Lie algebroid map, i.e.,
U(\alpha,\rho_{B}(\beta))=0
;

(iii)

kernel flat
if the associated linear connection
\nabla^{L}
is flat:
R^{\nabla^{L}}=0
.

The first condition is equivalent to involutivity of the distribution
E\subset TA
corresponding to the IM Ehresmann connection. This condition is very restrictive, as the following infinitesimal analogue of Proposition
2.42
shows:

Proposition 5.15
.

If a bundle of ideals
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
admits a totally flat IM Ehresmann connection, then there is a covering space of the base
p:\widetilde{M}\to M
such that the pullback of
A
to
\widetilde{M}
is isomorphic to a product
p^{*}A\simeq p^{*}B\times\mathfrak{g}
, where
p^{*}\mathfrak{k}\simeq\widetilde{M}\times\mathfrak{g}
.

Proof.

Let
(\nabla^{L},U=0)
be a totally flat coupling data. Let
p:\widetilde{M}\to M
denote the holonomy cover of
\nabla^{L}
. Parallel transport along
\nabla^{L}
induces a trivialization of the bundle of Lie algebras
p^{*}\mathfrak{k}\simeq\widetilde{M}\times\mathfrak{g}
, where
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{k}_{x}
, for some
x\in M
. Since
\nabla^{L}
becomes the trivial connection on
\widetilde{M}\times\mathfrak{g}
, formulas (
5.6
) show that the algebroid
p^{*}A
is a product (see also the example from Subsection
6.2
).
∎

The second flatness condition can be understood as a leafwise version of the first:

Proposition 5.16
.

Given a bundle of ideals
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
, an IM Ehresmann connection
(L,l)\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{IM}}(A;\mathfrak{k})
is leafwise flat if and only if, for any leaf
\mathcal{O}\subset M
of the Lie algebroid
A
, the pullback IM Ehresmann connection
(L,l)|_{\mathcal{O}}\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{IM}}(A|_{\mathcal{O}};\mathfrak{k}|_{\mathcal{O}})
is totally flat.

Proof.

Given a leaf
\mathcal{O}
of
A
, the coupling data associated to the pullback of
(L,l)
to
A|_{\mathcal{O}}
is
(\nabla^{\mathcal{O}},U^{\mathcal{O}})
, where
\nabla^{\mathcal{O}}
is the pullback of the connection
\nabla^{L}
to
\mathfrak{k}|_{\mathcal{O}}
and
U^{\mathcal{O}}
is the restriction of
U
to
B|_{\mathcal{O}}\otimes T\mathcal{O}
. Now,
(L,l)
being leafwise flat, i.e.,

U(\alpha,\rho_{B}(\beta))=0,\quad\textrm{for all}\ \alpha,\beta\in B,

is clearly equivalent to
U^{\mathcal{O}}=0
for all leaves
\mathcal{O}\subset M
. On the other hand, using that
A|_{\mathcal{O}}
is transitive and (S2), we see that
U^{\mathcal{O}}=0
implies that
\nabla^{\mathcal{O}}
is flat.
∎

Note that a leafwise flat IM Ehresmann connection turns
A
into a semi-direct product, in the sense of the following.

Let
B\Rightarrow M
be a Lie algebroid which acts on a bundle of Lie algebras
(\mathfrak{k},[\cdot,\cdot]_{\mathfrak{k}})\to M
by infinitesimal Lie algebra automorphisms. The
semi-direct product
is the Lie algebroid
A:=B\times_{M}\mathfrak{k}\Rightarrow M
with Lie bracket:

[(\alpha,\xi),(\beta,\eta)]=([\alpha,\beta],\nabla^{\mathfrak{k}}_{\alpha}\eta-\nabla^{\mathfrak{k}}_{\beta}\xi+[\xi,\eta]_{\mathfrak{k}})

and anchor
\rho_{B}\circ\operatorname{pr}_{B}
. Note that the short exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\times_{M}\mathfrak{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{pr}_{1}}

is canonically split by a Lie algebroid morphism:

\sigma:B\longrightarrow B\times_{M}\mathfrak{k},\quad\alpha\mapsto(\alpha,0).

Conversely, given a surjective Lie algebroid map
\phi:A\to B
covering the identity, which admits a
Lie algebroid splitting
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathfrak{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}

we obtain an action of
B\Rightarrow M
on the bundle of Lie algebras
(\mathfrak{k},[\cdot,\cdot]_{\mathfrak{k}})\to M
:

\nabla_{\alpha}^{\mathfrak{k}}\xi:=[\sigma(\alpha),\xi]_{A},

preserving the Lie algebra structure, and a Lie algebroid isomorphism:

B\times_{M}\mathfrak{k}\simeq A,\quad(\alpha,\xi)\mapsto\sigma(\alpha)+\xi.

Notice that the Lie algebroid splitting
\sigma:B\to A
allows to pull back
\mathfrak{k}
-valued IM forms on
A
to IM forms on
B
:

\sigma^{*}:\Omega_{\mathrm{IM}}^{\bullet}(A;\mathfrak{k})\longrightarrow\Omega_{\mathrm{IM}}^{\bullet}(B;\mathfrak{k}).

Therefore we conclude the following:

Corollary 5.17
.

Given a bundle of ideals
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
, with an IM Ehresmann connection
(L,l)\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{IM}}(A;\mathfrak{k})
that is leafwise flat, we obtain a IM 1-form
(U,0)\in\Omega_{\mathrm{IM}}^{1}(B;\mathfrak{k})
.

The following infinitesimal version of Proposition
2.39
gives yet another notion of flatness, weaker than totally flat and stronger than leafwise flat:

Proposition 5.18
.

Let
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
be a bundle of ideals with quotient
B:=A/\mathfrak{k}
. The following are equivalent:

(i)

There is an IM Ehresmann connection
(L,l)\in\Omega^{1}(A;\mathfrak{k})
on
A
and
\operatorname{pr}:A\to B
whose associated splitting
\sigma:B\to A
is a horizontal algebroid splitting: i.e.,
\sigma^{*}(L,l)=0
;

(ii)

There exists an IM Ehresmann connection
(L,l)\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{IM}}(A;\mathfrak{k})
with coupling data
(\nabla^{L},U=0)
;

(iii)

A
is isomorphic to a semi-direct product
A\simeq B\times_{M}\mathfrak{k}
and
\mathfrak{k}
admits an
B
-invariant connection
\nabla^{L}
preserving the bracket
[\cdot,\cdot]_{\mathfrak{k}}
(see Definition
A.9
).

Proof.

Note that
\sigma^{*}(L,l)=(U,0)
, so (i) and (ii) are equivalent.

If
U=0
, then the formula for the Lie bracket on
A
(
5.6
) together with (S1), (S2) are equivalent to
\nabla^{L}
being a
B
invariant connection on
\mathfrak{k}
which preserves
[\cdot,\cdot]_{\mathfrak{k}}
.

Conversely, if
A\simeq B\times_{M}\mathfrak{k}
, and the representation of
B
on
\mathfrak{k}
admits a
B
-invariant connection
\nabla^{L}
which preserves
[\cdot,\cdot]_{\mathfrak{k}}
, then
(\nabla^{L},U=0)
is a coupling data for an IM Ehresmann connection.
∎

Finally, we look at the kernel flat IM Ehresmann connections. For this, observe that the representation
\nabla^{\mathfrak{k}}
of the Lie algebroid
A
restricts to an
A
-representation on the center
z(\mathfrak{k})
. While the representation of
A
on the whole
\mathfrak{k}
, in general, does not factor to a representation of
B
, its restriction to the center
z(\mathfrak{k})
does factor. We still denote this representation by
\nabla^{\mathfrak{k}}
.
In terms of the coupling the representation of
B
on the center
z(\mathfrak{k})
is given by:

\nabla^{\mathfrak{k}}_{\alpha}\xi=\nabla^{L}_{\rho_{B}(\alpha)}\xi,

for all
\alpha\in\Gamma(B)
,
\xi\in\Gamma(z(\mathfrak{k}))
. This follows from the expression of the bracket given in Proposition
5.13
.

In the kernel flat case, the structure equations (S1)-(S3) of Proposition
5.11
simplify to:

Proposition 5.19
.

Let
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
be a partially split bundle of ideals with coupling data
(\nabla^{L},U)
. If
\nabla^{L}
is flat, then:

(S1’)

The connection
\nabla^{L}
preserves the bracket
[\cdot,\cdot]_{\mathfrak{k}}
;

(S2’)

The tensor
U
takes values in the center of
\mathfrak{k}
;

(S3’)

The pair
(\operatorname{d}^{\nabla^{L}}U,U)
is a
z(\mathfrak{k})
-valued IM 2-form on
B
.

Conversely, given a Lie algebroid
B\Rightarrow M
, a Lie algebra bundle
\mathfrak{k}\to M
with a flat connection
\nabla^{L}
, and a tensor field
U\in\Gamma(B^{*}\otimes T^{*}M\otimes\mathfrak{k})
satisfying (S1’)-(S3’),
then
\mathfrak{k}
is a partially split bundle of ideals in Lie algebroid
A=B\oplus\mathfrak{k}
with kernel flat coupling data
(\nabla^{L},U)
.

Proof.

If
\nabla^{L}
is flat, it is obvious that (S1)-(S2) are equivalent to (S1’)-(S2’).

Now consider the pair
(\operatorname{d}^{\nabla^{L}}U,U)
. The properties of
\operatorname{d}^{\nabla^{L}}
give immediately that:

\operatorname{d}^{\nabla^{L}}U(f\alpha)=f\operatorname{d}^{\nabla^{L}}U(\alpha)+\operatorname{d}f\wedge U(\alpha),

so
U:B\to T^{*}M\otimes\mathfrak{k}
is the symbol of
\operatorname{d}^{\nabla^{L}}U:\Gamma(B)\to\Omega^{2}(M,\mathfrak{k})
, i.e., (
A.7
) holds.
The skew-symmetry of
U
, given by (
5.8
), can be written as:

i_{\rho_{B}(\beta)}U(\alpha)=-i_{\rho_{B}(\alpha)}U(\beta),

which is the first equation in the IM conditions (
A.9
). Then (S3) can be rewritten
using the connection
\nabla^{L}
as:

U([\alpha,\beta]_{B})=\mathscr{L}^{\nabla^{L}}_{\rho_{B}(\alpha)}U(\beta)-i_{\rho_{B}(\beta)}\operatorname{d}^{\nabla^{L}}U(\alpha),

and since
\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}=\mathscr{L}^{\nabla^{L}}_{\rho_{B}(\alpha)}
this gives the third equation in the IM conditions (
A.9
).

Finally, applying
\operatorname{d}^{\nabla}
to this last equation, using that its square is zero and “Cartan’s magic formula” holds for
\mathscr{L}^{\nabla^{L}}
, we obtain:

\displaystyle\operatorname{d}^{\nabla^{L}}U([\alpha,\beta]_{B})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{d}^{\nabla^{L}}\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}U(\beta)-\operatorname{d}^{\nabla^{L}}i_{\rho_{B}(\beta)}\operatorname{d}^{\nabla^{L}}U(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}\operatorname{d}^{\nabla^{L}}U(\beta)-\mathscr{L}^{\nabla^{L}}_{\rho_{B}(\beta)}\operatorname{d}^{\nabla^{L}}U(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}\operatorname{d}^{\nabla^{L}}U(\beta)-\mathscr{L}_{\beta}\operatorname{d}^{\nabla^{L}}U(\alpha),

which is the second equation in the IM conditions (
A.9
). Hence,
(\operatorname{d}^{\nabla^{L}}U,U)
is a
z(\mathfrak{k})
-valued IM 2-form on
B
.

It should clear from this that the converse also holds: a pair
(\nabla^{L},U)
, with
\nabla^{L}
flat and satisfying (S1’)-(S3’), also satisfies (S1)-(S3). Hence, the last part follows from Proposition
5.13
.
∎

Remark 5.20
.

For a kernel flat partial splitting of
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
, the proposition yields the IM valued 2-form:

(\operatorname{d}^{\nabla^{L}}U,U)\in\Omega^{2}_{\mathrm{IM}}(B;z(\mathfrak{k})),

(5.10)

which is the infinitesimal analogue of the class from Proposition
2.29
. Namely, the IM form corresponding the multiplicative form
\underline{\Omega}
is
(-\operatorname{d}^{\nabla^{L}}U,-U)
– see (
5.9
).

Since
\nabla^{L}
is flat, by Proposition
A.10
we have an induced differential:

\operatorname{d}^{\nabla^{L}}_{\mathrm{IM}}:\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{IM}}(B;z(\mathfrak{k}))\longrightarrow\Omega^{\bullet+1}_{\mathrm{IM}}(B;z(\mathfrak{k})),

for which the element (
5.10
) is clearly closed. Applying the chain map from Lemma
A.11
we obtain a
B
-cocycle with values in
z(\mathfrak{k})
:

\lambda\in\Omega^{2}(B;z(\mathfrak{k})),\quad\lambda(\alpha,\beta):=U(\alpha,\rho_{B}(\beta)).

That
\lambda
is a cocycle can be seen directly also from (S3). Note that the classes

[(\operatorname{d}^{\nabla^{L}}U,U)]\in H^{2}_{\mathrm{IM}}(B;z(\mathfrak{k}))\quad\textrm{and}\quad[\lambda]\in H^{2}(B;z(\mathfrak{k}))

depend on the choice of the IM connection.

The remark can be developed into a criterion for the existence of kernel flat IM connection for
abelian bundle of ideals

\mathfrak{k}\subset A
. In this case, the canonical action of
B:=A/\mathfrak{k}
is on
\mathfrak{k}=z(\mathfrak{k})
and, after choosing a splitting
A\simeq B\oplus\mathfrak{k}
, the Lie bracket takes the form:

[(\alpha,\xi),(\beta,\eta)]_{A}:=([\alpha,\beta]_{B},\lambda(\alpha,\beta)+\nabla^{\mathfrak{k}}_{\alpha}\eta-\nabla^{\mathfrak{k}}_{\beta}\xi).

Then
\lambda\in\Omega^{2}(B;\mathfrak{k})
is a 2-cocycle, whose cohomology class:

c_{2}(A):=[\lambda]\in H^{2}(B;\mathfrak{k})

is independent of the splitting, and it determines the extension
\mathfrak{k}\to A\to B
up to isomorphism. We have the following:

Proposition 5.21
.

An abelian bundle of ideals
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
, with
B:=A/\mathfrak{k}
, admits a kernel flat partial splitting if and only if
\mathfrak{k}
admits a flat connection
\nabla
inducing
\nabla^{\mathfrak{k}}
and the class
c_{2}(A)
is in the image of the map (from Lemma
A.11
):

H_{\mathrm{IM}}^{2}(B;\mathfrak{k})\to H^{2}(B;\mathfrak{k}).

Proof.

Given a flat coupling data
(\nabla^{L},U)
, the connection
\nabla^{L}
induces
\nabla^{\mathfrak{k}}
, and, as remarked,
c_{2}(A)
is the image of the class
[(\operatorname{d}^{\nabla^{L}}U,U)]\in H_{\mathrm{IM}}^{2}(B;\mathfrak{k})
.

Conversely, assume one is given a flat connection
\nabla^{L}
inducing
\nabla^{\mathfrak{k}}
and that
c_{2}(A)
is the image of
c\in H^{2}_{\mathrm{IM}}(B;\mathfrak{k})
. Note that any representative of
c
if of the form
(\operatorname{d}^{\nabla^{L}}U,U)
. Choose a splitting inducing

\lambda(\alpha,\beta)=U(\alpha,\rho_{B}(\beta)).

Since the pair
(\nabla^{L},U)
satisfies (S1’)-(S3’), it follows from Proposition
5.13
that it is the coupling data of a kernel flat partial splitting for
A
.
∎

We also have the infinitesimal version of Corollary
2.41
:

Corollary 5.22
.

A semi-direct product
A=B\times_{M}\mathfrak{k}
, with
\mathfrak{k}
abelian is partially split if and only if the representation of
B
on
\mathfrak{k}
admits a
B
-invariant connection (in the sense of Definition
A.9
).

Proof.

If
\mathfrak{k}
admits a
B
-invariant connection
\nabla^{L}
, then
(\nabla^{L},U=0)
is the coupling data for an IM connection on
B\times_{M}\mathfrak{k}
.

Conversely, let
(L,l)\in\Omega^{1}(B\times_{M}\mathfrak{k};\mathfrak{k})
be an IM Ehresmann connection. Both the projection
\phi:B\times_{M}\mathfrak{k}\to B
and the inclusion
\sigma:B\to B\times_{M}\mathfrak{k}
are Lie algebroid morphisms which intertwine the actions on
\mathfrak{k}
. Therefore,

\phi^{*}\circ\sigma^{*}(L,l)
is an IM 1-form. By subtracting it from
(L,l)
, we may assume that

\sigma^{*}(L,l)=0
. By Proposition
5.18
, this is equivalent to
(L,l)
having coupling data
(\nabla^{L},U=0)
, with
\nabla^{L}
a
B
-invariant connection on
\mathfrak{k}
.
∎

6.
Examples and applications (algebroids)

Using the correspondence from Theorem
5.6
, many of the examples that follow can be seen as infinitesimal counterparts of the examples discussed in Section
3
. Note, however, that we will make no integrability assumption, so often we obtain more general classes of examples.

6.1.
Lie algebra bundles

Let
A\to M
be a Lie algebra bundle. A bundle of ideals
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
is not necessarily partially split. The obstructions are precisely those found in Corollaries
5.7
and
5.8
:

Proposition 6.1
.

A bundle of ideals
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
in a Lie algebra bundle
is partially split if and only if the following two conditions hold:

(i)

\mathfrak{k}
is a Lie algebra bundle;

(ii)

there exists a splitting
A\simeq B\oplus\mathfrak{k}
, for which the fiberwise Lie bracket is a direct product.

Proof.

By Corollaries
5.7
and
5.8
, a partial splitting for
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
yields a (usual) connection
\nabla^{L}
on
\mathfrak{k}
which preserves the Lie bracket and a splitting
A\simeq B\oplus\mathfrak{k}
, for which the fiberwise Lie bracket is a direct product.

Conversely, fix a connection
\nabla^{L}
on
\mathfrak{k}
which preserves the Lie bracket and a splitting
A\simeq B\oplus\mathfrak{k}
for which the Lie bracket is a direct product. Then we can obtain a partial splitting from Proposition
5.11
by setting
U:=0
.
∎

Remark 6.2
.

If
M
is connected, then for a bundle of Lie algebras
(\mathfrak{k},[\cdot,\cdot]_{\mathfrak{k}})\to M
to be a Lie algebra bundle, it suffices that:

H^{2}(\mathfrak{k}_{x},\mathfrak{k}_{x})=0,\quad\forall\,x\in M.

Indeed, this condition implies that each
\mathfrak{k}_{x}
is a
rigid
Lie algebra: any small deformation of the Lie bracket on
\mathfrak{k}_{x}
is isomorphic to
\mathfrak{k}_{x}
(see, e.g.,
[
13
]
). Therefore, all Lie algebras in
\mathfrak{k}
are isomorphic to each other and it follows that
\mathfrak{k}
is a bundle of Lie algebras.

6.2.
Products

Consider the Lie algebroid
A\Rightarrow M
obtained as the product of a Lie algebroid
B\Rightarrow M
and a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
:

A=B\times\mathfrak{g}.

Then
\mathfrak{k}:=M\times\mathfrak{g}\subset A
is a bundle of Lie algebras which is always partially split with a canonical, totally flat partial splitting. This follows by observing that we have canonical isomorphisms:

TA=TB\times(\mathfrak{g}\ltimes\mathfrak{g}),\quad A\times_{M}\mathfrak{k}=B\times(\mathfrak{g}\ltimes\mathfrak{g}),

in terms of which the inclusion (
5.2
) is

B\times(\mathfrak{g}\ltimes\mathfrak{g})\to TB\times(\mathfrak{g}\ltimes\mathfrak{g}),\quad(b,v,w)\mapsto(0_{b},v,w).

Hence, we have the canonical IM Ehresmann connection
E\subset TA
given by:

E_{(b,v)}:=\{(X,v,0):X\in T_{b}(B_{x}),v\in\mathfrak{g}\}.

It is also easy to determine the alternative data of specifying this connection given by Proposition
5.4
, namely:

\bullet

The canonical splitting
\theta:A\ltimes\mathfrak{k}^{*}\to T^{*}A
:

\theta:B\times(\mathfrak{g}\ltimes\mathfrak{g}^{*})\to T^{*}(B)\times(\mathfrak{g}\ltimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}),\quad(b,v,\xi)\mapsto(0_{b},v,\xi).

\bullet

The IM connection 1-form
(L,l)\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{IM}}(A,\mathfrak{k})
:

\displaystyle l:B\times\mathfrak{g}\to M\times\mathfrak{g},\quad L(b_{x},v)=(x,v),

\displaystyle L:\Gamma(B)\times C^{\infty}(M;\mathfrak{g})\to\Omega^{1}(M,\mathfrak{g}),\quad L(\alpha,f)=\operatorname{d}f;

\bullet

The linear, closed, IM 2-form
\mu\in\Omega^{2}_{\mathrm{IM}}(A\ltimes\mathfrak{k}^{*})
:

\mu:B\times(\mathfrak{g}\ltimes\mathfrak{g}^{*})\to T^{*}(M\times\mathfrak{g}^{*})=T^{*}M\times(\mathfrak{g}\ltimes\mathfrak{g}^{*}),\quad(\alpha,v,\xi)\mapsto(0,v,\xi).

6.3.
Transitive algebroids

Let
A\Rightarrow M
be a transitive Lie algebroid. Its isotropy
\mathfrak{k}=\ker\rho
is a bundle of ideals. We claim that
this is always partially split, and there is a 1-to-1 correspondence between splittings of the anchor:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho}
\scriptstyle{l}
\textstyle{TM\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau}
\textstyle{0}

and IM connection 1-forms
(L,l)\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{IM}}(A,\mathfrak{k})
.

Indeed, given a splitting
l
, we can define a linear operator
L:\Gamma(A)\to\Omega^{1}(M,\mathfrak{k})
by setting:

i_{X}L(\alpha):=l([\tau(X),\alpha]).

(6.1)

One checks easily that the pair
(L,l)
satisfies (
A.2
), so it is a
\mathfrak{k}
-valued, IM 1-form, with
l|_{\mathfrak{k}}=\mathrm{Id}
.

Conversely, given any IM connection 1-form
(L^{\prime},l)\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{IM}}(A,\mathfrak{k})
, the bundle map
l:A\to\mathfrak{k}
determines a splitting of the anchor. We claim that
L^{\prime}=L
, where
L
is given by (
6.1
). Note that the difference
L-L^{\prime}
is a
\mathfrak{k}
-valued, IM 1-form whose symbol vanishes. It follows from the last equation in (
A.2
) that:

i_{\rho(\beta)}(L(\alpha)-L^{\prime}(\alpha))=0,\quad\forall\beta\in A.

Since
\rho
is surjective, this means that
L=L^{\prime}
.

6.4.
Cartan connections

Recall that a
Cartan connection
is a connection
\nabla
on a Lie algebroid
A
whose basic curvature vanishes identically:

R^{\mathrm{bas}}_{\nabla}\equiv 0.

Hence, as an immediate consequence of Proposition
5.9
, we obtain:

Corollary 6.3
.

Let
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
be a bundle of ideals. If
A
admits a Cartan connection
\nabla
and a splitting
l:A\to\mathfrak{k}
which is
\overline{\nabla}
-invariant, then
\mathfrak{k}
is partially split. In particular, this holds when
A
admits a fiberwise metric that is
\overline{\nabla}
-invariant.

Proof.

The second part of the statement follows by choosing the splitting
l:A\to\mathfrak{k}
to be the orthogonal projection relative to the invariant metric.
∎

In Subsection
3.8
we saw that, for a Lie groupoid admitting a bi-invariant metric, every bundle of ideals is partially split. At the Lie algebroid level, the corresponding notion of a bi-invariant metric (see
[
24
]
) is given by a Cartan connection and a pair of metrics
(\eta^{A},\eta^{M})
on
A
and
M
satisfying:

\overline{\nabla}\eta^{A}=0,\quad\overline{\nabla}\eta^{M}=0.

The previous corollary shows that for a Lie algebroid
A
admitting a bi-invariant metric any bundle of ideals
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
is partially split. Notice that the metric
\eta^{M}
plays here no role.

6.5.
Action Lie algebroids

Let
A=\mathfrak{g}\ltimes M\Rightarrow M
be the action Lie algebroid associated with a Lie algebra action
\rho:\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{X}(M)
. The canonical flat connection
\nabla
on
A
has vanishing basic curvature, hence it is a Cartan connection.
Given a bundle of ideals
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
a splitting
l:A\to\mathfrak{k}
is
\overline{\nabla}
-invariant if and only if it is
\mathfrak{g}
-equivariant:

l([v,w]_{\mathfrak{g}})=[v,l(w)]_{\mathfrak{g}\ltimes M},

for all
v,w\in\mathfrak{g}
(here we identify elements of
\mathfrak{g}
with constant sections of
A
). Hence, we deduce from Corollary
6.3
the infinitesimal version of Proposition
3.1
:

Corollary 6.4
.

A bundle of ideals on an action algebroid
\mathfrak{g}\ltimes M\Rightarrow M
admitting a
\mathfrak{g}
-equivariant splitting is partially split.

For example, if
\mathfrak{g}
is a Lie algebra of compact type then it admits an
\operatorname{ad}
-invariant scalar product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
. Such an inner product defines a fiberwise metric
\eta
on
A
which is
\overline{\nabla}
-invariant. Hence, in this case, any bundle of ideals on
A=\mathfrak{g}\ltimes M
admits a
\mathfrak{g}
-equivariant splitting, so it is partially split.

6.6.
Infinitesimal principal type

Let
A^{\prime}\Rightarrow M
be a transitive Lie algebroid and
B\Rightarrow M
any Lie algebroid. Consider the Lie algebroid fibre product:

A:=A^{\prime}\times_{TM}B:=\{(\alpha,\beta)\,:\,\rho_{A^{\prime}}(\alpha)=\rho_{B}(\beta)\},

where the structure is such that the inclusion in the product
A\hookrightarrow A^{\prime}\times B
is a Lie algebroid morphism. The projection
\phi:=\operatorname{pr}_{B}:A\to B
is a Lie algebroid morphism, which is surjective because we assume
B
to be transitive. The kernel
\mathfrak{k}:=\ker\phi
is then a bundle of ideals which can be identified with
\ker\rho_{A^{\prime}}
via
\operatorname{pr}_{A^{\prime}}
. A bundle of ideals
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
obtained via this construction will be called of
principal type
. This is of course the infinitesimal counterpart of groupoids with bundle of ideals of principal type, discussed in Subsection
3.5
.

Since
A^{\prime}\Rightarrow M
is transitive, as discussed in Subsection
6.3
, a splitting
l_{A^{\prime}}:A^{\prime}\to\mathfrak{k}
of
\rho_{A^{\prime}}
determines an IM connection 1-form
(L_{A^{\prime}},l_{A^{\prime}})\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{IM}}({A^{\prime}},\mathfrak{k})
. Pulling back this connection 1-form to
A
, we obtain a multiplicative
\mathfrak{k}
-valued 1-form

(L:=L_{A^{\prime}}\circ\operatorname{pr}_{A^{\prime}},l:=l_{A^{\prime}}\circ\mathrm{pr}_{A^{\prime}})\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{IM}}(A,\mathfrak{k}),

which satisfies
l|_{\mathfrak{k}}=\mathrm{Id}_{\mathfrak{k}}
. So bundles of ideals of principal type are partially split.

The IM connection 1-form
(L,l)\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{IM}}(A,\mathfrak{k})
can also be described using the fact that the splitting
l_{A^{\prime}}
gives an identification
{A^{\prime}}\simeq TM\oplus\mathfrak{k}
, where the anchor becomes
\operatorname{pr}_{TM}
and the bracket is given by:

[(X,\xi),(Y,\eta)]_{{A^{\prime}}}=([X,Y],\Omega(X,Y)+\nabla_{X}\eta-\nabla_{Y}\xi+[\xi,\eta]_{\mathfrak{k}}),

(6.2)

for all
X,Y\in\mathfrak{X}^{1}(M)
,
\xi,\eta\in\Gamma(\mathfrak{k})
. Here:

-

\Omega
is
C^{\infty}(M)
-bilinear, so that
\Omega\in\Omega^{2}(M;\mathfrak{k})
;

-

\nabla
is a connection on
\mathfrak{k}
preserving
[\cdot,\cdot]_{\mathfrak{k}}
with curvature
R^{\nabla}=\operatorname{ad}(\Omega)
.

Then, one finds that, for
\alpha\in\Gamma(B)
and
\xi\in\Gamma(\mathfrak{k})
:

i_{X}L(\alpha,\xi)=\nabla_{X}(\xi)+\Omega(X,\rho_{B}(\alpha)).

In the notation of Proposition
5.13
, we have that

U(\alpha,X)=\Omega(\rho_{B}(\alpha),X),\qquad\nabla^{L}=\nabla.

Here is a class of bundle of ideals which are of principal type:

Proposition 6.5
.

Let
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
be a bundle of ideals such that:

(i)

\mathfrak{k}
is a locally trivial Lie algebra bundle;

(ii)

the typical fiber
(\mathfrak{g},[\cdot,\cdot])
of
\mathfrak{k}
satisfies:

H^{0}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g})=H^{1}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g})=0.

Then
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
is of principal type, and in particular it is partially split.

Corollary 6.6
.

If
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
is a bundle of semi-simple Lie algebras, then it is of principal type.

Proof.

For a semi-simple Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, we have that
H^{i}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{g})=0
, for all
i
. Also,
\mathfrak{k}
is automatically a locally trivial Lie algebra (see Remark
6.2
), hence Proposition
6.5
applies.
∎

Proof of Proposition
6.5
.

Consider the Lie algebroid
\operatorname{\mathfrak{gl}}(\mathfrak{k})
whose sections are the derivations of the vector bundle
\mathfrak{k}
, i.e., the bundle maps
D_{X}:\Gamma(\mathfrak{k})\to\Gamma(\mathfrak{k})
satisfying:

D_{X}(f\xi)=fD_{X}(\xi)+X(f)\xi,

for all
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
. The anchor of
\operatorname{\mathfrak{gl}}(\mathfrak{k})
assigns to the section
D_{X}
its symbol
X\in\mathfrak{X}(M)
, and the Lie bracket is the commutator of derivations. We let
A^{\prime}\subset\operatorname{\mathfrak{gl}}(\mathfrak{k})
be the Lie subalgebroid whose sections are the derivations of the bracket
[\cdot,\cdot]_{\mathfrak{k}}

\Gamma(A^{\prime})=\mathrm{Der}(\mathfrak{k},[\cdot,\cdot]_{\mathfrak{k}}).

The fact that
A^{\prime}
is a Lie subalgebroid and transitive, follows from (i).

Now, to prove the proposition, let
B=A/\mathfrak{k}
and consider the map

\psi:A\to A^{\prime}\times B,\quad\alpha\mapsto(\nabla^{\mathfrak{k}}_{\alpha},[\alpha]).

We claim that this is an injective Lie algebroid morphism, with image the fiber product
A^{\prime}\times_{TM}B
. That
\psi
is a Lie algebroid morphism follows the fact that both components
\nabla^{\mathfrak{k}}:A\to A^{\prime}
and
\operatorname{pr}:A\to B
are Lie algebroid maps. On the other hand, if
\alpha\in A
is such that
\psi(\alpha)=0
, then
\alpha\in\mathfrak{k}_{x}
satisfies:

\nabla^{\mathfrak{k}}_{\alpha}=[\alpha,-]_{\mathfrak{k}_{x}}=0.

But (ii) is equivalent to
\operatorname{ad}:\mathfrak{k}_{x}\to\operatorname{Der}(\mathfrak{k}_{x})
being an isomorphism of Lie algebras. Injectivity of this maps shows that
\alpha=0
and so
\psi
is injective. Finally, surjectivity of this map implies that the map
\psi
is onto
A^{\prime}\times_{TM}B
.
∎

Remark 6.7
.

As observed in the proof, condition (ii) of Proposition
6.5
is equivalent to
\operatorname{ad}:\mathfrak{g}\to\operatorname{Der}(\mathfrak{g})
being a Lie algebra isomorphism. Therefore,
G:=\operatorname{Aut}(\mathfrak{g})
is a Lie group integrating
\mathfrak{g}
and we have the principal
G
-bundle of
\mathfrak{g}
-frames:

P:=\{\varphi:\mathfrak{g}\to\mathfrak{k}_{x}\,:\,x\in M,\,\varphi\,\textrm{is a Lie algebra isomorphism}\}.

The transitive Lie algebroid
A^{\prime}\to M
in the proof is isomorphic to the Atiyah Lie algebroid of
P
and hence is integrable.

We also have a global version of Proposition
6.5
.

Proposition 6.8
.

Let
\Phi:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{H}
be a groupoid map covering the identity which is a surjective submersion, and let
\phi:A\to B
be the induced Lie algebroid map. If
\mathfrak{k}:=\ker\phi
satisfies the conditions of Proposition
6.5
then
\Phi
is partially split.

Proof.

The assumption implies that

\mathcal{G}^{\prime}:=\big\{\textrm{Lie algebra isomorphisms}\ \ \mathfrak{k}_{x}\xrightarrow{\raisebox{-1.59442pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\smash{$\sim$}}}\mathfrak{k}_{y},\ x,y\in M\big\}\rightrightarrows M

is a transitive Lie groupoid. The map (
2.1
)
\operatorname{Ad}:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
together with the map
\Phi
induce a groupoid map into the fiber product:

\Psi:=(\operatorname{Ad},\Phi):\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\times_{M\times M}\mathcal{H}.

Then
\Psi
integrates the Lie algebroid map
\psi
from the proof of Proposition
6.5
. Since
\psi
is an isomorphism, it follows that
\Psi
is a local diffeomorphism. On the other hand, since
\mathcal{G}^{\prime}
is transitive, it follows that
\operatorname{pr}_{2}:\mathcal{G}^{\prime}\times_{M\times M}\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}
is of principal type, so by the example of Subsection
3.5
,
\operatorname{pr}_{2}
admits a multiplicative Ehresmann connection
E
. The preimage of
E
under
\Psi
is a multiplicative Ehresmann connection for
\Phi
.
∎

6.7.
Bundles of ideals of principal type with kernel flat IM connections

Let
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
be an ideal of principal type, for which the 2-form (
6.2
) is center-valued:

\Omega\in\Omega^{2}(M,z(\mathfrak{k})).

Then the corresponding connection
\nabla
is flat, so we are in the case of an IM Ehresmann connection which is kernel flat. This kind of partially split ideals can be explicitly described as follows.

Let
B\Rightarrow M
be a Lie algebroid and let
\mathfrak{k}\to M
be a Lie algebra bundle with a flat connection
\nabla^{L}
preserving the bracket
[\cdot,\cdot]_{\mathfrak{k}}
. Given any center-valued,
\operatorname{d}^{\nabla^{L}}
-closed, 2-form
\Omega\in\Omega^{2}(M,z(\mathfrak{k}))
one can define a center-valued tensor
U
by:

U(\alpha,X):=\Omega(\rho_{B}(\alpha),X).

One checks that
U
satisfies (S3), so that (S1’)-(S3’) hold. Therefore, we obtain a partially split bundle of ideals
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
with flat coupling data. Moreover, it is of principal type: we have
A\simeq A^{\prime}\times_{TM}B
, where
A^{\prime}=TM\oplus\mathfrak{k}
is the transitive Lie algebroid
with Lie bracket:

[(X,\xi),(Y,\eta)]_{B}=([X,Y],\Omega(X,Y)+\nabla^{L}_{X}\eta-\nabla^{L}_{Y}\xi+[\xi,\eta]_{\mathfrak{k}}).

Note that in this case the
B
-cocycle
\lambda
in Remark
5.20
is given by
(\rho_{B})^{*}(\Omega)
, so its class is the image of the map:

(\rho_{B})^{*}:H^{2}(M,z(\mathfrak{k}))\to H^{2}(B,z(\mathfrak{k})).

When
\Omega=\operatorname{d}^{\nabla_{L}}\theta
, with
\theta\in\Omega^{1}(M,z(\mathfrak{k}))
we have
[\lambda]=0
and the resulting bundle of ideals
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
is isomorphic to a “trivial” coupling data
(\nabla^{L},0)
, via the map:

A\to B\oplus\mathfrak{k},\quad(\alpha,\xi)\mapsto(\alpha,\theta(\alpha)+\xi).

On the other hand, given any partially split bundle of ideals
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
with flat coupling data
(\nabla^{L},U)
and a center-valued,
\operatorname{d}^{\nabla^{L}}
-closed, 2-form
\Omega\in\Omega^{2}(M,z(\mathfrak{k}))
, we obtain a “gauge transformed” bundle of ideals with coupling data
(\nabla^{L},e^{\Omega}U)
where:

e^{\Omega}U(\alpha,X):=U(\alpha,X)+\Omega(\rho_{B}(\alpha),X).

The bracket of the underlying algebroid
B
changes by replacing the cocycle
\lambda
with the cocycle
\lambda+(\rho_{B})^{*}(\Omega)
.

6.8.
Bundles of ideals of rank one

We treat in detail the case of bundle of ideals of rank one. In this setting the existence of specific types of IM connections have cohomological interpretations.

Let
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
be a bundle of ideals of rank one, which, for simplicity, we assume to be orientable. As usual, we set
B=A/\mathfrak{k}
, and after choosing a splitting of the short exact sequence:

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathfrak{k}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

we obtain an identification

A\simeq B\oplus\mathfrak{k},

where the anchor and the Lie bracket on
A
become:

\displaystyle\rho_{A}(\alpha,\xi)=\rho_{B}(\alpha),

\displaystyle[(\alpha,\xi),(\beta,\eta)]_{A}=([\alpha,\beta]_{B},\lambda(\alpha,\beta)+\nabla^{\mathfrak{k}}_{\alpha}\eta-\nabla^{\mathfrak{k}}_{\beta}\xi).

Notice that:

\bullet

The flat
B
-connection
\nabla^{\mathfrak{k}}
is independent of the choice of splitting of
A
;

\bullet

Up to isomorphism, the representation of
B
on
\mathfrak{k}
is determined by its characteristic class (see, e.g., Section 11.1 in
[
11
]
):

c_{1}(\mathfrak{k})\in H^{1}(B).

Explicitly, if we choose a trivialization
\mathfrak{k}\simeq M\times\mathbb{R}
, then

\nabla^{\mathfrak{k}}_{\alpha}f=\mathscr{L}_{\rho_{B}(\alpha)}f+i_{V}(\alpha)f,\quad(f\in C^{\infty}(M))

for a cocycle
V\in\Omega^{1}(B)
, which is a representative of
c_{1}(\mathfrak{k})
. If we change the trivialization
\mathfrak{k}\simeq M\times\mathbb{R}
by multiplying with a non-zero function
h
, then
V
changes by
\rho_{B}^{*}(\operatorname{d}\log|h|)=\operatorname{d}_{B}\log|h|
.

\bullet

The
\mathfrak{k}
-valued 2-form
\lambda\in\Omega^{2}(B;\mathfrak{k})
is
\operatorname{d}_{B}
-closed, so it determines a class:

c_{2}(A):=[\lambda]\in H^{2}(B;\mathfrak{k}).

This class is well-defined: a different splitting of
A
is determined by an element
Z\in\Omega^{1}(B;\mathfrak{k})
, and this has the effect of changing
\lambda
by
\operatorname{d}_{B}Z
.

The anchor
\rho_{B}:B\to TM
, being a Lie algebroid morphism, defines a map in cohomology

\rho_{B}^{*}:H^{1}(M)\longrightarrow H^{1}(B).

The class
c_{1}(\mathfrak{k})
belongs to the image of this map if and only if there exists a flat connection
\nabla
on
\mathfrak{k}
such that, for all
\alpha\in\Gamma(B)
and
\xi\in\Gamma(\mathfrak{k})
:

\nabla^{\mathfrak{k}}_{\alpha}\xi=\nabla_{\rho_{B}(\alpha)}\xi.

(6.3)

To see this, choose a trivialization
\mathfrak{k}\simeq M\times\mathbb{R}
, giving rise to the representative
V
of
c_{1}(\mathfrak{k})
. We have a one-to-one correspondence between flat connections
\nabla
on
M\times\mathbb{R}
and closed forms
\theta\in\Omega^{1}(M)
, given by:

\nabla_{X}f=\mathscr{L}_{X}f+\theta(X)f,\quad(f\in C^{\infty}(M))

Under this correspondence, we have that (
6.3
) is equivalent to
V=\rho_{B}^{*}\theta
. The class

c_{1}(\nabla):=[\theta]\in H^{1}(M).

is just the characteristic class of the representation of
TM
on
\mathfrak{k}
given by
\nabla
, and we have by definition:

\rho_{B}^{*}(c_{1}(\nabla))=c_{1}(\mathfrak{k}).

Below, whenever
c_{1}(\mathfrak{k})
belongs to the image of
\rho^{*}_{B}
, and we choose a flat connection satisfying
6.3
, we will say that “
\nabla
is a flat connection with
\rho_{B}^{*}\nabla=\nabla^{\mathfrak{k}}
”. In particular, when this happens,
\nabla
is a
B
-invariant connection (see Definition
A.9
). Also, we have a map in cohomology with coefficients in
\mathfrak{k}
, for the representations defined by
\nabla
and
\nabla^{\mathfrak{k}}
:

\rho_{B}^{*}:H^{\bullet}(M;\mathfrak{k})\longrightarrow H^{\bullet}(B;\mathfrak{k}),

and another map in IM cohomology, for the differential
\operatorname{d}^{\nabla}_{\mathrm{IM}}
(see Lemma
A.11
):

H^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{IM}}(B;\mathfrak{k})\longrightarrow H^{\bullet}(B;\mathfrak{k}).

After these preliminaries, we can now state a result about the various special IM Ehresmann connections one can have for a bundle of ideals of rank one:

Proposition 6.9
.

Let
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
be a bundle of ideals of rank one, which is orientable.

(i)

The Lie algebroid
A
is isomorphic to a product,
A\simeq B\times\mathbb{R}
, with
\mathfrak{k}\simeq 0_{M}\times\mathbb{R}
(see Subsection
6.2
), if and only if

c_{1}(\mathfrak{k})=0\quad\textrm{and}\quad c_{2}(A)=0.

(ii)

There exists a totally flat IM Ehresmann connection for
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
if and only if

c_{1}(\mathfrak{k})\in\operatorname{Im}(H^{1}(M)\to H^{1}(B))\quad\textrm{and}\quad c_{2}(A)=0.

(iii)

There exists a leafwise flat IM Ehresmann connection for
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
if and only if
\mathfrak{k}
admits a
B
-invariant connection
\nabla
and
c_{2}(A)=0
.

(iv)

There exists a kernel flat IM Ehresmann connection for
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
if and only if

c_{1}(\mathfrak{k})\in\operatorname{Im}(H^{1}(M)\to H^{1}(B))\quad\textrm{and}\quad c_{2}(A)\in\operatorname{Im}(H^{2}_{\mathrm{IM}}(B;\mathfrak{k})\to H^{2}(B;\mathfrak{k})),

where the last map is for a flat connection
\nabla
with
\rho_{B}^{*}\nabla=\nabla^{\mathfrak{k}}
.

(v)

The Lie bundle of ideals
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
is of principal type if and only if

c_{1}(\mathfrak{k})\in\operatorname{Im}(H^{1}(M)\to H^{1}(B))\quad\textrm{and}\quad c_{2}(A)\in\operatorname{Im}(H^{2}(M;\mathfrak{k})\to H^{2}(B;\mathfrak{k})),

where the last map is for a flat connection
\nabla
with
\rho_{B}^{*}\nabla=\nabla^{\mathfrak{k}}
.

Before we look at the proof, let us discuss the general question of existence of partial splittings for ideals of rank one:

Proposition 6.10
.

A rank one bundle of ideals
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
admits an IM Ehresmann connection if and only if:

(i)

\mathfrak{k}
admits a
B
-invariant connection
\nabla^{L}
, i.e., for all
\alpha\in B
:

\nabla^{\mathfrak{k}}_{\alpha}=\nabla^{L}_{\rho_{B}(\alpha)}\quad\textrm{and}\quad i_{\rho_{B}(\alpha)}R^{\nabla^{L}}=0,

(ii)

and
c_{2}(A)
has a representative of the form

\lambda(\alpha,\beta)=i_{\rho_{B}(\beta)}U(\alpha),

where
U:B\to T^{*}M\otimes\mathfrak{k}
is a linear map satisfying the structure equation:

U([\alpha,\beta]_{B})=\mathscr{L}^{\nabla^{L}}_{\rho_{B}(\alpha)}U(\beta)-i_{\rho_{B}(\beta)}\operatorname{d}^{\nabla^{L}}U(\alpha),

for all
\alpha,\beta\in\Gamma(B)
.

Proof.

From the theory of couplings, discussed in Section
5.4
,
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
is partially split if and only if
there exists a pair
(\nabla^{L},U)
and a splitting
A\simeq B\oplus\mathfrak{k}
such that
\nabla^{\mathfrak{k}}_{\alpha}=\nabla^{L}_{\rho_{B}(\alpha)}
,
\lambda(\alpha,\beta)=U(\alpha,\rho_{B}(\beta))
, and the structure equations (S2) and (S3) hold. It follows easily that (S2) is equivalent to invariance of the connection, and that (S3) is equivalent to the equation in (ii) – as was mentioned already in the proof of Proposition
5.19
.
∎

Assume then that we have an IM Ehresmann connection with coupling data
(\nabla^{L},U)
, as in the previous proposition. After fixing a trivialization
\mathfrak{k}\simeq M\times\mathbb{R}
, we can write the connection as

\nabla^{L}_{X}=\mathscr{L}_{X}+\theta(X),

for a unique 1-form
\theta\in\Omega^{1}(M)
. Then
c_{1}(\mathfrak{k})=[V]
where
V\in\Omega^{1}(B)
satisfies:

V(\alpha)=i_{\rho_{B}(\alpha)}\theta.

Moreover, regarding
U
as a map
U:B\to T^{*}M
,
c_{2}(A)=[\lambda]
where
\lambda\in\Omega^{2}(B)
satisfies:

\lambda(\alpha,\beta)=i_{\rho_{B}(\beta)}U(\alpha).

The structure equations from (i) and (ii) become:

\displaystyle i_{\rho_{B}(\alpha)}\operatorname{d}\theta=0,

(S2”)

\displaystyle U([\alpha,\beta]_{B})=\mathscr{L}_{\rho_{B}(\alpha)}U(\beta)-i_{\rho_{B}(\beta)}\operatorname{d}U(\alpha)

(S3”)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad+\theta(\rho_{B}(\alpha))U(\beta)-i_{\rho_{B}(\beta)}\big(\theta\wedge U(\alpha)\big),

for all
\alpha,\beta\in\Gamma(B)
. This follows from the relations:

R^{\nabla^{L}}(X,Y)=\operatorname{d}\theta(X,Y),\quad\operatorname{d}^{\nabla^{L}}\omega=\operatorname{d}\omega+\theta\wedge\omega,\quad\mathscr{L}^{\nabla^{L}}_{X}\omega=\mathscr{L}_{X}\omega+\theta(X)\omega.

Recall that a class
c\in H^{k}(B;V)
is said to be
tangential
if it has some representative
P\in\Omega^{k}(B;V)
satisfying

\rho_{B}(\alpha)=0\quad\Longrightarrow\quad i_{\alpha}P=0.

The previous discussion implies the following obstruction for existence of IM Ehresmann connection:

Corollary 6.11
.

If a rank one bundle of ideals
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
admits an IM Ehresmann connection, then the classes
c_{1}(\mathfrak{k})\in H^{1}(B)
and
c_{2}(A)\in H^{2}(B;\mathfrak{k})
are tangential.

Proof of Proposition
6.9
.

(i) If
A
is isomorphic to a product, then clearly
c_{1}(\mathfrak{k})=0
and
c_{2}(A)=0
. Conversely,
c_{2}(A)=0
means that there exists a splitting
A\simeq B\times\mathfrak{k}
such that
\lambda=0
, and
c_{1}(\mathfrak{k})=0
means that there exists a trivialization
\mathfrak{k}\simeq M\times\mathbb{R}
such that
V=0
. Thus
A
is a product.

(ii) Assume that
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
admits a totally flat IM connection, with coupling data
(\nabla^{L},U=0)
. Then, as remarked above
c_{1}(\mathfrak{k})
belongs to the image of
\rho_{B}^{*}
, and
\lambda=0
, so clearly
c_{2}(A)=0
. Conversely, under the assumptions, there exists a splitting
A\simeq B\oplus\mathfrak{k}
, under which
\lambda=0
and there is a flat connection
\nabla^{L}
on
\mathfrak{k}
inducing
\nabla^{\mathfrak{k}}
. Then
(\nabla^{L},U)
is the coupling data of a totally flat IM connection for
\mathfrak{k}
.

(iii) Assume that
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
admits a leafwise flat IM connection, with coupling data
(\nabla^{L},U)
. This means that
\lambda=0
, so clearly,
c_{2}(A)=0
, and as remarked before
\nabla^{L}
is
B
-invariant. Conversely, under the assumptions, there exists a splitting
A\simeq B\times\mathbb{R}
, under which
\lambda=0
, and there is a connection
\nabla^{L}
which is
B
-invariant. Then the pair
(\nabla^{L},U=0)
is the coupling data of a leafwise flat IM connection for
\mathfrak{k}
.

(iv) Assume that
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
admits a kernel flat IM connection, with coupling data
(\nabla^{L},U)
. This means that
\nabla^{L}
is flat, so
c_{1}(\mathfrak{k})
is in the image of
\rho_{B}^{*}
. By Proposition
5.19
, the pair
(\operatorname{d}^{\nabla^{L}}U,U)
is an IM 2-form on
B
with values in
\mathfrak{k}
, which is clearly closed for the differential
\operatorname{d}_{\mathrm{IM}}^{\nabla^{L}}
from Proposition
A.10
, and which is sent under the map from Lemma
A.11
to
\lambda
. So
c_{2}(A)
it the image of
[\gamma]
. Conversely, the assumptions imply the existence of a flat connection
\nabla^{L}
on
\mathfrak{k}
which is
B
-invariant, and of a splitting
A\simeq B\oplus\mathfrak{k}
, under which
\lambda
is in the image of
\Omega_{\mathrm{IM}}^{2}(B;\mathfrak{k})\to\Omega^{2}(B;\mathfrak{k})
(see Lemma
A.11
) of an element
\gamma
which is
\operatorname{d}^{\nabla^{L}}_{\mathrm{IM}}
-closed. This implies that
\gamma=(\operatorname{d}^{\nabla^{L}}_{\mathrm{IM}}U,U)
. The second part of Proposition
5.19
implies that
(\nabla^{L},U)
is the coupling data of a kernel flat IM connection.

(v) Assume that
\mathfrak{k}\subset A
is of principal type, i.e., there exists a transitive Lie algebroid
\mathfrak{k}\hookrightarrow A^{\prime}\twoheadrightarrow TM
so that
A\simeq A^{\prime}\times_{TM}B
. As discussed in Subsection
6.6
, after choosing a splitting
A^{\prime}\simeq TM\oplus\mathfrak{k}
, the Lie bracket on
A^{\prime}
is given by

[(X,\xi),(Y,\eta)]_{{A^{\prime}}}=([X,Y],\Omega(X,Y)+\nabla_{X}\eta-\nabla_{Y}\xi),

(6.4)

where
\nabla
is a flat connection on
\mathfrak{k}
and
\Omega\in\Omega^{2}(M;\mathfrak{k})
is
\operatorname{d}^{\nabla}
-closed. Using the induced splitting
A\simeq B\oplus\mathfrak{k}
, we obtain that
\nabla^{\mathfrak{k}}_{\alpha}=\nabla_{\rho_{B}(\alpha)}
and
\lambda=\rho_{B}^{*}(\Omega)
; so
c_{1}(\mathfrak{k})
and
c_{2}(A)
are in the image of
\rho^{*}_{B}
. Conversely, if this happens, we have a flat connection
\nabla
on
\mathfrak{k}
such that
\nabla^{\mathfrak{k}}_{\alpha}=\nabla_{\rho_{B}(\alpha)}
, and a
\operatorname{d}^{\nabla}
-closed 2-form
\Omega\in\Omega^{2}(M;\mathfrak{k})
such that
\lambda=\rho_{B}^{*}(\Omega)
, for some splitting. This means precisely that
A\simeq A^{\prime}\times_{TM}B
, where
A^{\prime}=TM\oplus\mathfrak{k}
is the transitive Lie algebroid with bracket (
6.4
).
∎

Appendix A
Multiplicative and infinitesimal multiplicative forms

We recall here a few results about multiplicative and infinitesimal multiplicative (IM) forms that are required throughout the paper. For us, it will be specially important forms with coefficients, since they appear as “connection 1-forms” for Ehresmann connections. More details on the results mentioned in this section can be found in
[
6
,
7
,
10
,
14
,
18
]
and we only provide proofs of the results that cannot be found in those references.

A.1.
Multiplicative forms and IM forms

Let
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows M
be a Lie groupoid with source/target
\mathbf{s},\mathbf{t}:\mathcal{G}\to M
and multiplication
m:\mathcal{G}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22267pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.58762pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-15.29466pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22267pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.58762pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.071pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.5361pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.5361pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.19138pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.56526pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22267pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.58762pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-15.29466pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22267pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.58762pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.071pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.5361pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.5361pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.19138pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.56526pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22267pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.58762pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-10.82358pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22267pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.58762pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 5.6321pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.5361pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.5361pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.19138pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.56526pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 5.6321pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22267pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.58762pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-9.7347pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22267pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.58762pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.54321pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.5361pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.5361pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.19138pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.56526pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 4.54321pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}
.

Definition A.1
.

A differential form
\omega\in\Omega^{k}(\mathcal{G})
is called
multiplicative
if it lies in the kernel of the simplicial differential:

\delta\omega:=\operatorname{pr}_{1}^{*}\omega-m^{*}\omega+\operatorname{pr}_{2}^{*}\omega=0

where
\operatorname{pr}_{i}:\mathcal{G}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22267pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.58762pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-15.29466pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22267pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.58762pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.071pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.5361pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.5361pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.19138pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.56526pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22267pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.58762pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-15.29466pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22267pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.58762pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.071pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.5361pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.5361pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.19138pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.56526pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22267pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.58762pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-10.82358pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22267pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.58762pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 5.6321pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.5361pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.5361pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.19138pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.56526pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 5.6321pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22267pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.58762pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-9.7347pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22267pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.58762pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.54321pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.5361pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.5361pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.19138pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.56526pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 4.54321pt{\mathbf{t}}}}}\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}
are the projections on the factors.

The differential of a multiplicative form is again a multiplicative form, so we have a complex of multiplicative differential forms
(\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathcal{G}),\operatorname{d})
. A basic fact is that two multiplicative forms
\omega,\omega^{\prime}\in\Omega^{k}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathcal{G})
such that
\omega|_{M}=\omega^{\prime}|_{M}
and
(\operatorname{d}\omega)|_{M}=(\operatorname{d}\omega^{\prime})|_{M}
actually coincide, assuming that
\mathcal{G}
has connected target fibers.

Denote by
(A,[\cdot,\cdot],\rho)
the Lie algebroid of
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows M
. Given a multiplicative form
\omega\in\Omega^{k}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathcal{G})
one defines two vector bundle maps
\mu:A\to\wedge^{k-1}T^{*}M
and
\zeta:A\to\wedge^{k}T^{*}M
by setting:

\mu(a)=i_{a}\omega|_{TM},\quad\zeta(a)=i_{a}\operatorname{d}\omega|_{TM}.

(A.1)

The pair
(\mu,\zeta)
satisfies the following set of equations for any
\alpha,\beta\in\Gamma(A)
:

\displaystyle i_{\rho(\beta)}\mu(\alpha)

\displaystyle=-i_{\rho(\alpha)}\mu(\beta),

\displaystyle\mu([\alpha,\beta])

\displaystyle=\mathscr{L}_{\rho(\alpha)}\mu(\beta)-i_{\rho(\beta)}\operatorname{d}\mu(\alpha)-i_{\rho(\beta)}\zeta(\alpha),

(A.2)

\displaystyle\zeta([\alpha,\beta])

\displaystyle=\mathscr{L}_{\rho(\alpha)}\zeta(\beta)-i_{\rho(\beta)}\operatorname{d}\zeta(\alpha).

This leads to the notion of
infinitesimal multiplicative form
on an arbitrary Lie algebroid
A\Rightarrow M
, integrable or not:

Definition A.2
.

An
IM
k
-form
on a Lie algebroid
(A,[\cdot,\cdot],\rho)
is a pair
(\mu,\zeta)
, where
\mu:A\to\wedge^{k-1}T^{*}M
and
\zeta:A\to\wedge^{k}T^{*}M
are bundle maps satisfying (
A.1
).

The space of IM forms is denoted by
\Omega_{\mathrm{IM}}^{k}(A)
and it becomes a cochain complex with differential given by

\operatorname{d}_{\mathrm{IM}}:\Omega_{\mathrm{IM}}^{k}(A)\to\Omega_{\mathrm{IM}}^{k+1}(A),\quad\operatorname{d}_{\mathrm{IM}}(\mu,\zeta):=(\zeta,0).

For a
target 1-connected
Lie groupoid
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows M
with Lie algebroid
A\Rightarrow M
the assignment
\omega\mapsto(\mu,\zeta)
given by (
A.1
) is an isomorphism of complexes:

(\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathcal{G}),\operatorname{d})\simeq(\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{IM}}(A),\operatorname{d}_{\mathrm{IM}}).

A.2.
Multiplicative forms and IM forms with coefficients

This subsection follows
[
14
]
, where all claims are proven. Let
\mathcal{G}
be a Lie groupoid and
V
a
\mathcal{G}
-representation. We will work with differential forms on
\mathcal{G}
with coefficients in
\mathbf{s}^{*}V
(instead of
\mathbf{t}^{*}V
, as in
[
14
]
), which we denote by

\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{G};V):=\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{G};\mathbf{s}^{*}V).

Similarly, denote by
\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{G}^{(k)};V)
the space of differential forms on the manifold
\mathcal{G}^{(k)}
of composable
k
-strings of arrows, with values in the vector bundle
(\mathbf{s}\circ\operatorname{pr}_{k})^{*}(V)\to\mathcal{G}^{(k)}
, where
\operatorname{pr}_{i}:\mathcal{G}^{(k)}\to\mathcal{G}
is the projection onto the
i
-th component.

Definition A.3
.

A form
\omega\in\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{G};V)
is called
multiplicative
if

\delta\omega=0,

where
\delta:\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{G};V)\to\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{G}^{(2)};V)

is the simplicial differential defined by:

\delta\omega|_{(g_{1},g_{2})}=g_{2}^{-1}\cdot\operatorname{pr}_{1}^{*}\omega|_{g_{1}}-m^{*}\omega|_{g_{1}g_{2}}+\operatorname{pr}_{2}^{*}\omega|_{g_{2}}.

(A.3)

We denote by
\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathcal{G};V)
the space of
V
-valued multiplicative
k
-forms.

A simple way to produce multiplicative forms is by using the simplicial differential in degree zero
\delta:\Omega^{\bullet}(M;V)\to\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathcal{G};V)
given by:

\delta\omega|_{g}:=g^{-1}\cdot\mathbf{t}^{*}\omega-\mathbf{s}^{*}\omega,\quad g\in\mathcal{G}.

(A.4)

We will see later other examples.

Let us pass to the infinitesimal level, so denote by
A\Rightarrow M
the Lie algebroid of
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows M
. Given a representation
p:V\to M
of
\mathcal{G}
, at the algebroid level we obtain a
flat
A
-connection
on
V
:

\nabla:\Gamma(A)\times\Gamma(V)\to\Gamma(V),\quad(\alpha,s)\mapsto\nabla_{\alpha}s.

This means that for all
\alpha,\beta\in\Gamma(A)
,
s\in\Gamma(V)
, and
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
, we have:

\nabla_{f\alpha}s=f\nabla_{\alpha}s,\ \ \ \nabla_{\alpha}(fs)=f\nabla_{\alpha}s+(\mathscr{L}_{\rho(\alpha)}f)s,\ \ \ \nabla_{[\alpha,\beta]}=[\nabla_{\alpha},\nabla_{\beta}].

We also call
(V,\nabla)
a
representation
of the Lie algebroid
A
.

For a
V
-valued multiplicative
k
-form
\omega\in\Omega^{k}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathcal{G};V)
, define a vector bundle map
l:A\to\wedge^{k-1}T^{*}M\otimes V
by setting

l(a):=(i_{a}\omega)|_{TM},

(A.5)

and a linear operator
L:\Gamma(A)\to\Omega^{k}(M;V)
by letting

L(\alpha)_{x}(v_{1},\dots,v_{k}):=\frac{\operatorname{d}}{\operatorname{d}\epsilon}\Big|_{\epsilon=0}\phi_{\alpha^{L}}^{\epsilon}(x)\cdot\omega(\operatorname{d}_{x}\phi_{\alpha^{L}}^{\epsilon}(v_{1}),\dots,\operatorname{d}_{x}\phi_{\alpha^{L}}^{\epsilon}(v_{k}))

(A.6)

where
\phi_{\alpha^{L}}^{\epsilon}
denotes the flow of the left-invariant vector field
\alpha^{L}
.
This operator is a kind of differential operator with symbol
l
, in the sense that for any
f\in C^{\infty}(M)
and
\alpha\in\Gamma(A)
it satisfies:

L(f\alpha)=fL(\alpha)+\operatorname{d}f\wedge l(\alpha).

(A.7)

Furthermore, the pair
(L,l)
satisfies the following set of equations:

\displaystyle i_{\rho(\alpha)}l(\beta)

\displaystyle=-i_{\rho(\beta)}l(\alpha),

\displaystyle L([\alpha,\beta])

\displaystyle=\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}L(\beta)-\mathscr{L}_{\beta}L(\alpha),

(A.8)

\displaystyle l([\alpha,\beta])

\displaystyle=\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}l(\beta)-i_{\rho(\beta)}L(\alpha),

where, for
\alpha\in\Gamma(A)
and
\gamma\in\Omega^{k}(M;V)
, we denoted:

\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}\gamma(X_{1},\dots,X_{k}):=\nabla_{\alpha}(\gamma(X_{1},\dots,X_{k}))-\sum_{i=1}^{k}\gamma(X_{1},\dots,[\rho(\alpha),X_{i}],\dots,X_{k}).

(A.9)

Now observe that all this makes sense for an arbitrary algebroid
A\Rightarrow M
, integrable or not, provided one has a representation
(V,\nabla)
:

Definition A.4
.

Let
(V,\nabla)
be a representation of the Lie algebroid
A\Rightarrow M
. An
V
-valued IM
k
-form
is a pair
(L,l)
, where
L:\Gamma(A)\to\Omega^{k}(M,V)
is a linear map and
l:A\to\wedge^{k-1}T^{*}M\otimes V
is a vector bundle map satisfying (
A.7
) and (
A.2
).

We denote by
\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{IM}}(A;V)
the space of
V
-valued IM
k
-forms. For historical reasons, these are also sometime called
Spencer operators
. For a
target 1-connected
Lie groupoid
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows M
with Lie algebroid
A\Rightarrow M
the assignment
\omega\mapsto(L,l)
given by (
A.5
) and (
A.6
) gives an isomorphism:

\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathcal{G};V)\simeq\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{IM}}(A;V).

Example A.5

(
V
-valued forms of degree 1)
.

For us the
V
-valued multiplicative forms of degree 1 will be important. Such a form
\omega\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathcal{G};V)
is the same thing as a morphism of VB groupoids
\omega:T\mathcal{G}\to\mathbf{s}^{*}V=\mathcal{G}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22267pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.58762pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-15.29466pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22267pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.58762pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.071pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.46532pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.76094pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{p}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22267pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.58762pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-15.29466pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22267pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.58762pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.071pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.46532pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.76094pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{p}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22267pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.58762pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-10.82358pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22267pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.58762pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 5.6321pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.46532pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.76094pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 5.6321pt{p}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22267pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.58762pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-9.7347pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22267pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.58762pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.54321pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.46532pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.76094pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 4.54321pt{p}}}}V
covering the identity morphism
\mathrm{Id}:\mathcal{G}\to\mathcal{G}
. Let us explain this in more detail.

First, the tangent bundle of a Lie groupoid
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows M
is a VB-groupoid:

\textstyle{T\mathcal{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TM\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M}

Next, if
p:V\to M
is a representation of
\mathcal{G}
, then we have the groupoid extension
\mathcal{G}\times_{M}V:=\mathcal{G}\mathchoice{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22267pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.58762pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-15.29466pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22267pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.58762pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.071pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.46532pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.76094pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{p}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22267pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.58762pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-15.29466pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22267pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.58762pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 8.071pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.46532pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.76094pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-10.40479pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 8.071pt{p}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22267pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.58762pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-10.82358pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22267pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.58762pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 5.6321pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.46532pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.76094pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-7.33083pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 5.6321pt{p}}}}{\hphantom{{}^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22267pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.58762pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{\mathbf{s}}}}}\times^{{\kern-9.7347pt\mathchoice{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.58582pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.22267pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.58762pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\kern 4.54321pt\mathchoice{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.12234pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.46532pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.76094pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\kern-6.24194pt{\mathbf{s}}\kern 4.54321pt{p}}}}V\rightrightarrows M
, where composition of arrows is given by:

(g,v)\cdot(h,w)=(gh,h^{-1}\cdot v+w).

It can also be viewed as a VB-groupoid:

\textstyle{\mathcal{G}\times_{M}V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0_{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M}

Now an
V
-valued multiplicative differential form
\omega\in\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathcal{G};V)
is just a map:

\omega:T\mathcal{G}\to\mathbf{s}^{*}V=\mathcal{G}\times_{M}V,\quad v_{g}\mapsto(g,\omega(v_{g})),

which is a morphism of VB groupoids covering the identity. In other words, it is a groupoid morphism, which is a vector bundle map making the diagram commute:

\textstyle{T\mathcal{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\omega}
\textstyle{\mathcal{G}\times_{M}V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Id}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TM\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0_{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M}

A similar discussion holds at the infinitesimal level for
V
-valued IM forms of degree 1: a 1-form
(L,l)\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{IM}}(A;V)
is the same thing as a VB algebroid morphism
\theta^{\vee}:TA\to A\oplus V
covering the identity morphism
\mathrm{Id}:A\to A
, as we now explain.

First, the tangent bundle of a Lie algebroid
A\Rightarrow M
is a VB-algebroid:

\textstyle{TA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TM\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M}

where the double arrow is used to distinguish the algebroid projections from the algebroid morphisms. Next, given a representation
(V,\nabla)
of
A
, then we have the algebroid extension
A\oplus V
with anchor
\rho\circ\operatorname{pr}_{A}
and bracket:

[(\alpha,s),(\beta,t)]=([\alpha,\beta],\nabla_{\alpha}t-\nabla_{\beta}s).

It can also be viewed as a VB-algebroid:

\textstyle{A\oplus V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0_{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M}

Now, given a morphism of VB algebroids
\theta^{\vee}:TA\to A\oplus V
covering the identity morphism
\mathrm{Id}:A\to A
:

\textstyle{TA\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta^{\vee}}
\textstyle{A\oplus V\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{Id}}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{TM\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0_{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M}

we can define an
V
-valued IM form
(L,l)\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{IM}}(A,V)
by setting for any section
\alpha\in\Gamma(A)
and
v\in TM
:

\displaystyle L(\alpha)(v)

\displaystyle:=\operatorname{pr}_{V}(\theta^{\vee}(\operatorname{d}\alpha(v))),

\displaystyle l(\alpha)

\displaystyle:=\operatorname{pr}_{V}\theta^{\vee}(\hat{\alpha})|_{M},

where
\hat{\alpha}\in\mathfrak{X}(A)
is the vertical lift of the section
\alpha
. Conversely, given an
V
-valued IM form
(L,l)\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{IM}}(A;V)
there exists a unique VB algebroid morphism
\theta^{\vee}:TA\to A\oplus V
which covers the identity and corresponds to
(L,l)
under the above assignment.

A.3.
Differentiation of multiplicative forms with coefficients

In this section we discuss differentiation of multiplicative forms with coefficients. The material here seems to be new, so we include detailed proofs.

On a manifold
M
, in order to be able to differentiate forms with coefficients in a vector bundle
p:V\to M
one needs to choose a connection
\nabla
. This gives a
\nabla
-de Rham operator
\operatorname{d}^{\nabla}:\Omega^{k}(M;V)\to\Omega^{k+1}(M;V)
defined by

\displaystyle\operatorname{d}^{\nabla}\omega(X_{0},\dots,X_{k})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{i}(-1)^{i}\nabla_{X_{i}}\omega(x_{0},\dots,\widehat{X_{i}},\dots,X_{k})+

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{i<j}(-1)^{i+j}\omega([X_{i},X_{j}],X_{0},\dots,\widehat{X_{i}},\dots,\widehat{X_{j}},\dots,X_{k}).

Note that
\operatorname{d}^{\nabla}
is a differential if and only if
\nabla
is flat.

Now, consider a representation
p:V\to M
of a Lie groupoid
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows M
, with Lie algebroid
A\Rightarrow M
. If we fix a connection
\nabla
on
V
, we can take the pullback connection
\nabla^{\mathbf{s}}
on
\mathbf{s}^{*}V\to\mathcal{G}
and use it to differentiate
V
-valued forms. However, in general, given a multiplicative form
\omega\in\Omega^{k}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathcal{G};V)
, its differential
\operatorname{d}^{\nabla^{\mathbf{s}}}\omega
will not be a multiplicative form. We need the connection
\nabla
to be compatible with the representation.

Definition A.6
.

Let
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows M
be a Lie groupoid and
p:V\to M
a
\mathcal{G}
-representation. A (usual) connection
\nabla
on
V
is called
\mathcal{G}
-invariant
if the isomorphism of vector bundles:

\mathbf{s}^{*}V\to\mathbf{t}^{*}V,\quad(g,v)\mapsto(g,g\cdot v),

preserves the connections
\nabla^{\mathbf{s}}:=\mathbf{s}^{*}\nabla
and
\nabla^{\mathbf{t}}:=\mathbf{t}^{*}\nabla
.

The
\mathcal{G}
-invariance of a connection is equivalent to the commutation relation:

\operatorname{d}^{\nabla^{\mathbf{s}}}\delta=\delta\,\operatorname{d}^{\nabla},

where
\delta:\Omega^{\bullet}(M;V)\to\Omega^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{M}}(\mathcal{G};V)
is the degree zero simplicial differential (
A.4
). It turns out that this is equivalent to require this commutation relation to hold for any simplicial degree. We only need the case of degree 1:

Proposition A.7
.

Let
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows M
be a Lie groupoid, let
p:V\to M
be a
\mathcal{G}
-representation, and let
\nabla
be a connection on
V
. We denote by
\nabla^{\mathbf{s}}
both the pullback connections on
\mathbf{s}^{*}V\to\mathcal{G}
and on
(\mathbf{s}\circ\operatorname{pr}_{2})^{*}V\to\mathcal{G}^{(2)}
. The
\nabla^{\mathbf{s}}
-de Rham differential
\operatorname{d}^{\nabla^{\mathbf{s}}}
and the simplicial differential (
A.3
) commute:

\operatorname{d}^{\nabla^{\mathbf{s}}}\delta=\delta\,\operatorname{d}^{\nabla^{\mathbf{s}}},

if and only if the connection
\nabla
is
\mathcal{G}
-invariant. In particular, if this is the case, then
\operatorname{d}^{\nabla^{\mathbf{s}}}
maps multiplicative forms to multiplicative forms.

Proof.

Let
E\to M
be a vector bundle equipped with a linear connection
\nabla
. Given a map
\phi:N\to M
, denote by
\nabla^{\phi}
the pullback connection on
\phi^{*}V
. Then:

\operatorname{d}^{\nabla^{\phi}}\phi^{*}=\phi^{*}\operatorname{d}^{\nabla}.

Now, we can write the simplicial differential with coefficients as:

\delta=\Phi^{*}\operatorname{pr}_{1}^{*}-m^{*}+\operatorname{pr}_{2}^{*},

where
\Phi
is the vector bundle isomorphism covering the identity:

\Phi:(\mathbf{s}\circ\operatorname{pr}_{1})^{*}V=(\mathbf{t}\circ\operatorname{pr}_{2})^{*}V\to(\mathbf{s}\circ\operatorname{pr}_{2})^{*}V,\quad(g,h,v)\mapsto(g,h,h^{-1}v).

The condition that the connection
\nabla
is
\mathcal{G}
-invariant is easily seen to be equivalent to this map preserving the pullback connections:

(\mathbf{t}\circ\operatorname{pr}_{2})^{*}\nabla=\operatorname{pr}_{2}^{*}\nabla^{\mathbf{t}},\quad(\mathbf{s}\circ\operatorname{pr}_{2})^{*}\nabla=\operatorname{pr}_{2}^{*}\nabla^{\mathbf{s}}.

This condition, in turn, is equivalent to:

\Phi^{*}\operatorname{pr}_{1}^{*}\operatorname{d}^{\nabla}=\operatorname{d}^{\nabla^{\mathbf{s}}}\Phi^{*}\operatorname{pr}_{1}^{*}.

Hence, we conclude that
\nabla
is
\mathcal{G}
-invariant if and only if
\operatorname{d}^{\nabla^{\mathbf{s}}}\delta=\delta\,\operatorname{d}^{\nabla^{\mathbf{s}}}
.
∎

We now pass to infinitesimal level. First we deduce the infinitesimal analogue of the
\mathcal{G}
-invariance condition on a connection:

Proposition A.8
.

Let
\mathcal{G}\rightrightarrows M
be an
\mathbf{t}
-connected Lie groupoid with Lie algebroid
A\Rightarrow M
and let
p:V\to M
be a
\mathcal{G}
-representation. Denote by
\nabla^{A}
the corresponding flat
A
-connection on
V
. A connection
\nabla
is
\mathcal{G}
-invariant if and only if

\nabla^{A}_{a}=\nabla_{\rho(a)}\quad\textrm{and}\quad R^{\nabla}(\rho(a),v)=0,

(A.10)

for all
a\in A_{x}
,
v\in T_{x}M
, and
x\in M
.

Proof.

We claim that
\mathcal{G}
-invariance of
\nabla
is equivalent to the statement that for any path
g(t)\in\mathcal{G}
, one has a commutative diagram:

\textstyle{V_{\mathbf{s}(g(0))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau^{\nabla}_{\mathbf{s}(g(t))}}
\scriptstyle{g(0)}
\textstyle{V_{\mathbf{s}(g(t))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g(t)}
\textstyle{V_{\mathbf{t}(g(0))}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau^{\nabla}_{\mathbf{t}(g(t))}}
\textstyle{V_{\mathbf{t}(g(t))}}

(A.11)

where
\tau^{\nabla}
denotes parallel transport for the connection
\nabla
. To see this note that the
\mathcal{G}
-invariance condition on
\nabla
is equivalent to require the map
\Phi:\mathbf{s}^{*}V\to\mathbf{t}^{*}V
,
(g,v)\mapsto(g,g\cdot v)
, to preserve parallel transport along any curve
g(t)
:

\Phi\circ\tau^{\nabla^{\mathbf{s}}}_{g(t)}=\tau^{\nabla^{\mathbf{t}}}_{g(t)}.

The claim now follows by observing that since the connections are obtained by pulling back
\nabla
along
\mathbf{s}
and
\mathbf{t}
, we have:

\tau^{\nabla^{\mathbf{s}}}_{g(t)}=(g(t),\tau^{\nabla}_{\mathbf{s}(g(t))}),\qquad\tau^{\nabla^{\mathbf{t}}}_{g(t)}=(g(t),\tau^{\nabla}_{\mathbf{t}(g(t))}).

Fix
g\in\mathcal{G}
, and let
x:=\mathbf{t}(g)
. Since
\mathcal{G}
is
\mathbf{t}
-connected we can choose a path
g(\varepsilon)
in the target fiber
\mathbf{t}^{-1}(x)
with
g(0)=1_{x}
and
g(1)=g
. Then we have the
A
-path
a:[0,1]\to T^{*}M
,
a(\varepsilon):=g^{-1}(\varepsilon)g^{\prime}(\varepsilon)
, and parallel transport along
a(\varepsilon)
for the
A
-connection
\nabla^{A}
amounts to acting by the inverse of
g
:

\tau_{a}^{\nabla^{A}}:V_{\mathbf{t}(g)}\to V_{\mathbf{s}(g)},\quad\tau_{a}^{\nabla^{A}}(v)=g^{-1}v.

We prove the proposition by showing the equivalence between
\mathcal{G}
-invariance in the form of the diagram (
A.11
) and conditions (
A.10
). If we assume
\mathcal{G}
-invariance, then given any
a\in A_{x}
we choose a path
g(\varepsilon)
in the target fiber
\mathbf{t}^{-1}(x)
such that
g(0)=1_{x}
and
g^{\prime}(0)=a
. Then the
A
-path
a(\varepsilon):=g^{-1}(\varepsilon)g^{\prime}(\varepsilon)
satisfies

\tau^{\nabla^{A}}_{a}=\tau^{\nabla}_{\mathbf{s}\circ g}.

Since
\mathbf{s}\circ g
is the base path of
a
, by differentiating at
t=0
, we obtain:

\nabla^{A}_{a}=\nabla_{\rho(a)}.

Next, given
a\in A_{x}
and
v\in T_{x}M
we choose a smooth family of paths
g(t,\varepsilon)
where for each fixed
t
the path
\varepsilon\mapsto g(t,\varepsilon)
lies in the target fiber
\mathbf{t}^{-1}(x(t))
and starts at an identity,
g(t,0)=1_{x(t)}
, and moreover:

\frac{\operatorname{d}g}{\operatorname{d}\varepsilon}(0,0)=a,\quad\frac{\operatorname{d}}{\operatorname{d}t}(\mathbf{s}\circ g)(0,0)=v.

It follows from diagram (
A.11
) that for the family
\gamma(t,\varepsilon):=\mathbf{s}(g(t,\varepsilon))
the
\nabla
