-parallel transports commute:

\tau^{\nabla}_{\varepsilon\mapsto\gamma(t,\varepsilon)}\circ\tau^{\nabla}_{t\mapsto\gamma(t,0)}=\tau^{\nabla}_{t\mapsto\gamma(t,\varepsilon)}\circ\tau^{\nabla}_{\varepsilon\mapsto\gamma(0,\varepsilon)}.

By differentiation, this gives:

R^{\nabla}(\rho(a),v)=R^{\nabla}\Big(\frac{\operatorname{d}\gamma}{\operatorname{d}\varepsilon}(0,0),\frac{\operatorname{d}\gamma}{\operatorname{d}t}(0,0)\Big)=0.

This argument can be reversed, to prove the converse. If the curvature condition in (
A.10
) holds for all
x\in M
,
a\in A_{x}
and
v\in T_{x}M
, then one obtains that for any smooth family of paths
g(t,\varepsilon)
, where for each fixed
t
the path
\varepsilon\mapsto g(t,\varepsilon)
lies in a target fiber, the
\nabla
-parallel transports commute:

\tau^{\nabla}_{\varepsilon\mapsto\gamma(t,\varepsilon)}\circ\tau^{\nabla}_{t\mapsto\gamma(t,0)}=\tau^{\nabla}_{t\mapsto\gamma(t,\varepsilon)}\circ\tau^{\nabla}_{\varepsilon\mapsto\gamma(0,\varepsilon)},

where
\gamma(t,\varepsilon):=\mathbf{s}(g(t,\varepsilon))
. Then if the relationship between connections in (
A.10
) holds, we conclude that (
A.11
) must hold for the path
g(t):=g(t,1)
. Since one can obtain any path
g(t)
in
\mathcal{G}
in this way, the result follows.
∎

This motivates the following:

Definition A.9
.

Let
A\Rightarrow M
be a Lie algebroid and let
(V,\nabla^{A})
be an
A
-representation. We call a (usual) connection
\nabla
on
V

A
-invariant
if (
A.10
) holds.

In the presence of an invariant connection we have an operator on IM-forms, which is the infinitesimal version of the operator on multiplicative forms that we saw before:

Proposition A.10
.

Let
A\Rightarrow M
be a Lie algebroid and
(V,\nabla^{A})
an
A
-representation. An
A
-invariant connection
\nabla
on
V
induces operator on IM-forms, as follows:

\operatorname{d}_{\mathrm{IM}}^{\nabla}:\Omega^{k}_{\mathrm{IM}}(A,V)\to\Omega^{k+1}_{\mathrm{IM}}(A,V),\quad(L,l)\mapsto(\operatorname{d}^{\nabla}L,L-\operatorname{d}^{\nabla}l).

If
\nabla
is flat, this operator is a differential.

Proof.

If
\nabla^{A}_{a}=\nabla_{\rho(a)}
then we obtain:

\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}=\mathscr{L}^{\nabla}_{\rho(\alpha)}.

On the other, this together with the condition
R^{\nabla}(\rho(a),v)=0
, gives:

\displaystyle\operatorname{d}^{\nabla}\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}-\mathscr{L}_{\alpha}\operatorname{d}^{\nabla}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{d}^{\nabla}\mathscr{L}^{\nabla}_{\rho(\alpha)}-\mathscr{L}^{\nabla}_{\rho(\alpha)}\operatorname{d}^{\nabla}

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{d}^{\nabla})^{2}i_{\rho(\alpha)}-i_{\rho(\alpha)}(\operatorname{d}^{\nabla})^{2}

\displaystyle=R^{\nabla}\wedge(i_{\rho(\alpha)}\,\cdot)-i_{\rho(\alpha)}(R^{\nabla}\wedge\cdot)=-(i_{\rho(\alpha)}R^{\nabla})\wedge\cdot=0.

It follows that if
(L,l)
satisfies the IM conditions (
A.2
), so does
(\operatorname{d}^{\nabla}L,L-\operatorname{d}^{\nabla}l)
.
∎

There is also a natural map:

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mathrm{IM}}^{k}(A,V)\to\,

\displaystyle\Omega^{k}(A,V),\quad(L,l)\mapsto\omega_{l}

(A.12)

\displaystyle\text{where}\quad\omega_{l}(\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{k}):=l(\alpha_{1})(\rho(\alpha_{2}),\dots,\rho(\alpha_{k})).

The corresponding operation on the Lie groupoid level is the restriction of multiplicative forms to the target fibers. It is immediate to check:

Lemma A.11
.

Let
A\Rightarrow M
be a Lie algebroid and
(V,\nabla^{A})
an
A
-representation. If
\nabla
is an
A
-invariant connection then (
A.12
) intertwines the differentials:

(\Omega_{\mathrm{IM}}^{\bullet}(A,V),\operatorname{d}^{\nabla}_{\mathrm{IM}})\to(\Omega^{\bullet}(A,V),\operatorname{d}^{\nabla^{A}}).

Hence, when
\nabla
is flat we obtain a map of complexes, and so a map in cohomology:

H^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{IM}}(A,V)\to H^{\bullet}(A,V).

(A.13)

Remark A.12
.

In the flat case we also obtain a alternative way of expressing IM-forms with coefficients in
V
, which is similar to (in fact extends) the case of trivial coefficients. Namely, an
A
-invariant connection
\nabla
gives a 1:1 correspondence between maps
L:\Gamma(A)\to\Omega^{k}(M;V)
with symbol
l:A\to\wedge^{k-1}T^{*}M\otimes V
and pairs of tensors
(\mu,\zeta)
, where
\mu:A\to\wedge^{k-1}T^{*}M\otimes V
and
\zeta:A\to\wedge^{k}T^{*}M\otimes V
, by setting:

\mu:=l,\quad\zeta:=L-\operatorname{d}^{\nabla}l.

When
\nabla
is flat, one checks that
(L,l)
is an IM-form if and only if the pair
(\mu,\zeta)
satisfies the
\nabla
-analogue of equations (
A.1
), namely:

\displaystyle i_{\rho(\beta)}\mu(\alpha)

\displaystyle=-i_{\rho(\alpha)}\mu(\beta),

\displaystyle\mu([\alpha,\beta])

\displaystyle=\mathscr{L}^{\nabla}_{\rho(\alpha)}\mu(\beta)-i_{\rho(\beta)}\operatorname{d}^{\nabla}\mu(\alpha)-i_{\rho(\beta)}\zeta(\alpha),

\displaystyle\zeta([\alpha,\beta])

\displaystyle=\mathscr{L}^{\nabla}_{\rho(\alpha)}\zeta(\beta)-i_{\rho(\beta)}\operatorname{d}^{\nabla}\zeta(\alpha).

A.4.
Linear forms

A differential form
\omega\in\Omega^{k}(F)
on the total space of a vector bundle
F\to M
is called
linear
if

m_{t}^{*}\omega=t\,\omega,\quad\forall t>0.

where
m_{t}:F\to F
denotes fiberwise multiplication by
t
. Let
\Omega_{\mathrm{lin}}^{k}(F)
the space of linear
k
-forms on
F
.

To any vector bundle map
\Theta:F\to\wedge^{k}T^{*}M
covering the identity, one can associate a linear
k
-form:

\Theta^{*}(\alpha^{k}_{\mathrm{can}})\in\Omega^{k}_{\mathrm{lin}}(F),

where
\alpha^{k}_{\mathrm{can}}\in\Omega^{k}(\wedge^{k}T^{*}M)
is the tautological
k
-form:

\alpha^{k}_{\mathrm{can}}|_{\xi}:=\operatorname{pr}_{\wedge^{k}T^{*}M}^{*}(\xi).

We have the following decomposition of linear forms:

Lemma A.13
.

Any linear
k
-form
\omega\in\Omega^{k}_{\mathrm{lin}}(F)
can be decomposed as:

\omega=\Xi^{*}(\alpha^{k}_{\mathrm{can}})+\operatorname{d}\Theta^{*}(\alpha^{k-1}_{\mathrm{can}}),

for unique linear maps

\Xi:F\to\wedge^{k}T^{*}M,\quad\Theta:F\to\wedge^{k-1}T^{*}M.

Moreover,
\omega
is closed if and only if
\omega=\operatorname{d}\Theta^{*}(\alpha^{k-1}_{\mathrm{can}})
, i.e., if and only if
\Xi\equiv 0
.

We have the corresponding notion on a VB groupoid:

Definition A.14
.

Given a VB groupoid:

\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M}

a form
\omega\in\Omega^{k}_{\mathrm{M}}(F)
is called a
linear multiplicative form
if it is multiplicative for the groupoid
F\rightrightarrows E
and linear for the vector bundle
F\to\mathcal{G}
.

One important example is the canonical symplectic form
\omega_{\mathrm{can}}\in\Omega^{2}_{\mathrm{M}}(T^{*}\mathcal{G})
on the cotangent VB groupoid

\textstyle{T^{*}\mathcal{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{G}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M}

where
A
denotes the Lie algebroid of
\mathcal{G}
. The canonical primitive of
\omega_{\mathrm{can}}
, i.e., the Liouville 1-form
\alpha_{\mathrm{can}}^{1}\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{M}}(T^{*}\mathcal{G})
, is also a linear multiplicative 1-form.

We now turn to the infinitesimal version of these results.

Definition A.15
.

A
linear IM form
on a VB algebroid

\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M}

is an IM form
(\mu,\zeta)\in\Omega^{k}_{\mathrm{IM}}(B)
on the algebroid
B\to E
satisfying

m_{t}^{*}\mu=t\,\mu,\quad m_{t}^{*}\zeta=t\,\zeta,\quad\forall t>0,

where
m_{t}:B\to B
is the scalar multiplication on the vector bundle
B\to A
.

Under the correspondence between multiplicative forms and IM forms, linear multiplicative forms on a VB groupoid correspond to linear IM forms on the associated VB algebroid.

For any Lie algebroid
A\to M
, its cotangent algebroid is a VB algebroid:

\textstyle{T^{*}A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{M}

and carries a canonical closed, linear, multiplicative 2-form
\mu_{\mathrm{can}}\in\Omega^{2}_{\mathrm{IM}}(T^{*}A)
, namely the canonical isomorphism (called the reverse isomorphism in
[
26
]
):

\mu_{\mathrm{can}}:T^{*}A\to T^{*}A^{*}.

This IM 2-form is exact with canonical primitive the linear, multiplicative 1-form
(f_{\mathrm{can}},\mu_{\mathrm{can}})\in\Omega^{1}_{\mathrm{IM}}(T^{*}A)
, where
f_{\mathrm{can}}:T^{*}A\to\mathbb{R}
is the tautological map

f_{\mathrm{can}}(\alpha)=\alpha\Big(\frac{\operatorname{d}}{\operatorname{d}t}\big|_{t=0}e^{t}u\Big),\quad\text{ for }\quad\alpha\in T^{*}_{u}A.
